The variety of group actions on all algebraic real hyperbolic spaces
Bruno Duchesne, Christopher-Lloyd Simon

Abstract
For a cardinal κ \kappa , denote by 𝐇 κ \mathbf{H}^{\kappa} the algebraic real hyperbolic space of dimension κ \kappa . For a topological group Γ \Gamma , we study the set of continuous representations Γ → Isom ⁡ ( 𝐇 κ ) \Gamma\to\operatorname{Isom}(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}) up to continuous self-representations Isom ⁡ ( 𝐇 κ ) → Isom ⁡ ( 𝐇 κ ) \operatorname{Isom}(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})\to\operatorname{Isom}(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}) . The novelty of this work relies in considering simultaneously all cardinals, finite or infinite. We will endow this set of classes of representations with a natural topology, and show that this character variety is compact. This will also enable us to recover all previous compactifications of actions on 𝐇 n \mathbf{H}^{n} by certain actions on real trees for the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology. A class of representations recovers in particular the homothety class of its marked length spectrum. We will define the notion of algebraic cross-ratio and prove a GNS-embedding result, enabling us to generalize some rigidity properties of the marked length spectrum. We will also introduce a notion of abstract cross-ratio, and use it to show that a wide class of groups Γ \Gamma (characterized by the existence of what we call a 3 3 -full action on a CAT ⁡ ( − 1 ) \operatorname{CAT}(-1) -space) admit at most one class of irreducible representations into Isom ⁡ ( 𝐇 κ ) \operatorname{Isom}(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}) whose boundedness properties are controlled by those of ( X , d ) (X,d) . We will apply this to topological groups Γ \Gamma such as the isometry group Isom ⁡ ( 𝐇 κ ) \operatorname{Isom}(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}) itself, the automorphism group Aut ⁡ ( T ω ) \operatorname{Aut}(T_{\omega}) of the simplicial tree with countably infinite valency, and the automorphism group PGL 2 ⁡ ( K , | ⋅ | ) \operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\lvert\cdot\rvert) of the projective line over a non-Archimedean field. This work of foundational nature opens many directions of research: the size of the character variety for other special examples of groups (mapping class groups, Cremona groups), the definition and study of geometric structures on the character variety (symmetric Thurston metric, Goldman symplectic form), the description of the subset associated to convex cocompact representations. The background sections are detailed enough to make the work self-contained, and contain various new observations of independent interest (about strongly hyperbolic spaces, cross-ratios, kernels of hyperbolic type). Keywords: strong hyperbolicity, CAT ⁡ ( − 1 ) \operatorname{CAT}(-1) -space, Ptolemaic metric, cross-ratio, kernel of hyperbolic type, Möbius group, exotic representations, character variety, marked length spectrum, cross-ratio, rigidity, Polish group, Gromov-Hausdorff convergence.



Introduction

This work concerns continuous actions of topological groups by isometries on algebraic hyperbolic spaces of arbitrary dimensions.
For a cardinal
\kappa
, denote by
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
the algebraic hyperbolic space of dimension
\kappa
.
For a topological group
\Gamma
, we wish to study the set of continuous representations
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
up to continuous representations
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}})
.
The main questions are:

1.

How to distinguish actions, measure their proximity, and move between one another?

2.

What is a reasonable topology on this space and its properties (compact, connected)?

3.

When is that space empty (obstruction) or reduced to a singleton (rigidity) ?

This work addresses some aspects of these questions, as explained in this introduction.

Variety of actions up to exotic deformations

An element
g\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
has translation length
\ell(g)=\inf\{d(gx,x)\colon x\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\}
.
A group representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
has a
length function

\ell_{\rho}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by
\ell_{\rho}\colon\gamma\mapsto\ell(\rho(\gamma))
.
If two representations are conjugate (related by inner automorphism of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
) then they have the same length functions.

When the dimension
\kappa
is a finite cardinal
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the only self-representations of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
are inner automorphisms, hence for a discrete group
\Gamma
we recover its well-studied character varieties of actions on
\mathbf{H}^{n}
.
In particular, its subset corresponding to irreducible representations is well understood: they are characterized by their length functions
[
39
]
and they form a smooth variety compactified by actions on real trees with small edge stabilizers
[
51
,
62
]
.

The main novelty of this work is to consider simultaneously all cardinals
\kappa
. Observe that denoting
\omega=\Card(\mathbb{N})
the infinite countable cardinal, we have
\kappa+\omega=\max\{\kappa,\omega\}
.
A crucial difference with finite dimension is the existence of a family parametrized by
t\in(0,1]
of exotic deformations
\exoH_{t}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa+\omega}
characterized by the metric relations (where
\dH
is the distance on a algebraic hyperbolic space):

\forall x,y\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\colon\cosh\dH(\exoH_{t}(x),\exoH_{t}(y))=(\cosh\dH(x,y))^{t}.

These exotic embeddings come with exotic representations
\chi_{t}\colon\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa+\omega})
.
It turns out that by
[
47
]
, all irreducible representations
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}})
are induced by isometric embeddings or exotic inclusions of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
into
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}}
.

Exotic deformations induce homotheties of length functions

\forall g\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})\colon\ell(\chi_{t}(g))=t\ell(g)

so a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and its exotic deformation
\chi_{t}\circ\rho
have homothetic length functions.
In particular the marked length spectrum rigidity for irreducible representations
[
39
, Theorem A]
breaks down for infinite dimensions, and we will see how to restore it.
Let us emphasize that we wish to consider all possible representations
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
, not only irreducible ones, and for that (the homothety class of) the length function is insufficient as it cannot distinguish elliptic or parabolic representations (with fixed points in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
or
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, and no hyperbolic elements), since they all have trivial length function.

Thus, to consider representations
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
up to self-representations, we will use a tool introduced by Monod and Py in
[
47
]
called
functions of hyperbolic type
.
To a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and a point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, is attached the function of hyperbolic type
F_{\rho,o}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
F_{\rho,o}\colon\gamma\mapsto\cosh\dH(\rho(\gamma)x,x)
, which captures all distances between points in the orbit
\rho(\Gamma)\cdot o
.
Let us explain how such functions of hyperbolic type can be characterized with only one inequality.

On a topological space
X
, a
kernel
is a continuous function
K\colon X^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
which is symmetric (
\forall x,y\in X\colon K(x,y)=K(y,x)
). We say that it has diagonal
s\in\mathbb{R}
when
\forall z\in X\colon K(z,z)=s
. We say that it is non-negative when
\forall x,y\in X\colon K(x,y)\geq 0
.

Definition A

(kernel of hyperbolic type.
Definition

3.11
)
.

A kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
is of
hyperbolic type
when it is nonnegative with diagonal
1
, and for all
x_{0}\in X
the
visual kernel

K_{0}\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by
K_{0}\colon(x_{i},x_{j})\mapsto K(x_{0},x_{i})K(x_{0},x_{j})-K(x_{i},x_{j})
is of positive type, namely we have the following
hyperbolic Cauchy-Schwartz inequalities
:

\textstyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\;\forall x\in X^{n},\;\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\quad\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K(x_{i},x_{j})\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}K(x_{0},x_{k})\right)^{2}.

(HCS)

Similarly to the GNS construction for Hilbert spaces, from such a kernel of hyperbolic type, one constructs a hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and a continuous map
f\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
such that for all
x,y\in X
,
K(x,y)=\cosh\dH(f(x),f(y))
.
Moreover, if the kernel is invariant under some group action
\Gamma\to\mathfrak{S}(X)
then there is a representation
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that
f
is equivariant.

Remark B

(Cayley-Menger derterminants)
.

The idea to characterize when a metric space
(X,d)
embeds isometrically in some
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is not recent and goes back (at least) to Blumenthal’s work
[
4
]
(first published in 1950).
An isometric embedding exists if and only if all its hyperbolic Cayley-Menger determinants are non-positive:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\,\forall x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in X\colon\det(-\cosh(d(x_{i},x_{j}))\leq 0.

We will explain the relation with kernels of hyperbolic type in
Section

3.2
.
One advantage of Cayley-Menger determinants is to provide, when
X
is finite, a simple characterization of the minimal dimension
\kappa
for which an isometric embedding exists.

To encode group representations in some
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
we will use the following notion.

Definition C

(function of hyperbolic type)
.

On a topological group
\Gamma
, a function
F\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
is of
hyperbolic type
when the kernel
\Gamma\times\Gamma\ni(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto F(\alpha^{-1}\beta)\in\mathbb{R}
is of hyperbolic type.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
the space of functions of hyperbolic type on
\Gamma
endowed with the pointwise convergence topology.
When
\Gamma
is discrete, it is a closed subspace of the product
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}^{\Gamma}
.

For a continuous representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, the function

F_{\rho,o}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\qquad F_{\rho,o}\colon\gamma\to\cosh\dH(\rho(\gamma)o,o)

is of hyperbolic type.
Conversely, for every function of hyperbolic type
F\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
, there is a continuous representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and a point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
such that
F=F_{\rho,o}
.
Note that one may recover the length function
\ell_{F}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
of any associated representation by the formula
\ell_{F}(\gamma)=\lim_{+\infty}\tfrac{1}{n}\log F(\gamma^{n})
.

For two representations
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
that are conjugated by
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and two points
o,o^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, the associated functions of hyperbolic type are
comparable
:

\|\log(F_{\rho,o})-\log(F_{\rho^{\prime},o^{\prime}})\|_{\infty}<\infty
.
Moreover for
t\in(0,1]
the exotic deformation and representation
(\chi_{t},\exoH_{t})
exponentiates functions of hyperbolic type:
F_{\chi_{t}\circ\rho,\exoH_{t}(o)}=F_{\rho,o}^{t}
.
To encompass all self-representations of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
, we define two functions of hyperbolic type
F,F^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
as
homothetic
when there exists
t>0
such that
F^{t},F^{\prime}
are comparable, namely
\log(F^{t}/F^{\prime})
is bounded on
\Gamma
.
We thus define the
variety of actions

\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
as the topological quotient space of
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
by this homothety equivalence.

The space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
has some pathological properties. For example, the class of the function
\mathbf{1}
constant to
1
lies in the closure of any other class.
But classes of representation can develop other (less dramatic) degeneracies leading to singular points of the character variety (as in the classical Teichmüller theory) and this leads to considering the following subsets of representations (for instance analogue to quasi-Fuchsian loci).

A representation
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
(or a function of hyperbolic type) is called
neutral
when the length function is trivial and
elementary
when there is an invariant non-empty subset with at most
2
points in the boundary
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
or a fixed point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. The notions of non-neutrality and non-elementarity are preserved by homothety of functions of hyperbolic type (
Corollary

4.26
), and we denote the corresponding subspaces of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
by
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
.

If two functions of hyperbolic type are comparable or homothetic then they have equal or homothetic length functions, so we obtain a function
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\Gamma})
.
A function of hyperbolic type which is not trivial (not in the class of the constant function
\mathbf{1}
) may still have trivial length function which is trivial (equal to
0
).

Our main Theorems
6.2
and
6.4
on the topology of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
and of the projectivized length function
\ell\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\Gamma})
are the following.

Theorem D

(length function on
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a topological group.

The length function
\ell\colon\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
defined by
F\mapsto\ell_{F}
quotients to a function
\ell\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
which is continuous and injective in restriction to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
; in particular the sets
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
are Hausdorff and open in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.

The following statement shows how the topology of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma),\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(G)
and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(G)
behaves when
\Gamma
is finitely generated. The subspaces
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;2,\varepsilon)
and
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;2,\varepsilon)
of
\mathcal{F}
, defined in
Section

6.1
, are parametrized by a generating set
S
and a positive number
\varepsilon
.

Theorem E

(topology of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(G)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(G)
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group.

1.

The variety of actions
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is compact.

2.

The subspace
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
is a filtering union of compact metrizable subspaces
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;2,\varepsilon)
so the restriction
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;2,\varepsilon)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

3.

The subspace
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
is a filtering union of compact metrizable subspaces
\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;2,\varepsilon)
so the restriction
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;2,\varepsilon)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

The injectivity of
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
means that the homothety class of the length function determines the class of the function of hyperbolic type: this a length spectrum rigidity which we obtained using the methods developed in the next subsection.

Cross-ratios and length spectrum rigidity

We will be interested in character varieties of groups admitting faithful actions on certain hyperbolic spaces of non-positive curvature: such actions are often better understood on the boundary at infinity, where the cross-ratio becomes an important tool encoding the asymptotic properties of the metric.

Fix a metric space
(X,d)
, which may not be proper or complete.
At a base point
o\in X
are defined the
Gromov product

(x\mid y)_{o}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(d(o,x)+d(o,y)-d(x,y)\right)
and the
visual metametric

d_{o}(x,y)=\exp\left(-(x\mid y)_{o}\right)
.
Hence the
cross-ratio
of
(x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime})\in X^{2}\times X^{2}
is defined (for any
o\in X
) as the positive real number:

\left[{x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime}}\right]=\frac{d_{o}(x,x^{\prime})\times d_{o}(y,y^{\prime})}{d_{o}(x,y^{\prime})\times d_{o}(y,x^{\prime})}=\exp\tfrac{1}{2}\left(d(x,x^{\prime})+d(y,y^{\prime})-d(x,y^{\prime})-d(y,x^{\prime})\right)

The cross-ratio satisfies the (
Invariance
), (
Inversion
) and (
Cocycle
) identities (see
Section

1.2
).

For
\varepsilon>0
,
(X,d)
is strongly-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolic when every tetrahedron
(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\in X^{4}
satisfies
1\leq\left[{x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime}}\right]^{-\varepsilon}+\left[{x,x^{\prime};y^{\prime},y}\right]^{-\varepsilon}
, that is the Ptolemy inequality for
d_{o}^{\varepsilon}
:

d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime})d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(y,y^{\prime})\leq d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,y^{\prime})d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(y,x^{\prime})+d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

This implies Gromov-
\delta
-hyperbolicity for
\delta=\log(2)/\varepsilon
; indeed, it is an exponential strengthening of the Gromov-hyperbolicity, see
Remark

1.1
).
The class of strongly-
1
-hyperbolic spaces contains the class of
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-spaces, hence of trees and algebraic hyperbolic spaces of any dimension.

The
Section

1.1
will provide background (and new results) about strongly hyperbolic spaces and their isometry groups.
Our main motivation for this setting stems from the fact that strong-hyperbolicity ensures a continuous extension of the cross-ratio to subsets of quadruples in the bordification
(X\cup\partial X)
of which at least
3
are distinct, which further enjoys separation properties in restriction to such quadruples in the boundary
\partial X
(see
Equation

=

[
⋅
]

0

and
Equation

=

[
⋅
]

∞

).

Remark F

(coarse strong hyperbolicity)
.

We will see that to have this continuous extension property, it suffices to assume that
X
is coarsely strongly hyperbolic, a notion equivalent to that of asymptotic
PT(-1)
-space introduced in
[
45
]
in relation to the Ptolemaic inequality.

Remark G

(why strongly-hyperbolic?)
.

Even though all strongly hyperbolic spaces for the applications in this paper will be
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
, this level of generality is natural because of the following remark and question.

All “exotic” embeddings metric spaces (such as trees
[
12
]
or algebraic hyperbolic spaces
[
48
, Proposition 8.2]
) into
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
derive from the fact that for some
\lambda>1
the function
\lambda^{d}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type on
X
.
Thus we may wonder: for which metric spaces
(X,d)
is there some
\lambda>1
for which
\lambda^{d}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type on
X
?

Our
Proposition

4.13
will show that such a space must be coarsely strongly hyperbolic.

For a topological space
Y
, denote
Y^{(4)}\subset Y^{4}
the quadruples of which at least
3
are distinct.
We will define the notion of
abstract cross-ratio
on a topological space
Y
as a continuous function
B\colon Y^{(4)}\to[0,\infty]
satisfying the (
Invariance
), (
Inversion
) and (
Cocycle
) relations as well as the characterizations (

=

[
⋅
]

0

) and (

=

[
⋅
]

∞

‣
1.2
) of the fibres above
0
and
\infty
.
We say that an abstract cross-ratio
generates the topology
when for every triple of distinct points
x,y,z\in Y
, the collection of subsets
\{w\in Y\colon B(w,x,y,z)<\varepsilon\}
for
\varepsilon>0
is a basis of neighbourhoods of
x
.

For example, the cross-ratio in a strongly hyperbolic space is an abstract cross-ratio that generates the topology.
We will show the following
Proposition

4.6
and
Corollary

4.7
.

Proposition H

(abstract cross-ratios on
3
-full strongly hyperbolic spaces)
.

Consider a strongly hyperbolic space
(X,d)
and a subgroup
\Gamma<\Isom(X)
acting
3
-transitively on
\partial X
.
If an abstract cross-ratio
B
on
\partial X
generating the topology is
\Gamma
-invariant, then there exists
t>0
such that for all
(w,x,y,z)\in(\partial X)^{(4)}
we have
B(w,x,y,z)=\left[{w,x,y,z}\right]^{t}
.

Consequently if an embedding of strongly hyperbolic spaces
f\colon\partial X_{1}\to\partial X_{2}
is a homeomorphism on its image, and is equivariant under the action of a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X_{1})
acting
3
-transitively on
\partial X_{1}
, then there is
t>0
such that for all
(w,x,y,z)\in(\partial X_{1})^{(4)}
we have

\left[{f(w),f(x),f(y),f(z)}\right]_{\partial{X_{2}}}=\left[{w,x,y,z}\right]_{\partial X_{1}}^{t}

Such notions of abstract cross-ratios have been used to show many rigidity results for representations (see for example
[
58
,
9
,
41
]
).

Here, we will characterize abstract cross-ratios coming from an embedding into some
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, which we call
algebraic cross-ratios
.
Let us first mention that bijections of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
preserving the cross-ratio are induced by a unique isometry of
X
, namely the Möbius groups of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
coincides with the isometry group of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
Next, observe that in the upper half-space model for
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, for any three distinct boundary points
0,1,\infty\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, there is a Hilbert space structure on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\setminus\{\infty\}
such that for all
x,y\neq\infty
:

\left[{x,y,0,\infty}\right]\left[{y,0,1,\infty}\right]=\tfrac{\|y-x\|}{\|0-1\|}

Moreover isometries of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
fixing
\infty
acts by similarities on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\setminus\{\infty\}
.
This suggests the following
Definition

4.15
.

Definition I

(algebraic cross-ratio)
.

On a topological space
Y
, we define an
algebraic cross-ratio
on
Y

as an abstract cross-ratio
such that for any triple of distinct points denoted
0,1,\infty\in Y
the function on
(Y\setminus\{\infty\})^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by

(x,y)\mapsto\left(R(x,y,0,\infty)R(y,0,1,\infty)\right)^{2}

is a kernel conditionally of negative type on
Y\setminus\{\infty\}
.

For a topological space
Y
, the classical GNS construction for kernel conditionally of negative type
K\colon Y^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
gives the existence of a Hilbert space
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
and a continuous map
f\colon Y\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
such that
K(x,y)=\|f(x)-f(y)\|^{2}
. Moreover, if there is a group
\Gamma
acting on
Y
preserving
K
then the construction also yields a representation
\rho\colon G\to\Isom(\mathbb{R}^{\kappa})
such that
f
is
\rho
-equivariant.
The following
Theorem

4.18
is an analogous statement for algebraic cross-ratios. Recall that a subset of Hilbert space is total when it is not included in a strict closed affine subspace.

Theorem J

(GNS for algebraic cross-ratios)
.

Consider a topological space
Y
having at least four points with an algebraic cross-ratio
R\colon Y^{(4)}\to[0,+\infty)
.

There is a cardinal
\kappa
and a continuous function
f\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
with total image such that for all
(x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+})\in Y^{(4)}
we have
\left[{f(x^{-}),f(x^{+});f(y^{-}),f(y^{+})}\right]=R(x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+})
.

Moreover, for any two such maps
f_{1}\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}
and
f_{2}\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
, there exists a unique isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
such that
f_{2}=\varphi\circ f_{1}
, in particular
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
.

Consequently, if a group
\Gamma
acts on
Y
preserving
R
then there is a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that
f
is
\rho
-equivariant.

We finally turn to results of (global) marked length spectrum rigidity, namely distinguishing representations
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(X,d)
from their length function
\ell\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
The following
Proposition

4.23
provides rigidity of the marked length spectrum, generalizing the finite dimensional case
[
39
]
. A representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is
minimal
when there is no non-trivial closed invariant totally geodesic subspaces
\emptyset\subsetneq\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}}\subsetneq\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
).

Proposition K

(rigidity of marked length spectrum)
.

Consider a group
\Gamma
with minimal representations
\rho_{i}\colon G\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
for
i=1,2
.
If
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
have non-trivial and equal length functions
\ell_{\rho_{1}}=\ell_{\rho_{2}}
, then
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
and
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
are conjugated by an isometry
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
.

In finite dimension
n
,
[
39
, Theorem A]
proves that two Zariski-dense representations
\rho_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
for
i=1,2
with homothetic length functions (for which there is
\lambda>0
such that
\ell_{\rho_{1}}=\lambda\ell_{\rho_{2}}
) must be conjugated.
In infinite dimensions this statement fails, since for every representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
with non-trivial length function, for every
t\in(0,1)
the exotic deformations
\chi_{t}\circ\rho
has homothetic length function but is not conjugated to
\rho
. The following
Corollary

4.26
provides the appropriate generalization to arbitrary dimensions characterizing representations and functions of hyperbolic type corresponding to homothetic marked length spectrum.

Corollary L

(homothetic marked length spectra)
.

Consider functions of hyperbolic type
F_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
yielding non-zero length functions
\ell_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and any minimal representations
\rho_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
realizing
F_{i}
.
For every
t\in(0,1]
the following are equivalent:

F
:

The
F_{i}
are
t
-equivalent
, namely
\log(F_{1}/F_{2}^{t})
is bounded.

\ell_{F}
:

The
\ell_{i}
are
t
-homothetic
, namely
\ell_{1}=t\ell_{2}
.

\rho_{F}
:

The representation
\rho_{1}
is conjugate to the
t
-deformed representation
\chi_{t}\circ\rho_{2}
.

Geometric convergence and comparison with previous results

Fix a finitely generated torsion-free group
\Gamma
. In our compact space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
, we may consider the closure of various subsets of classes of representations to obtain compactifications.
For example, for each finite cardinal
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, we may consider the subset
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
of homothety classes associated to faithful and discrete representations
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
.

We should compare this compactification with various previously known compactifications obtained for instance in
[
50
,
51
,
3
,
62
]
of certain character varieties by certain actions on real trees.
More precisely, Paulin introduced the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology on spaces of actions to show the following.
Let
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
be the Gromov-Hausdorff space of faithful and discrete
\Gamma
-actions on
\mathbf{H}^{n}
and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
the quotient by homothety of the Gromov-Hausdorff space of
\Gamma
-actions on real trees having small edge stabilizers (stabilizers of non-trivial geodesic segments do not contain free subgroups of rank
\geq 2
).
Note that for
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, if
n_{1}<n_{2}
then
\mathcal{H}^{n_{1}}_{fd}\subset\mathcal{H}^{n_{2}}_{fd}
.
Paulin proved
[
62
]
that if
\Gamma
is a finitely generated torsion-free group containing a free group of rank
2
, then
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
is compactified by a closed subspace of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
. When
\Gamma
is the fundamental group of a closed surface of genus
\geq 2
, it follows from
[
71
, Theorem 3.2]
that for all
n\geq 2
, the closure of
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
in this compactification is all of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
, and this coincides with Thurston’s compactification of the Teichmüller space by measured laminations.

Example

6.21

exhibits many finitely generated groups
\Gamma
for which the inclusion
\bigcup_{n}\overline{\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)}\subset\bigcup_{n}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is strict.

Our
Theorem

6.18
confirms that our compactification recovers that of Paulin, namely that convergence for the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology is equivalent to convergence of homothety classes of length functions.

Theorem M

(recovering compactification of faithful and discrete representations)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated torsion-free group containing a free group of rank
2
.
The Gromov-Hausdorff space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is homeomorphic to its image
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.

Our compact space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
may contain more actions on real trees than
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
, and those may be limits of actions on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
for varying
\kappa_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\sqcup\{\omega\}
as we now explain.

On a real tree
(T,d)
, the kernel
\lambda^{d}
is of hyperbolic type, thus every
\Gamma
-action on a real tree yields a homothety class of functions of hyperbolic type: those define a subset
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.
Note that
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is closed in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
, so points in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\setminus\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
correspond to new classes of representations, that are only encompassed by our compactification.
Examples of
\Gamma
for which
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\setminus\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset
include free groups on
\geq 2
generators and automorphism groups of real trees
(T,d)
such that
\partial T
is infinite.
The following
Theorem

5.23
shows that the convergence of a rescaled hyperbolic space to a real tree can be obtained equivariantly inside
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

Theorem N

(geometric convergence to actions on trees)
.

Consider a group
\Gamma
finitely generated by
S
, and a sequence of representations
\rho_{m}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}})
for cardinals
\kappa_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
.

If no
\rho_{m}
has a fixed point at infinity, but the infimum displacement length over
S
is unbounded as
m\to\infty
, then up to extracting a subsequence of
(\rho_{m})
there are:

1.

a real tree
(T,d)
and an isometric action
\rho_{T}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(T)

2.

an embedding
\exoT_{\lambda}\colon T\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
equivariant for
\pi_{\lambda}\colon\Isom(T)\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
with
\lambda=e
,

3.

an isometric action
\rho_{\omega}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
preserving
\exoT_{\lambda}(T)
,

4.

a sequence of representations
\rho_{m}^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
such that
F_{\rho_{m}^{\prime}}\sim F_{\rho_{m}}
,

5.

a sequence of
(\rho_{m},\rho_{m}^{\prime})
-equivariant exotic embeddings
\exoH_{t_{m}}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

such that
(\rho^{\prime}_{m})_{m}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
converges to
\rho_{\omega}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
and
\rho_{\omega}=\pi_{\lambda}\circ\rho_{T}
.

Moreover, there exist points
o_{m}\in\operatorname{Core}(\rho_{m})
and
o\in T
such that for every finite set
K\subset\Gamma
, we have Hausdorff convergence of
\exoH_{t_{m}}(\Conv(\rho_{m}(K)\cdot o_{m}))
to
\exoT_{\lambda}(\Conv(\rho_{\omega}(K)\cdot o))
.

Remark O

(the use of Gromov-Hausdorff convergence)
.

A similar result has been obtained by Courtois–Guilloux
[
16
]
, also using the rescaling method by exotic deformations representations
\chi_{t}
, in order to obtain continuity results for the Hausdorff dimension of limit sets.

In our statement above the sequence of dimensions can be arbitrary (of various unbounded cardinals), so to obtain our Gromov-Hausdorff convergence we need a metric estimate for the convex hull of finitely many points which is independent on the dimension, which we proved in
[
20
]
and recall here.

Proposition P

(Hausdorff distance from convex hull to
1
-skeleton)
.

For a set of points
X\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\cup\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, every point of its convex hull
\Conv(X)
is at distance at most
\sinh^{-1}(1)=\log(1+\sqrt{2})
from its
1
-skeleton
X^{(1)}=\bigcup_{x_{1},x_{2}\in Y}[x_{1},x_{2}]
.

Our
Theorem

5.23
constructs the limiting representation, the action on the limiting tree, and also describes the geometric convergence towards this limit. The
Section

5.4
will show that this action on the tree realized as a subspace of
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
can be constructed immediately from ultraproducts of hyperbolic spaces
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
.

Example

6.23
shows that for many groups (which split as an amalgam over
\mathbb{Z}
), the space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
may be much larger than
\bigcup_{n}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
.

Groups with a unique homothety class of actions on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}

In the final part of this paper, we prove that some groups of special interests have exactly one non-elementary action on some
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
up to the equivalence introduced above.

Theorem Q

(
Corollary

7.17
)
.

If
G
is one the following topological groups:

1.

the isometry group
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
of the real hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
of countable dimension
\kappa
,

2.

the isometry group
\Aut(T_{\kappa})
of the
\kappa
-regular simplicial tree
T_{\kappa}
where
\kappa\geq 3
is countable,

3.

the group
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
where
\mathbb{K}
is a complete non-Archimedean valued field

then the space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(G)
has exactly one point.

Some cases of this statement were already known, for example the first item
[
47
]
or the two other items when the group is locally compact
[
12
]
. We group them in one result since we derive (the unicity) from a more general result: the main point is that these groups admit an action on a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
space
(X,d)
(a hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
or a real tree), that is sufficiently rich to reflect the structure of the group. More precisely, we will prove the following
Theorem

7.14
, where boundedness properties and
3
-full actions are defined in
Section

7
.

Theorem R

(rigidity of groups acting fully on
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
spaces)
.

Consider a topological group
G
with a
3
-full action on a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-space
(X,d)
.
If every non-elementary continuous action of
G
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous, then
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(G)
has at most one point.

Remark S

(no local compactness, no Gelfand pairs)
.

The main novelty in this statement is the fact that
G
is not necessarily locally compact. In particular, the notion of Gelfand pairs, on which previous proofs rely, is not available in this context. Thus we use a different strategy relying on our unicity of cross-ratios up to powers (
H
) and length spectrum rigidity (
K
).

Ideas in the proof of
R
.

We first construct a
G
-equivariant boundary map
\partial X\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
that is a homeomorphism on its image. Using this map, we can pullback the cross-ratio from
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
to
\partial X
. We then show bi-continuity of this map (which would be automatic in a locally compact setting) using boundedness properties. We conclude the proof by using uniqueness of the cross-ratio up to powers on
\partial X
.
∎

Further results, Some questions, Relations to other works

Geometry of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
: dimension grading, connectivity, metric

Remark T

(critical exponent and dimension graduation of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
)
.

For a function of hyperbolic type
F\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
, we define its critical exponent as the supremum of
t>0
such that
F^{t}
is of hyperbolic type (
Definition

5.1
).
We show that the critical exponent is upper-semi-continuous on
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
(
Proposition

5.5
).
We characterize actions on real trees as those with infinite critical exponent (
Proposition

5.3
).

For homothety classes of functions of hyperbolic type with finite critical exponent, we obtain a preferred lift
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\to\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
by choosing those with critical exponent
1
: this yields a critical dimension
\kappa
and critical representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.
We show that where the critical exponent is finite, the critical dimension is upper-semi-continuous on
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
(
Proposition

5.8
).

Thus, the critical dimension yields a graduation on
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
, whose infinite locus is the boundary at infinity consisting of actions on trees.

Question U

(connectedness and bendings)
.

For which groups
\Gamma
is
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
or
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}(\Gamma)
connected? When so, does there exist
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that the corresponding space
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{ne}(\Gamma)
or
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
is connected?
Can we find obstructions to such connectedness in terms of the (bounded) homology invariants or the homotopy type of the group
\Gamma
?

On a topological space, the kernels of positive type form a convex
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
cone stable by product, and kernels of conditionally negative type form a convex
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
-cone).
In a similar vein, we wonder whether kernels of hyperbolic type are stable under certain weighted averages (which only depend on the kernels and not on the choice of points in the space).

For
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
the fundamental group of a closed orientable surface of genus
g\geq 2
,
Example

6.23
shows how the bending procedure enables to move between distinct classes of representations, yielding a family of distinct points in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
parametrized by an infinite dimensional Lie group.
This raises the question:
If two points in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
or
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}(\Gamma)
are connected, can they be connected through bendings and limits?
Let us note that
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}^{2}_{fd}(\Gamma)
is connected through bendings, since any two points in Teichmüller space are connected through a sequence of Goldman twists
[
30
]
, which one may write explicitly using their Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates, hence the compactification
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}^{2}_{fd}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is connectd through bendings and limits.
This question could be generalized to
\Gamma
the fundamental groups of a graph-of-groups (in the sense of Bass-Serre) such that all edges with a non-trivial group separate the graph.

Question V

(type structures and metrics on strata)
.

On a group
\Gamma
, define a
type structure
as a partition of its conjugacy classes into
3
types (elliptic, parabolic, loxodromic) coming from an action on a Gromov-hyperbolic metric space.
Many groups have natural type structures: hyperbolic groups acting on their Cayley-Graphs, mapping class groups of surfaces acting on curve complexes (see
Z
), or the Cremona group acting on
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
(see
Example

2.11
).

Each type structure
\iota
on
\Gamma
yields a
stratum

\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{\iota}(\Gamma)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
of type-preserving classes, and this yields a partition into all possible types
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)=\sqcup_{\iota}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.
One may wonder for a group
\Gamma
, how many strata, how do they wrap around one another, what is the topology of a special stratum?
Such questions on the size and topology of the partition into types
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)=\sqcup_{\iota}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{\iota}(\Gamma)
would shed brighter light onto rigidity properties.

Finally, one may define a metric on each stratum
D\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{\iota}(\Gamma)\to[0,+\infty]
, inspired by
[
72
]
and
[
55
]
, as follows.
For
\left[{F_{1}}\right],\left[{F_{2}}\right]\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{\iota}(\Gamma)
, consider the infimum over representatives
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{\iota}(\Gamma)
of

D(F_{1},F_{2})=\inf\left\{\log\lvert\ell_{F_{1}}(\gamma)/\ell_{F_{2}}(\gamma)\rvert\colon\gamma\in\Gamma\;\text{is $\iota$-loxodromic}\right\}

One may study the properties of these metrics on strata, together with the geometry and dynamics of the isometric actions by
\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma)
.

Geometric actions on strongly hyperbolic and
\operatorname{CAT}(-\varepsilon)
spaces

Question W

(Strongly hyperbolic &
\operatorname{CAT}(0)\implies\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
)
.

Gromov’s Jungentraum (
[
32
, p. 193]
is to show that every (finitely generated) hyperbolic group
\Gamma
admits geometric action (proper cocompact) on a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
space.
While this is wide open, it is expected to fail, but no counterexamples are known.
A weaker but equally open request is to ask whether it admits a
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
model.

The introduction of strongly hyperbolic spaces, a nice class of Gromov-hyperbolic spaces containing
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
spaces, is partly motivated by this question
[
53
]
.
Indeed
[
52
]
shows that on a hyperbolic group
\Gamma
, the Green metric arising from random walks are strongly hyperbolic, thus
\Gamma
acts properly cocompactly on a strongly hyperbolic space.

While working in the general context of strongly hyperbolic spaces, we were led to obtain a couple of new properties relating the cross-ratio to the metric, such as
Lemma

1.17
and
Lemma

1.18
.
Such properties would have been much easier to prove under an additional
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
assumption (see
Remark

1.15
).

This leads us to the following
1.16
.
Let
(X,d)
be strongly-
1
-hyperbolic and
\operatorname{CAT(0)}
: when is it
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
? (a converse to
[
52
, Theorem 4.2]
).

Remark X

(convex cocompact)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a hyperbolic group.
Following ideas of Furman
[
27
]
, Reyes
[
55
]
considers the space
\mathcal{D}(\Gamma)
of classes of pseudo-metrics on
\Gamma
that are quasi-isometric to a length metric modulo rough similarity.

If
F\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
is a function of hyperbolic type on a group
\Gamma
then
d(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=\cosh^{-1}(F(\gamma^{-1}_{1}\gamma_{2}))
is a pseudo-metric on
\Gamma
(since there is a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
such that
\cosh^{-1}(F(\gamma^{-1}_{1}\gamma_{2}))=\dH(\rho(\gamma_{1})o,\rho(\gamma_{2})o)
. Moreover, two functions of hyperbolic type are homothetic if and only if their associated pseudo-metrics are roughly similar in the sense of
[
55
]
.
Finally, Xu
[
75
]
shows that for a finitely generated group
\Gamma
acting on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, an orbit map
\Gamma\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is a quasi-isometry if and only if the action is convex compact (this is an open condition in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
).

This yields an injective map from the space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{cc}(\Gamma)
of such classes of convex cocompact actions to the space
\mathcal{D}(\Gamma)
.
Reyes conjectures that for a finitely generated free group
\Gamma
, the set of classes corresponding to finite dimensional convex-cocompact actions
\bigcup_{n}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{cc}(\Gamma)
has dense image in
\mathcal{D}(\Gamma)
.

Which groups act faithfully on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
?

Question Y

(automorphism groups of simple graphs)
.

A simple graph is given by an adjacency matrix
A
on a set of vertices
X
, that is an
X\times X
matrix with values in the boolean semi-field
\{0,1\}
having diagonal
0
.
This yields a metric space
(X,d)
, where the distance between the vertices
u,v\in X
is given by the path metric:
d(u,v)=\min\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}\colon A^{n}_{uv}>0\}
.
The automorphism group of the graph
\Aut(A)
coincides with the isometry group
\Isom(X,d)
.

A general question is: which graphs admit a function of the distance
d
(such as a polynomial in
d
and its exponential
\exp(\varepsilon d)
) that is a kernel of hyperbolic type
C\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
, which is separating (in the sense of
Remark

3.7
).
This would yield an embedding
X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
that is equivariant under some representation of their automorphism group
\Isom(X,d)\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.

In particular, we may define the critical exponent of
(X,d)
as the supremum of
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for which the kernel
C=\exp(\varepsilon d)
is of hyperbolic type (note that
\varepsilon=0
always yields the trivial function of hyperbolic type).
The critical exponent is
+\infty
if and only if
(X,d)
embeds isometrically in a real tree (see
Proposition

1.6
and the surrounding remarks), and such graphs admit a combinatorial characterization (known as block graphs, see
[
34
, Theorem 4.3]
).

Is there a combinatorial characterization of infinite simplicial graphs with positive critical exponent, namely for which there is
\varepsilon>0
such that
C=\exp(\varepsilon d)
is a kernel of hyperbolic type?

Question Z

(Mapping class groups)
.

Consider a surface
\Sigma_{g,n}
with genus
g
and
n
punctures, of negative Euler characteristic.
We wonder which mapping class groups
\Mod(\Sigma_{g,n})
admit faithful representation into
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
for some
\kappa
.
If such a mapping class group is non-linear over
\mathbb{R}
then
\kappa
must be infinite.
If there exists such a representation without fixed points then
\Mod(\Sigma_{g,n})
would not have Kazhdan’s property (T), and if there exists such a representation that is metrically proper then
\Mod(\Sigma_{g,n})
would have the Haagerup’s property.

The Teichmüller spaces of complex dimension
3g-3+n\geq 2
are not Gromov-hyperbolic for all the classical metrics (Teichmüller
[
44
]
, Weil-Petersson
[
11
]
, Thurston
[
35
]
).
However Bonahon
[
6
]
showed (for
n=0,g\geq 2
) that the space of geodesic currents on
\mathcal{GC}(\Sigma_{g,n})
on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n})
with its intersection form
\It\colon\mathcal{GC}(\Sigma_{g,n})\times\mathcal{GC}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, contains a copy of Teichmüller space in a positive-level set
\{x\in\mathcal{GC}(\Sigma_{g,n})\colon\It(x,x)=\pi\lvert\chi\rvert\}
, while the space of measured laminations identifies with the isotropic cone
\mathcal{ML}(\Sigma_{g,n})=\{\xi\in\mathcal{GC}(\Sigma_{g,n})\colon\It(\xi,\xi)=0\}
, which enables him to recover Thurston’s compactification.
This is reminiscent of the hyperboloid model in hyperbolic geometry, except that the intersection form has signature
\geq 3g-3+n
(since equality holds in restriction to
\mathcal{ML}(\Sigma_{g,n})
); in particular the intersection form does not define a separated pairing on
\mathcal{ML}(\Sigma_{g,n})
in the sense of
Example

4.4
.

However if we restrict to the subset of projective measured laminations that are filling
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{MLF}(\Sigma_{g,n})\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{ML}(\Sigma_{g,n})
, the intersection function is separating and generates the topology in the sense of
Example

4.4
, which implies that we obtain an abstract cross-ratio on
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{MLF}(\Sigma_{g,n})
that generates the topology by the formula:

\forall(u,v,x,y)\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{MLF}(\Sigma_{g})^{(4)}\quad\colon\quad B(u,v,x,y)=\tfrac{\It(u,v)\It(x,y)}{\It(u,y)\It(x,v)}.

We may further restrict this to the space of projective measured laminations that are fixed by pseudo-Anosov elements
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{MLA}(\Sigma_{g,n})
.
Is this abstract cross-ratio an algebraic cross-ratio in the sense of
I
?
A positive answer would yield (by
J
) an embedding
\mathcal{MLA}(\Sigma_{g})\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
which is equivariant under a faithful representation
\Mod(\Sigma_{g})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.

A weaker question is whether
\Mod(\Sigma_{g,n})
admits a faithful action (maybe fixed point-free or metrically proper) on some strongly hyperbolic space.
When
3g-3+n\geq 2
and
(g,n)\neq(1,2)
, the extended mapping class group
\Mod^{\pm}(\Sigma_{g,n})
acts faithfully by automorphisms of the curve graph
\mathcal{SC}(\Sigma_{g,n})
(it fact
\Mod^{\pm}(\Sigma_{g,n})
coincides with
\Aut\mathcal{SC}(\Sigma_{g,n})
by
[
36
,
42
]
).
By
[
43
]
, the curve graph is Gromov-hyperbolic and its boundary identifies with the space of ending laminations
\partial\mathcal{SC}(\Sigma_{g,n})=\mathcal{MLE}(\Sigma_{g,n})
, which are in particular filling.
Moreover, the loxodromic elements for the action of
\Mod(\Sigma_{g,n})
on
\mathcal{SC}(\Sigma_{g,n})
are precisely the pseudo-Anosov elements, whose fixed points identify with
\mathcal{MLA}(\Sigma_{g,n})\subset\partial\mathcal{SC}(\Sigma_{g,n})
.
Thus we wonder which curves graphs
\mathcal{SC}_{g,n}
are strongly hyperbolic. And for those which are, does their metric cross-ratio on the boundary coincide with the intersection cross-ratio defined above?

In relation to the previous
Y
, we also wonder whether curve graphs admit a function of the distance yielding a separating kernel of hyperbolic type.

Conventions and notations

The expression “for any” is systematically used in the precise sense, to mean “for some or equivalently for all”.
The set of natural integers
\mathbb{N}
contains
0
, whereas
\mathbb{N}^{*}=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
. For a set of numbers, we denote in index some restrictions on certain sets of numbers, such as
\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
.

1
Geometry of strongly hyperbolic spaces

We will recall from
[
52
,
18
]
the notion and properties of strongly hyperbolic spaces, which are metric spaces (maybe not proper or complete) whose distance satisfies a strengthening of Gromov’s hyperbolicity condition, ensuring that their boundary is endowed with
a continuous cross-ratio satisfying appropriate separation properties.
The class of strongly hyperbolic spaces contains the class of
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-spaces, hence of trees and algebraic hyperbolic spaces of any dimension, which will be at the centre of our interest.

Let us briefly recall from
[
10
, Chapter II.1]
that for
\varepsilon\geq 0
a metric space is
\operatorname{CAT}(-\varepsilon)
when it is geodesic (any two points are connected by some geodesic) and the geodesic triangles have lengths satisfying the
\operatorname{CAT}(-\varepsilon)
inequality (saying that they are thinner than the comparison triangles with the same edge lengths in the unique complete Hadamard space of curvature
-\varepsilon
).

1.1
Strongly hyperbolic space and their boundary

Fix a metric space
(X,d)
.
At a base point
o\in X
, the
Gromov product

(\mid)_{o}\colon X^{2}\to[0,+\infty)
and the
visual metametric

d_{o}\colon X^{2}\to(0,+\infty)
are defined for
x,y\in X
by:

(x\mid y)_{o}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(d(o,x)+d(o,y)-d(x,y)\right)\qquad d_{o}(x,y)=\exp\left(-(x\mid y)_{o}\right).

For
\delta>0
, the space
(X,d)
is
Gromov-
\delta
-hyperbolic
when:

\forall o,x,y,z\in X\colon\quad(x\mid z)_{o}\geq\min\{(x\mid y)_{o},(y\mid z)_{o}\}-\delta

or equivalently for every tetrahedron
(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in X^{4}
:

d(x,x^{\prime})+d(y,y^{\prime})\leq\max\{d(x,y^{\prime})+d(y,x^{\prime}),d(x,y)+d(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\}+2\delta.

For
\varepsilon>0
, the space
(X,d)
is
strongly-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolic
when:

\forall o,x,y,z\in X\colon\quad d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,z)\leq d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)+d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(y,z)

(1)

or equivalently every tetrahedron
(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\in X^{4}
satisfies Ptoleme’s inequality for
d_{o}^{\varepsilon}
:

d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime})d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(y,y^{\prime})\leq d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,y^{\prime})d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(y,x^{\prime})+d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

(2)

which can also be rewritten (independently of
o
) as a Ptoleme inequality for
\exp(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon d)
:

\exp\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(d(x,x^{\prime})+d(y,y^{\prime})\right)\leq\exp\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(d(x,y^{\prime})+d(y,x^{\prime})\right)+\exp\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(d(x,y)+d(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\right)

(3)

From now on we assume that
(X,d)
is strongly-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolic for some parameter
\varepsilon>0
. This implies (
[
52
, Theorem 4.2]
) that
(X,d)
is Gromov-
\delta
-hyperbolic for
\delta=\ln(2)/\varepsilon
.

Remark 1.1

(strong hyperbolicity is an exponential strengthening of the Gromov-hyperbolicity)
.

By
[
52
, Lemma 6.2]
, a Gromov-hyperbolic space
(X,d)
is strongly hyperbolic if and only if there exist
C,\varepsilon_{2},R_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for every tetrahedron
(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in X^{4}
and
R\geq R_{0}
with
d(x,x^{\prime})+d(y,y^{\prime})\geq R
we have
\lvert d(x,x^{\prime})+d(y,y^{\prime})-d(x,y^{\prime})-d(y,x^{\prime})\rvert\leq C\exp(-\varepsilon_{2}R)
.

We say that a sequence
(x_{n})\in X{N}

converges to infinity
when
(x_{n}\mid x_{m})_{o}\to\infty
as
m,n\to\infty
, and that two sequences
(x_{n}),(y_{n})\in X{N}
converging to infinity are
equivalent
when
(x_{n}\mid y_{m})_{o}\to\infty
as
m,n\to\infty
.
These notions do not depend on
o\in X
.
The Gromov boundary
\partial X
is the set of equivalence classes of sequences converging to infinity.

The
bordification
of
(X,d)
is the disjoint union
X\sqcup\partial X
endowed with the following topology: a subset
U\subset X\sqcup\partial X
is open when
U\cap X
is open in
X
and for all
u\in U\cap\partial X
there exists
t>0
such that
V_{o}^{t}(u)=\{v\in X\sqcup\partial X\colon(u\mid v)_{o}>t\}
is contained in
U
; which is independent of
o
and completely metrizable (see
[
18
, §3.4.2]
).

The strong hyperbolicity ensures a continuous extension to the bordification
X\sqcup\partial X
of the Gromov product
(\mid)_{o}\colon(X\sqcup\partial X)^{2}\to[0,+\infty]
(by
[
18
, Lemma 3.4.22]
) hence of the Ptolemaic metametric
d_{o}^{\varepsilon}\colon(X\sqcup\partial X)^{2}\to[0,+\infty)
where (by
[
18
, Observation 3.6.7]
) it becomes a complete metametric that is compatible with the topology on
X\sqcup\partial X
and which restricts to a metric precisely on the boundary, and this
boundary visual metric
from
o
for the parameter
\varepsilon
denoted
d_{o}^{\varepsilon}\colon(\partial X)^{2}\to[0,+\infty)
thus recovers topology of
\partial X
.

Remark 1.2

(Hamenstädt visual metric)
.

Let us recall
[
18
, Proposition 3.6.19]
. For every pair
(o,\infty)\in X\times\partial X
, there is complete metametric
d_{o,\infty}^{\varepsilon}
on
(X\sqcup\partial X)\setminus\{\infty\}
defined by:

d_{o,\infty}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\exp\left[-\varepsilon\left((x\mid y)_{o}-(x\mid\infty)_{o}-(y\mid\infty)_{o}\right)\right]=\frac{d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)}{d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,\infty)d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(y,\infty)}

(4)

which is compatible with the topology on
(X\cup\partial X)\setminus\{\infty\}
induced by that on
(X\sqcup\partial X)
, such that
B\subset(X\sqcup\partial X)\setminus\{\infty\}
is bounded if and only if
\infty\notin\overline{B}

; and, moreover, the restriction of
d_{o,\infty}^{\varepsilon}
to
\partial X\setminus\{\infty\}
is a metric called the Hamenstädt visual metric.

Note that for all
x\in(X\sqcup\partial X)\setminus\{\infty\}
we have
d_{o,\infty}^{\varepsilon}(x,o)=1/d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,\infty)
.

Remark 1.3

(Buseman function)
.

For
\xi\in X
, the
Buseman function

\Bus_{\xi}\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
is defined for
y,z\in X
by
\Bus_{\xi}(y,z)=d(y,\xi)-d(z,\xi)=(z\mid\xi)_{y}-(y\mid\xi)_{z}
.
The latter formula extends continuously to
\xi\in X\sqcup\partial X
by
[
18
, Lemma 3.4.10]
, where it remains invariant under the diagonal action of
\Gamma
on
(X\cup\partial X)\times X^{2}
.
It satisfies for all
\xi\in(X\cup\partial X)
and
x,y,z\in X
the triangle inequality
\lvert\Bus_{\xi}(y,z)\rvert\leq d(y,z)
and the additivity
\Bus_{\xi}(y,z)=\Bus_{\xi}(y,x)+\Bus_{\xi}(x,z)
.

The following class of strongly hyperbolic spaces plays an important role in this work.

Definition 1.4

(real tree)
.

A metric space
(T,d)
is called a
real tree
when any two points are joined by a unique injective segment and such segments are isometries.

Remark 1.5

(connected Gromov-
0
-hyperbolic)
.

A metric space
(T,d)
is a real tree if and only if it is connected and Gromov-
0
-hyperbolic (see
[
25
, Chapter 3]
).
The Gromov-
0
-hyperbolicity means that every quadruple
o,x,y,z\in T
satisfies
(x\mid z)_{o}=\max\{(x\mid y)_{o},(y\mid z)_{o}\}
, namely for every
o\in T
the visual metametric
d_{o}\colon T\times T\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is an ultrametric.

The following lemma shows that one can interpolate the points of a
0
-hyperbolic metric space minimally so as to obtain a real tree which is uniquely defined up to equivariant isometry.

Proposition 1.6

(embedding
0
-hyperbolic spaces in real trees)
.

Let
(X,d)
be a
0
-hyperbolic metric space.
There is a real tree
T
and an isometric embedding
\varphi\colon X\to T
such that
T=\bigcup_{x,y\in X}[\varphi(x),\varphi(y)]
and a representation
\Isom(X)\to\Isom(T)
such that
\varphi
is equivariant.

Proof.

Part of this well-known fact are proposed in
[
29
, Exercise 8]
.
A proof of the full proposition follows from that of the very similar
[
25
, Theorem 3.38]
(one should only check that their construction does not depend on their initial choice of base point).
∎

Remark 1.7

(connected strongly-
(+\infty)
-hyperbolic)
.

A real tree is
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
hence strongly hyperbolic with parameter
1

[
52
, Theorem 5.1]
.
Moreover, since the definition of real trees is invariant under rescaling the metric by a positive constant, a real tree is strongly-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolic for all
\varepsilon\in(0,+\infty)
.
Conversely, a connected metric space that is strongly-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolic for all
\varepsilon\in(0,+\infty)
is Gromov-
0
-hyperbolic hence a real tree.

1.2
Cross-ratio for strongly hyperbolic spaces

Let
(X,d)
be a strongly hyperbolic metric space.
The
cross-ratio
of
(x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime})\in X^{2}\times X^{2}
is the positive real number:

\left[{x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime}}\right]=\exp\tfrac{1}{2}\left(d(x,x^{\prime})+d(y,y^{\prime})-d(x,y^{\prime})-d(y,x^{\prime})\right).

(5)

The name cross-ratio is justified by the fact that for all
o\in X
we have:

\left[{x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime}}\right]=\frac{d_{o}(x,x^{\prime})\times d_{o}(y,y^{\prime})}{d_{o}(x,y^{\prime})\times d_{o}(y,x^{\prime})}.

(6)

The cross-ratio is invariant under the
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}
-action which exchanges the pair or inverts both of their orientations, in formulae:

\left[{x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime}}\right]=\left[{y,y^{\prime};x,x^{\prime}}\right]=\left[{x^{\prime},x;y^{\prime},y}\right].

(Invariance)

Moreover, it satisfies the inversion rule given by swapping either
(x,y)
or
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
:

\left[{x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime}}\right]=\left[{y,x^{\prime};x,y^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}=\left[{x,y^{\prime};y,x^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}.

(Inversion)

More generally it satisfies the cocycle relation, as for every
x^{\prime\prime}\in X
we have:

\left[{x,y;x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\right]=\left[{x,y;x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime}}\right]\times\left[{x^{\prime\prime},y;x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\right].

(Cocycle)

(Note that this notion of cross-ratio can be defined for any metric space
(X,d)
, and according to Equation (
3
) it can be used to characterize the strong-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolicity by saying that all tetrahedra
(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\in X^{4}
satisfy
1\leq\left[{x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime}}\right]^{-\varepsilon}+\left[{x,x^{\prime};y^{\prime},y}\right]^{-\varepsilon}
.)

The strong hyperbolicity ensures that the cross-ratio extends continuously to quadruples of distinct points in the bordification
(X\sqcup\partial X)
with values in
(0,\infty)
on which it defines a continuous function that is invariant under the diagonal action of
\Isom(X,d)
on
(\partial X)^{(4)}
, satisfying the (
Invariance
), (
Inversion
) and (
Cocycle
) relations.

Moreover, the cross-ratio further extends by continuity to the subspace
\partial^{4}X\subset\left(\partial X\right)^{4}
of quadruples of which at least three are distinct with values in
[0,\infty]
, and by the separation property of the boundary visual metric, it satisfies:

\displaystyle\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]=0

\displaystyle\iff(a^{-}=a^{+})\;\mathrm{or}\;(b^{-}=b^{+})

(
\left[{\cdot}\right]=0
)

\displaystyle\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]=\infty

\displaystyle\iff(a^{-}=b^{+})\;\mathrm{or}\;(b^{-}=a^{+}).

(
\left[{\cdot}\right]=\infty
)

Note that if
a^{-}=b^{-}
or
a^{+}=b^{+}
, we have
[a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}]=1
, but the converse may often fail.

In formula, recalling that
\partial X
is endowed for each
o\in X
with the visual distance
d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\exp\left(-\varepsilon(x\mid y)_{o}\right)
, we have for
(a^{-},a^{+},b^{-},b^{+})\in\partial^{4}X
:

\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]^{\varepsilon}=\frac{d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(a^{-},a^{+})\times d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(b^{-},b^{+})}{d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(a^{-},b^{+})\times d_{o}^{\varepsilon}(b^{-},a^{+})}

(7)

Remark 1.8

(cross-ratio on
\partial^{4}X
)
.

We introduced the cross-ratio on
X^{4}
only to define it conveniently on
\partial^{4}X
and clarify its properties.
We use the same denomination and notation for both, but will mostly work with its restriction to
\partial^{4}X
.

Remark 1.9

(Conventions)
.

Several other conventions for the cross-ratio are used, both regarding the order of the variables in the brackets and the actual formula.

Our formula and order are compatible with those of Labourie in
[
41
,
40
]
. Our formula for
\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]
matches the one defined by Bourdon in
[
9
]
for
[\xi\xi^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}]
with the variables in the order
(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime})=(a^{-},b^{-},a^{+},b^{+})
.

The formula introduced by Otal in
[
58
]
is the logarithm of that in Bourdon, hence the logarithm of ours after swapping the variables
(a^{+}b^{-})
in the bracket.

In
[
39
]
, Kim uses the square of the definition of Bourdon in
[
9
]
, hence the square of ours after swapping the variables
(a^{-}b^{+})
in the bracket.

1.3
Isometries of strongly hyperbolic spaces

The isometry group
\Isom(X)
is endowed with the pointwise-convergence topology induced by the product topology on the set of functions
X^{X}
.

An isometry between strongly hyperbolic spaces preserves the finite cross-ratio, and it extends to a homeomorphism between their boundaries which preserves the boundary cross-ratio.
In particular, the elements and subgroups of
\Isom(X,d)
act on
X\sqcup\partial X
by homeomorphisms, and can thus be classified according to their dynamical behaviour.

For
\gamma\in\Isom(X,d)
its
stable translation length
is defined for any base point
o\in X
by:

\ell(\gamma)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{n}d(o,\gamma^{n}o)=\inf\{\tfrac{1}{n}d(o,\gamma^{n}o)\colon n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\}

(8)

Note that for all
\alpha\in\Isom(X,d)
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
\ell(\alpha\gamma\alpha^{-1})=\ell(\gamma)
and
\ell(\gamma^{n})=\lvert n\rvert\ell(\gamma)
.

Let us recall
[
54
, Theorem 1.9]
.

Theorem 1.10

(continuity of stable translation-length)
.

When
\Isom(X)
is endowed with the pointwise-convergence topology, the function
\ell\colon\Isom(X)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is continuous.

The following is an equivalent reformulation of
[
18
, Theorem 6.1.4]
.

Proposition 1.11

(classification of isometries)
.

A non-trivial
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
is either:

elliptic
:

the
\gamma
-orbit of any point of
X
has
0
accumulation points in
\partial X
;

parabolic
:

the
\gamma
-orbit of any point of
X
has
1
accumulation point in
\partial X
;

loxodromic
:

the
\gamma
-orbit of any point of
X
has
2
accumulation points in
\partial X
.

It follows that a non-trivial element
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
is elliptic if and only if the
\gamma
-orbit of any point of
X
is bounded.
A loxodromic element
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
has a pair of (attractive, repulsive) fixed points
(\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+})\in(\partial X)\times(\partial X)
.

The following classification is
[
18
, Theorem 6.2.3]
.

Theorem 1.12

(classification of group actions)
.

A sub(semi)group
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X)
is either:

elliptic
:

when the
\Gamma
-orbit of some any point of
X
is bounded;

loxodromic
:

when
\Gamma
contains a loxodromic element;

parabolic
:

when it is neither elliptic nor hyperbolic;

and parabolic subgroups must have a unique
\Gamma
-fixed point in
\partial X
.

Hence the translation length vanishes on
\Gamma
if and only if it is elliptic or parabolic.
However, beware that some parabolic subgroups contain only elliptic elements
[
18
, Example 11.2.18]
.

Definition 1.13

(limit set)
.

A subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X)
has
limit set
defined for any
o\in X
by:

\Lambda(\Gamma)=\{c\in\partial X\colon\ \exists(\gamma_{n})\in G^{\mathbb{N}},\,\gamma_{n}o\to c\}.

Thus
\Gamma
is of non-elliptic type if and only if
\Lambda(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset
in which case
\Lambda(\Gamma)
is the minimal non-empty closed
\Gamma
-invariant subset of
\partial X
(
[
18
, Proposition 7.4.1]
).
Note that
\Card\Lambda(\Gamma)\geq 2
if and only if
\Gamma
is loxodromic.

By
[
18
, Proposition 7.3.1]
, a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X)
has limit set satisfying either
\Card\Lambda(\Gamma)\leq 2
or
\Card\Lambda(\Gamma)\geq\Card(\mathbb{R})
.
The following is
[
18
, Propositions 7.4.7]
.

Proposition 1.14

(loxodromic axes are dense)
.

For a loxodromic subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X)
,
the subset of pairs
(\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+})\in(\partial X)\times(\partial X)
of (repulsive, attractive) fixed points of loxodromic
\gamma\in\Gamma
is contained and dense in
\Lambda(\Gamma)\times\Lambda(\Gamma)
.

A representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(X)
inherits all objects and attributes of its image
\rho(\Gamma)\subset\Isom(X)
, in particular its translation length function denoted
\ell_{\rho}
, its limit set denoted
\Lambda(\rho)
, its type (elliptic, parabolic, loxodromic), et caetera.

Remark 1.15

(infimum displacement)
.

When
(X,d)
is also
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
, the
[
10
, Exercise II.6.(1)]
leads to showing that the translation length of
\gamma\in\Isom(X,d)
equals its
infimum displacement
:

\ell(\gamma)=\inf\{d(o,\gamma(o))\colon o\in X\}.

In that case, for a loxodromic
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
, there exists a unique geodesic connecting its fixed points
(\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+})
, and that is
\{o\in X\colon d(o,\gamma o)=\ell(\gamma)\}
.
This follows from the description of loxodromic-axes in
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
spaces by
[
10
, Theorem 6.8]
, and the closeness of long geodesic segments with close endpoints in strongly hyperbolic spaces as measured by
Remark

1.1
.

In particular, denoting the limit set of
\Isom(X)
by
\Lambda
, this yields a dense subset of
\Lambda\times\Lambda
that are joined by a unique geodesic.

Question 1.16

(when strongly-
1
-hyperbolic implies
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
)
.

Let
(X,d)
be a strongly-
1
-hyperbolic space that is
\operatorname{CAT(0)}
: when is it
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
? (a converse to
[
52
, Theorem 4.2]
).

1.4
Characterizing subgroups by length functions and cross-ratios

This subsection shows that the translation length function or cross-ratio contains enough information to recognize which subgroups of
\Isom(\Gamma)
have limit set of cardinal
\geq 3
(hence uncountably infinite), and when so that they determine one another.

Recall from
Remark

1.3
the definition and properties of Buseman functions for strongly hyperbolic spaces.
We will first recover from Buseman functions the translation lengths (never mentioned in
[
18
]
, where only dynamical derivatives are considered
[
18
, §4.2.3]
).

Lemma 1.17

(translation length from Buseman)
.

Consider a loxodromic
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
with (attractive, repulsive) fixed points
(\gamma^{+},\gamma^{-})
.
For every
o\in X
we have

\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma o)=\ell(\gamma)=\Bus_{\gamma^{-}}(\gamma o,o).

In particular, the dynamical derivative defined in
[
18
, §4.2.3]
for the parameter
b=e
, of the loxodromic element
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
at the points
\gamma^{\pm}\in\partial X
is
\exp(\pm\ell(\gamma))
.

Proof.

By antisymmetry and
\Gamma
-invariance of the Buseman function we have
\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma^{-1}o)=-\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(\gamma^{-1}o,o)=-\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma o)
.
Moreover the second equality follows by applying the first to
\gamma^{-1}
and
\gamma o
, using
\ell(\gamma^{-1})=\ell(\gamma)
and
(\gamma^{-1})^{+}=\gamma^{-}
.

For all
o\in X
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have, using additivity and equivariance of the Buseman function
\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
that
\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma^{n}o)=\sum_{1}^{n}\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(\gamma^{k-1}o,\gamma^{k}o)=n\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma o)
.

From the
[
18
, Corollary 3.4.12]
applied to the identity
[
18
, Proposition 3.3.3 (h)]
we have

\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma^{-n}o)=-d(o,\gamma^{-n}o)+2(o\mid\gamma^{+})_{\gamma^{-n}o}

but
(o\mid\gamma^{+})_{\gamma^{-n}o}\rightarrow(o\mid\gamma^{+})_{\gamma^{-}}\in\mathbb{R}
.

By equivariance and antisymmetry
\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma^{n}o)=-\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(\gamma^{n}o,o)=-\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma^{-n}o)

so

\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma o)=\lim_{n}\tfrac{1}{n}\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma^{n}o)=-\lim_{n}\tfrac{1}{n}\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma^{-n}o)=-\lim_{n}\frac{-d(o,\gamma^{-n}o)}{n}=\ell(\gamma)
.
∎

Lemma 1.18

(translation-length from cross-ratio)
.

An element
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
is loxodromic if and only if
\ell(\gamma)>0
.
In that case, it has (repulsive, attractive) fixed points
(\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+})\in\partial^{2}X
and:

\forall\xi\in X\sqcup\partial X\setminus\{\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\}\colon\quad\ell(\gamma)=\log\left[{\gamma\xi,\gamma^{-};\xi,\gamma^{+}}\right]

(9)

Proof.

The criterion on
\ell(\gamma)>0
is
[
15
, Chapter 10, Proposition 6.3]
.
Now assume
\ell(\gamma)>0
.

For all
o\in X
we compute that
\log\left[{\gamma o,\gamma^{-},o,\gamma^{+}}\right]=\tfrac{1}{2}(\Bus_{\gamma^{-}}(\gamma o,o)+\Bus_{\gamma^{+}}(o,\gamma o))
and by
[
18
, Proposition 4.2.16]
both terms are equal to the translation length
\ell(\gamma)
by
Lemma

1.17
.
(One may also work in the polar coordinates of
(\gamma^{-},o,\gamma^{+})
and apply
[
18
, Lemma 4.6.3]
.)
The continuous extension of the cross-ratio to quadruple of distinct points in the bordification shows that the identity holds in the limit where
o\in X
converges to
\xi\in X\sqcup\partial X\setminus\{\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\}
.
∎

Lemma 1.19

(cross-ratio from translation-length)
.

For loxodromic
\alpha,\beta\in\Isom(X)
with distinct (attractive, repulsive) fixed points
(\alpha^{+},\alpha^{-}),(\beta^{+},\beta^{-})
:

\left[{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+};\beta^{-},\beta^{+}}\right]=\lim_{m,n\to+\infty}\exp\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\ell(\alpha^{m})+\ell(\beta^{n})-\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})\right)

(10)

Remark 1.20

(another proof for
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
)
.

The
Equation

10
is shown for rank one symmetric spaces in
[
39
, Theorem 1]
.
One may adapt its proof whenever a loxodromic
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
has a unique geodesic translation axis connecting its fixed points given by
\{o\in X\colon d(o,\gamma o)=\ell(\gamma)\}
, for instance when
X
is strongly hyperbolic and
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
by
Remark

1.15
.

This proof does not generalize so easily to the general setting of strongly hyperbolic spaces.
Our proof exploits the continuity and precise asymptotics for Buseman functions and Gromov products over triples of distinct points in the bordification (
[
18
, 3.4.10 & 3.4.12]
)

Proof.

Observe that for
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
we have
\alpha^{m}(\beta^{n}\alpha^{m})^{\pm}=(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{\pm}
and
\beta^{n}(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{\pm}=(\beta^{n}\alpha^{m})^{\pm}
.
Let
o\in X
.
By
Lemma

1.17
we have
\ell(\alpha^{m})+\ell(\beta^{n})=\Bus_{\alpha^{+}}(\alpha^{-m}o,o)+\Bus_{\beta^{+}}(\beta^{-n}o,o)
and using the antisymmetry and additivity of the Buseman function we have:

-\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})=\Bus_{(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{+}}(\alpha^{m}o,\beta^{-n}o)=\Bus_{(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{+}}(\alpha^{m}o,o)+\Bus_{(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{+}}(o,\beta^{-n}o).

We may rewrite the term
\Bus_{(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{+}}(\alpha^{m}o,o)=\Bus_{(\beta^{n}\alpha^{m})^{+}}(o,\alpha^{-m}o)
using
\alpha^{m}
-invariance of the Buseman function and
\alpha^{-n}(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{+}=(\beta^{n}\alpha^{m})^{+}
.

We say that two real sequences
s_{k},s_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
indexed by
k\in\mathbb{N}
are asymptotic and denote
s_{k}\asymp s_{k}^{\prime}
when
\lim_{k\to+\infty}(s_{k}-s_{k}^{\prime})=0
.
For sequences depending on several variables, we put the variable(s) tending to infinity in index.

As
m\to+\infty
we have
(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{+}\to\alpha^{+}
hence

\Bus_{(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})^{+}}(o,\beta^{-n}o)\asymp_{m}\Bus_{\alpha^{+}}(o,\beta^{-n}o)
,
and as
n\to+\infty
we have
(\beta^{n}\alpha^{m})^{+}\to\beta^{+}
hence

\Bus_{(\beta^{n}\alpha^{m})^{+}}(o,\alpha^{-m}o)\asymp_{n}\Bus_{\beta^{+}}(o,\alpha^{-m}o)
.
Hence
-\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})\asymp_{m,n}\Bus_{\alpha^{+}}(o,\beta^{-n}o)+\Bus_{\beta^{+}}(o,\alpha^{-m}o)

so using once again antisymmetry and additivity of the Buseman function we have

\ell(\alpha^{m})+\ell(\beta^{n})-\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})\asymp_{m,n}\Bus_{\alpha^{+}}(\alpha^{-m}o,\beta^{-n}o)+\Bus_{\beta^{+}}(\beta^{-n}o,\alpha^{-m}o)

and expanding in terms of Gromov-products, using its continuity over triples in the bordification (
[
18
, Corollary ]
), and expressing everything in terms of distances, we have that
\ell(\alpha^{m})+\ell(\beta^{n})-\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})
is asymptotic to:

\displaystyle\asymp(\alpha^{+},\beta^{-n}o)_{\alpha^{-m}o}-(\alpha^{+},\alpha^{-m}o)_{\beta^{-n}o}+(\beta^{+},\alpha^{-m}o)_{\beta^{-n}o}-(\beta^{+},\beta^{-n}o)_{\alpha^{-m}o}

\displaystyle\asymp(\alpha^{m}o,\beta^{-n}o)_{\alpha^{-m}o}-(\alpha^{m}o,\alpha^{-m}o)_{\beta^{-n}o}+(\beta^{n}o,\alpha^{-m}o)_{\beta^{-n}o}-(\beta^{n}o,\beta^{-n}o)_{\alpha^{-m}o}

\displaystyle=d(\alpha^{m}o,\alpha^{-m}o)+d(\beta^{n}o,\beta^{-n}o)-d(\alpha^{m}o,\beta^{-n}o)-d(\beta^{n}o,\alpha^{-m}o)

\displaystyle\asymp 2\log\left[{\alpha^{+},\alpha^{-};\beta^{+},\beta^{-}}\right]

as desired.
∎

Remark 1.21
.

Consider loxodromic
\alpha,\beta\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
with distinct fixed points.
Applying identity (
10
) to
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\alpha,\beta^{-1})
, and taking the ratio yields:

\tfrac{\left[{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+};\beta^{+},\beta^{-}}\right]}{\left[{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+};\beta^{-},\beta^{+}}\right]}=\lim_{m,n\to+\infty}\exp\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})-\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{-n})\right)

(11)

which measures the
complex distance
between the axes
(\alpha^{+},\alpha^{-})
and
(\beta^{+},\beta^{-})
(see
Remark

2.10
).

Proposition 1.22

(translation length and limit set cross-ratio)
.

For a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X)
with infinite limit set
\Lambda(\Gamma)\subset\partial X
, its translation length function
\ell\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
determines and is determined by the cross-ratio on
\Lambda(\Gamma)^{4}\cap\partial^{4}X
.

Proof.

The cross-ratio determines the translation length since by assumption, for any loxodromic
\gamma\in\Gamma
we may choose
\xi\in\Lambda(\Gamma)\setminus\{\gamma^{\pm}\}
, hence use equation (
9
).
The translation length determines the cross-ratio on
\Lambda^{4}\cap\partial^{4}X
because by
Proposition

1.14
the set of quadruples of distinct points of the form
(\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+};\beta^{-},\beta^{+})
for loxodromic
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
is dense in
\Lambda(\Gamma)^{4}
, so we conclude using equation (
10
) and continuity of the cross-ratio on
\partial^{4}X
.
∎

We will need the following lemma to characterize when a subgroup contains two elements with disjoint fixed points. A function between two topological spaces is called
proper
when the preimage of a compact subset is compact.

Lemma 1.23

(equal fixed points from length function)
.

Consider loxodromic
\alpha,\beta\in\Isom(X)
, and denote
\Mon(\alpha,\beta)
and
\Grp(\alpha,\beta)
the monoid and group that they generate.

We have
\{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+}\}\cap\{\beta^{-},\beta^{+}\}=\emptyset\iff\exists p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\colon(m,n)\mapsto\ell(\alpha^{pm}\beta^{pn})
is proper on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

We have
(\alpha^{+}=\beta^{+})\vee(\alpha^{-}=\beta^{-})\iff\ell\colon\Mon(\alpha,\beta)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a morphism of monoids.

We have
(\alpha^{+}=\beta^{-})\vee(\alpha^{-}=\beta^{+})\iff\ell\colon\Mon(\alpha,\beta^{-1})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a morphism of monoids.

In particular
\alpha,\beta
have disjoint fixed point pairs if and only if
\ell\colon\Grp(\alpha,\beta)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is not the absolute value of a homomorphism to
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We will first show all the implications
\implies
.

Suppose that
\{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+}\}\cap\{\beta^{-},\beta^{+}\}=\emptyset
.
Fix a base point
o\in X
and for
\delta>0
and consider the open neighbourhood of
\xi\in\partial X
defined by
V_{o}^{\delta}(\eta)=\{\xi\in\partial X\colon(\eta\mid\xi)_{o}<\delta\}
.
Choose
\delta>0
so that
V_{o}^{\delta}(\alpha^{\pm}),V_{o}^{\delta}(\beta^{\pm})
are all disjoint at positive distance from one another for the visual metric
d^{\varepsilon}_{o}
.
There exist
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\alpha^{\pm p}
sends the complement of
V_{o}^{\delta}(\alpha^{\mp})
into
U_{o}^{\delta}(\alpha^{\pm})
and similarly
\beta^{\pm k}
sends the complement of
V_{o}^{\delta}(\beta^{\mp})
into
U_{o}^{\delta}(\beta^{\pm})
.
In such a Schottky position, we may play ping-pong to deduce that the elements
a=\alpha^{p}
and
a=\beta^{p}
freely generate a rank-
2
free group.
Denoting the
\{a^{\pm 1},b^{\pm 1}\}
-word length by
\lvert\cdot\rvert\colon\Grp(a,b)\to\mathbb{N}
, there is by
[
18
, Equation (10.3.6)]
a
C\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all reduced
\gamma=a^{m_{1}}b^{n_{1}}\cdots a^{m_{l}}b^{n_{l}}\in\Grp(a,b)
, we have, denoting
C^{\prime}=C\min\{\ell(a),\ell(b)\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, that

d(\gamma o,o)\geq C\sum_{k=1}^{l}d(a^{n_{k}}o,o)+d(b^{m_{k}}o,o)\geq C\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lvert m_{k}\rvert\ell(a)+\lvert n_{k}\rvert\ell(b)\geq C^{\prime}\lvert\gamma\rvert

In particular, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that
\gamma=a^{m}b^{n}
satisfies
\lvert\gamma^{l}\rvert=l\lvert\gamma\rvert
hence
\ell(\gamma)\geq C^{\prime}\lvert\gamma\rvert
proving properness of the length function on the subset
\{a^{n}b^{m}\colon n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

Suppose that
\alpha^{+}=\beta^{+}
.
Every non-trivial element
\gamma\in\Mon(\alpha,\beta)
is loxodromic with attractive fixed point
\gamma^{+}=\alpha^{+}=\beta^{+}
, and using
[
18
, Proposition 4.2.16]
at that point yields the additivity of translation lengths (the cocycle rule f or
\ell(\gamma)=\log\lvert\left[{\gamma\xi,\gamma^{-},\xi,\gamma^{+}}\right]\rvert
).
The implications of
\alpha^{-}=\beta^{-}
and of
(\alpha^{+}=\beta^{-})\vee(\alpha^{-}=\beta^{+})
follow by applying the previous one to
(\alpha^{-1},\beta^{-1})
and to
(\alpha^{+1},\beta^{-1})
.

Now we may easily prove the converse implications
\impliedby
.

If
\alpha,\beta
do not have disjoint fixed point pairs then any of the previous morphism properties implies that for all
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
the function
(m,n)\mapsto\ell(\alpha^{pm},\beta^{pn})
is not proper on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

If
\ell\colon\Mon(\alpha,\beta)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a morphism, then
\forall p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
the function
(m,n)\mapsto\ell(\alpha^{pm},\beta^{pn})
is not proper on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, so the contrapositive of the first implication implies
\{\alpha^{\pm}\}\cap\{\beta^{\pm}\}\neq\emptyset
. If
\alpha^{+}\neq\beta^{+}
and
\alpha^{-}\neq\beta^{-}
, then
\alpha^{+}=\beta^{-}
or
\alpha^{-}=\beta^{+}
.
By the previous discussion, we deduce that
\ell\colon\Mon(\alpha,\beta^{-1})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a morphism of monoids.
Consequently
\ell\colon\Grp(\alpha,\beta)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a group homomorphism, hence it is trivial, namely
\ell(\beta)=0
, a contradiction.
∎

2
Geometry of the algebraic hyperbolic space

For a cardinal
\kappa
, let
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
be the real algebraic hyperbolic space of dimension
\kappa
.
Denote by
\infty
the infinite countable cardinal, thus
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
is the separable infinite dimensional hyperbolic space.
The metric space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
, thus strongly hyperbolic for
\varepsilon=1
by
[
52
, Theorem 5.1]
.
