Consequently all the notions and results in the previous section apply.
In this section will recall the refinements of these results and the additional structures that will be needed in the sequel.

2.1
Models in other geometries: Cartan and Iwasawa decompositions

Minkowski-Lorentz hyperboloid model

Denote the Hilbert space of dimension
\kappa
by
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
.
The Minkowski space of dimension
1+\kappa
is the oriented vector space
\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
with the symmetric bilinear form of signature
(1,\kappa)
defined for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
by
\langle x,y\rangle=x_{0}y_{0}-\sum_{i>0}x_{i}y_{i}
.
Its isotropic cone
\mathbf{X}_{0}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}\colon\langle x,x\rangle=0\}
is asymptotic to the
2
-sheet hyperboloid
\mathbf{X}_{+1}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}\colon\langle x,x\rangle=+1\}
and the
1
-sheet hyperboloid
\mathbf{X}_{-1}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}\colon\langle x,x\rangle=-1\}
.

The
hyperboloid model
of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is obtained by endowing the upper-half component of the
2
-sheeted hyperboloid
\{x\in\mathbf{X}_{+1}\colon x_{0}>0\}
with the distance
\dH
given by

\cosh(\dH(x,y))=\langle x\mid y\rangle
.

Cayley-Klein projective model

The
projective model
for
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is the subset
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{+1}
of lines inside the cone, with the distance
\dH([x],[y])
given in terms of the hyperbolic angle by:

\cosh(\dH([x],[y]))=\tfrac{\langle x,y\rangle}{\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle\langle y,y\rangle}}

(12)

Here, the boundary
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
identifies with the projectivized isotropic cone
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{0}
.
Note that the upper component
\{x\in\mathbf{X}_{+1}\colon x_{0}>0\}
yields a section to the projectivization
\mathbf{X}_{+1}\to\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{+1}
.

Hence, the isometry group
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
identifies with the projective orthogonal group
\PO(1,\kappa)
(one can mimic the proof in finite dimension
[
10
, Theorem I.2.24]
).
It acts transitively on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{+1}
, and the stabilizer of
o=\mathbf{P}(1,0^{\kappa})
is the projective orthogonal group
\operatorname{PO}(\kappa)
, which acts freely transitively on orthonormal frames at
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.

Euclidean ball model and Cartan decomposition

The
Euclidean ball model
is obtained from the projective space
\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
by choosing the Euclidean chart
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}\colon x_{0}=1\}
with origin
o=(1,0^{\kappa})
so that the quadric pair
(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{+1})
is identified with the unit sphere and unit open ball
(\mathbf{S}^{\kappa-1},\mathbf{B}^{\kappa})
.
At the centre
o
, the tangent space of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
with its induced inner product is isometric to that of
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
with its Euclidean inner product.
The geodesic map from the unit tangent sphere at
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
to the Gromov boundary
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is a homeomorphism, and under the identification with
\mathbf{S}^{\kappa-1}
the visual distance is given by
d_{o}(x,y)=\sin\tfrac{1}{2}(\widehat{xoy})
(see
[
9
, §1.2]
or
[
18
, Lemma 3.5.1]
).
This yields an equivariant identification between isometry group of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and the conformal group of
\mathbf{S}^{\kappa-1}
.

Let us recall the Cartan decomposition of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.
Consider polar coordinates for
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
given by the bi-infinite geodesic
(0,\infty)\in(\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})^{(2)}
passing through our fixed point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
The stabilizer of such a geodesic contains a one parameter loxodromic group
A\colon\mathbb{R}\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
with
A(t)
acting on this axis by translation of
\lvert\ell(A(t))\rvert=t
.
Consider an orthonormal frame at a point
x\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
: on may act by
\operatorname{O}(\kappa)
to send
x
to the intersection of the geodesic arc
[o,\infty)
with the sphere
\mathbf{S}_{o}(\dH(o,x))
; then by
A(-d(o,x))
to bring this new point back to
o
; and finally by
\operatorname{PO}(\kappa)
to adjust the frame.
Thus, every
\gamma\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
has a unique factorization
\gamma=\gamma_{1}A(t)\gamma_{2}
with
\gamma_{i}\in\operatorname{PO}(\kappa)
.

Affine model and special dimension
\leq 3
.

The
affine model
for the boundary of the hyperbolic space
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is obtained in the projective space
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa})
by projecting
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{0}
from a point
\infty\in\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{0}
onto the tangent space at another point
0\in\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{0}
, yielding an identification
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}=\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}\sqcup\{\infty\}
hence an identification
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}=\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}\times(0,\infty)
where the metric is
(dx+dy)/y^{2}
.
The group
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})=\PO(1,\kappa)
acts transitively on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}=\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
and the stabilizer of
\infty\in\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
is the group of affine similitudes
\Isom(\mathbb{R}^{\kappa})\rtimes\mathbb{R}_{>0}=(\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}\rtimes\operatorname{O}(\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}))\rtimes\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

For
\kappa=2,3
we find the upper-half-space models
\mathbf{H}^{2}\simeq(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{H}^{3}\simeq(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{C}
identifying
\partial\mathbf{H}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{1}\simeq\{\infty\}\sqcup\mathbb{R}
and
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}\simeq\{\infty\}\sqcup\mathbb{C}
.
The action of the pair
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{2})\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{3})
on the pair of boundaries
\partial\mathbf{H}^{2}\subset\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}
identifies with the action of the pair of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\subset\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on the pair of projective lines
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
.
Recall that the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is simply transitive on triples of distinct points.
From this it follows that
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
acts triply transitively on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, since one may act on a triple of distinct points by moving one point at a time (and reordering the triple) while remaining each time in a copy of
\mathbf{H}^{3}
, until reaching any other triple of distinct points.
After reordering the triples the initial triple
(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0})
and target triple
(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1})
so that their common points appear at the beginning in the same order, we then successively map
(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0})\mapsto(x_{0},y_{0},z_{1})\mapsto(x_{0},y_{1},z_{1})\mapsto(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1})
.

2.2
Convex hull and Hausdorff distance to the
1
-skeleton

For a subset
X\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\sqcup\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, its
span

\Span(X)
is the smallest closed totally geodesic and complete subspace
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}}
containing all geodesics between pairs of points in
X
: in the linear and projective linear models, it corresponds to the linear and projective linear spans, intersected with
\mathbf{X}_{+1}
and
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{+1}
.
Such a subset
X
is called
total
when it spans the whole space and is called
independent
when any subset of
n\in\mathbb{N}
points in
X
span a totally geodesic copy of
\mathbf{H}^{n-1}
.
In particular, two distinct geodesics in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
span a unique totally geodesic copy of
\mathbf{H}^{2}
or
\mathbf{H}^{3}
.

For a subset
X\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\sqcup\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, its
convex hull

\Conv(X)\subset\Span(X)\cup\partial\Span(X)
is the smallest closed geodesically convex subset that contains it:
in the linear and projective linear models, it corresponds to the linear and projective linear closed convex hulls, intersected with the closed cone
\mathbf{X}_{\geq 0}
and filled quadric
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{\geq 0}
.
The convex hull of
X
can be constructed inductively, by considering its skeleta of increasing dimensions: the
k
-skeleton

X^{(k)}
is the union of all geodesic simplices constructed on
1+k
points of
X
.
For instance,
X^{(1)}=\bigcup_{x_{1},x_{2}\in X}[x_{1},x_{2}]
.

We obtained the following in
[
20
, Theorem 0.4]
as a corollary of a more precise result concerning hyperbolic simplices, providing optimal bounds for the Hausdorff distances between their skeleta of various dimensions, and characterizing the simplices achieving that bound.

Proposition 2.1

(Hausdorff distance from convex hull to
1
-skeleton)
.

For a set of points
X\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\cup\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, every point of its convex hull
\Conv(X)
is at distance at most
\sinh^{-1}(1)=\log(1+\sqrt{2})
from its
1
-skeleton
X^{(1)}=\bigcup_{x_{1},x_{2}\in Y}[x_{1},x_{2}]
.

Remark 2.2

(uniform bound)
.

The fact that in finite dimensions
\kappa
, the distance to the
1
-skeleton is uniformly bounded (independently of
Y
) was used in
[
3
, Theorem 3]
, which refers to
[
5
]
.
We were motivated to obtain the uniformity of this bound on the dimension, as it will enable the statement about Gromov-Hausdorff convergence of our Theorem
5.11
(proved in Step 7) to hold for infinite dimensions
\kappa_{m}
.

We refer to
[
20
]
for the optimal value of the bound and other interesting results concerning the characterization of hyperbolic simplices with maximal inradius.

2.3
Cross-ratio for algebraic hyperbolic spaces

Let us express the cross-ratio on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
in terms of the Hilbert-Minkowski model
(\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa},\langle,\rangle)
.

Lemma 2.3
.

For quadruples of distinct points
a^{-},a^{+},b^{-},b^{+}
in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, and for any of their representatives in the isotropic cone
\mathbf{X}_{0}
of the Hilbert-Minkowski space
(\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa},\langle\ ,\ \rangle)
, we have

[a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}]^{2}=\frac{\langle a^{-},a^{+}\rangle\langle b^{-},b^{+}\rangle}{\langle a^{-},b^{+}\rangle\langle b^{-},a^{+}\rangle}

(13)

Proof.

Consider sequences
x_{n},x^{\prime}_{n},y_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{+1}
respectively converging to
a^{-},a^{+},b^{-},b^{+}\in\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X}_{0}
.
By Equation (
12
), we have:

\frac{\langle x_{n},x^{\prime}_{n}\rangle\langle y_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}\rangle}{\langle x_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}\rangle\langle y_{n},x^{\prime}_{n}\rangle}=\frac{\cosh(\dH(x_{n},x^{\prime}_{n}))\cosh(\dH(y_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}))}{\cosh(\dH(x_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}))\cosh(\dH(y_{n},x^{\prime}_{n}))}

The left-hand side converges to
\frac{\langle a^{-},a^{+}\rangle\langle b^{-},b^{+}\rangle}{\langle a^{-},b^{+}\rangle\langle b^{-},b^{+}\rangle}
.
By the defining Equation (
5
) for the cross-ratio in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, and the limit
\cosh(r)/e^{r}\to 1/2
as
r\to+\infty
, the right-hand side is asymptotic to
\left[{x_{n},x^{\prime}_{n},y_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}}\right]^{2}
as
n\to\infty
, which converges to
\left[{a^{-},a^{+},b^{-},b^{+}}\right]^{2}
by the continuous extension to quadruples of distinct points in the boundary
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
∎

Remark 2.4

(projective cross-ratio from Veronese embedding)
.

For
\mathbf{H}^{2}
, the formula (
13
) recovers the absolute value of the projective cross-ratio of four points in
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{2}
.

On the one had, given four lines
a^{-},a^{+},b^{-},b^{+}\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the absolute value of their projective cross-ratio can be obtained (as known to Euclid
[
24
, Book VI]
) by lifting them to vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and considering the square root of the cross-ratio of areas:

\sqrt{\left|\tfrac{\det(a^{-},a^{+})\det(b^{-},b^{+})}{\det(a^{-},b^{+})\det(b^{-},a^{+})}\right|}.

On the other hand, the space of real binary quadratic forms with the discriminant is a model for the Minkowski space
(\mathbb{R}^{1+2},\langle,\rangle)
.
The Veronese map sending a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
to the degenerate form
q_{v}\colon u\mapsto\det(u,v)^{2}
yields a quadratic function
\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbf{X}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{1+2}
sending the determinant
\det(v,v^{\prime})
to the square of the scalar product
\langle q_{v},q_{v^{\prime}}\rangle^{2}
.
This projectifies to an isomorphism of projective lines
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{X}_{0})\subset\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}^{1+2})
(see
[
70
, Lemma 3.1]
).

Lemma 2.5

(unicity of the cross-ratio)
.

If a continuous function
(\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})^{(4)}\to(0,+\infty)
is invariant under the diagonal action of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and satisfies the (
Invariance
) & (
Cocycle
) identities, then it is equal to a power of the cross-ratio, namely
\left[{\cdot}\right]^{t}
for some
t\in[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Any four points are contained in a copy of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}
.
In restriction to each copy of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{2}\subset\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}
one can mimic the proof of
[
33
, Lemma 3.2]
, using the simple transitivity for the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
on triples of distinct points in
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{1}
and the (
Invariance
) & (
Cocycle
).
The
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{3})
-invariance implies that the exponent
t
does not depend on the copy of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{2}\subset\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}
, since
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{3})
acts transitively on the triples of distinct points hence on circles
\partial\mathbf{H}^{2}\subset\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}
.
∎

Remark 2.6

(generalisation to abstract cross-ratios)
.

Proposition

4.6
will extend
Lemma

2.5
to abstract cross-ratios on a more general class of spaces.

We thus deduce Corollary
2.7
, confirming that our normalization for the cross-ratio coincides with the modulus of the projective cross-ratio.

Corollary 2.7

(projective cross-ratio)
.

In the upper-half-space models
\mathbf{H}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty)
and
\mathbf{H}^{3}\simeq\mathbb{C}\times(0,+\infty)
identifying
\partial\mathbf{H}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{RP}^{1}\simeq\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}\simeq\mathbb{CP}^{1}\simeq\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
, we find that the cross-ratio of
(a^{+},a^{-},b^{+},b^{-})
is the modulus of their projective cross-ratio:

\left[{a^{-},a^{+},b^{-},b^{+}}\right]=\left|\tfrac{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{+}-b^{-})}{(a^{+}-b^{-})(b^{+}-a^{-})}\right|.

(14)

Proof.

Remark
2.4
implies that the result holds for quadruples of distinct points contained in any conformal copy
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{1}
inside
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
.
Moreover the modulus of the projective cross-ratio on
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
satisfies the conditions in
Lemma

2.5
, so it is a power of the metric cross-ratio, which must be
1
as they agree on
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{1}
.
∎

Corollary 2.8

(recovering projective cross-ratio)
.

For quadruple of points
(a^{-},a^{+};b^{+},b^{-})
in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}=\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
, the projective cross-ratio
\tfrac{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{+}-b^{-})}{(a^{+}-b^{-})(b^{+}-a^{-})}
has argument
\pm\vartheta\bmod{2\pi}
given in terms of
X^{+}=\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]
and
X^{-}=\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{+},b^{-}}\right]
by

2\cos(\vartheta)=X^{+}(1+1/(X^{+})^{2}-1/(X^{-})^{2})
.

Consequently, a quadruple of distinct points
(a^{-},a^{+};b^{+},b^{-})
in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}
is uniquely determined up to
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{3})
by the cross-ratios
X^{+}=\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]
and
X^{-}=\left[{a^{-},a^{+};b^{+},b^{-}}\right]
.

Proof.

Let
Z^{+}=\tfrac{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{+}-b^{-})}{(a^{+}-b^{-})(b^{+}-a^{-})}
and
Z^{-}=\tfrac{(a^{+}-b^{-})(b^{+}-a^{-})}{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{+}-b^{-})}
so that
X^{+}=\lvert Z^{+}\rvert
and
X^{-}=\lvert Z^{-}\rvert
.
The inverses of these complex projective cross-ratios sum up to one:

\tfrac{1}{Z^{+}}+\tfrac{1}{Z^{-}}=\tfrac{(a^{+}-b^{-})(b^{+}-a^{-})}{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{+}-b^{-})}+\tfrac{(a^{+}-b^{+})(b^{-}-a^{-})}{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{-}-b^{+})}=1.

Moreover
\vartheta=\arg(Z^{+})
satisfies
\lvert Z^{+}-1\rvert^{2}=\lvert Z^{+}\rvert^{2}-2\cos(\vartheta)\lvert Z^{+}\rvert+1
, so after dividing by
\lvert Z^{+}\rvert
and rewriting
\lvert Z^{+}-1\rvert^{2}/\lvert Z^{+}\rvert=\lvert Z^{+}\rvert/\lvert Z^{-}\rvert^{2}
we have the result.

Recall that
\Isom^{+}(\mathbf{H}^{3})=\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{Z}/2
acts uniquely transitively on quadruple of distinct points in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}=\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
with a given cross-ratio up to complex conjugacy, that is with a given modulus and argument
\bmod{\pi}
.
∎

Corollary 2.9

(cocyclic points)
.

A quadruple of points
(a^{+},a^{-};b^{+},b^{-})
in (a copy of)
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}=\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
is cocyclic (belong to a copy of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{P}^{1}
) in this order if and only if we have:

\left[{a^{+},a^{-};b^{+},b^{-}}\right]^{-1}+\left[{a^{+},a^{-};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]^{-1}=1.

This identity characterizes when loxodromic elements in
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
have coplanar axes.

Proof.

For
a^{\pm},b^{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
, the inverses of the complex projective cross-ratios sum up to one:

\tfrac{(a^{+}-b^{-})(b^{+}-a^{-})}{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{+}-b^{-})}+\tfrac{(a^{+}-b^{+})(b^{-}-a^{-})}{(a^{+}-a^{-})(b^{-}-b^{+})}=1

so the sum of the moduli is greater than one with equality if and only if both complex projective cross ratios were real numbers in
[0,1]
.

The same computation (expressing cross ratios in terms of visual distances) that the four points on a Euclidean sphere satisfy Ptoleme’s inequality, with quality if and only if the four points are cocyclic.
(Note that any four points in Euclidean space lie on a common sphere.)
∎

Remark 2.10

(complex distance)
.

For distinct
a^{\pm},b^{\pm}\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}
, the geodesics
(a^{-},a^{+}),(b^{-},b^{+})\subset\mathbf{H}^{3}
are joined by a unique orthogeodesic arc of length
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
, along which they are screwed by an angle
\theta
: the complex number
\lambda+i\theta
is called the
complex distance
between them.
The isometry
C\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{3})
sending
a^{\pm}
to
b^{\pm}
which is a translation-rotation along their common orthogeodesic has
\tfrac{1}{2}\Tr(C)=\cosh\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda+i\theta)
.

Up to the action of
\Isom^{+}(\mathbf{H}^{3})=\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{3}=\partial\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
, we may fix
a^{\pm}=\pm 1
and find
b^{\pm}=\pm\exp(\lambda+i\theta)
so that
C\colon z\mapsto\exp(\lambda+i\theta)z
.

We may recover the complex distance from the cross-ratios since with the previous notations we have the projective cross-ratios
Z^{\pm}=\pm 4e^{\lambda+i\theta}/(1\pm e^{\lambda+i\theta})^{2}
so their absolute values
X^{\pm}
satisfy:

\tfrac{1}{\left[{a^{+},a^{-};b^{+},b^{-}}\right]}-\tfrac{1}{\left[{a^{+},a^{-};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]}=\tfrac{1}{X^{+}}-\tfrac{1}{X^{-}}=\tfrac{\lvert 1+\exp(\lambda+i\theta)\rvert^{2}-\lvert 1-\exp(\lambda+i\theta)\rvert^{2}}{4\exp(\lambda)}=\cos(\theta)

\tfrac{\left[{a^{+},a^{-};b^{+},b^{-}}\right]}{\left[{a^{+},a^{-};b^{-},b^{+}}\right]}=\tfrac{X^{+}}{X^{-}}=\tfrac{\lvert 1+\exp(\lambda+i\theta)\rvert^{2}}{\lvert 1-\exp(\lambda+i\theta)\rvert^{2}}=\left|\tanh\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda+i\theta)\right|^{2}.

Note that for loxodromic
\alpha,\beta\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{3})
with distinct fixed points,
Equation

11
gives:

\tfrac{X^{+}}{X^{-}}=\tfrac{\left[{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+};\beta^{+},\beta^{-}}\right]}{\left[{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+};\beta^{-},\beta^{+}}\right]}=\lim_{m,n\to+\infty}\exp\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})-\ell(\alpha^{m}\beta^{-n})\right).

Example 2.11

(Cremona group action)
.

Let
\Gamma=\operatorname{Bir}(\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{2})
be the group of birational functions of the complex projective plane (also known as the Cremona group), and consider the representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
constructed in
[
14
]
.

An element
g\in\Gamma
has a dynamical degree denoted
\lambda(g)
and by
[
13
, Remark 4.5]
it satisfies
\ell\rho(g)=\log\lambda(g)
.
One may compare this with our
Lemma

1.17
expressing translation lengths in terms of Buseman functions, and the relation between Buseman functions and dynamical derivatives
[
18
, §4.2.3]
.
By
Equation

10
, for
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
with positive dynamical degree and distinct fixed points, we have:

\log\left[{\rho(\alpha)^{-},\rho(\alpha)^{+};\rho(\beta)^{-},\rho(\beta)^{+}}\right]=\lim_{m,n\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\lambda(\alpha^{m})+\lambda(\beta^{n})-\lambda(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})\right).

2.4
Isometry groups of algebraic hyperbolic spaces

For algebraic hyperbolic spaces, the classification of non-trivial isometries
1.11
can be made more explicit (see
[
18
, Proposition 6.1.11]
).

Proposition 2.12

(classification of isometries)
.

If a non-trivial
\gamma\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is:

elliptic

then it is conjugate to an element in the group
\operatorname{O}(\kappa-1)
of isometries of
\mathbf{S}^{\kappa-1}
.

parabolic

then it is conjugate to an element in the group
\Isom(\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1})
of affine isometries of
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}
which has no fixed points.

loxodromic

then it is conjugate to an element in the group
\operatorname{O}(\kappa-2)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
of linear similarities of
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}
with non-trivial dilatation factor.

Consider a loxodromic
\gamma\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.
Since
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
, it follows from
Remark

1.15
that its stable translation length defined by formulae (
8
) is equal to its infimum translation length:

\ell(\gamma)=\inf\{\dH(o,\gamma(o))\colon o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\}.

The
translation axis
of
\gamma
is the set of points
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
such that
\dH(o,\gamma o)=\ell(\gamma)
: it is a hyperbolic geodesic joining its attractive and repulsive fixed points
\gamma^{+},\gamma^{-}\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, on which
\gamma
acts by translation of
\ell(\gamma)
.
Moreover, for all
\xi\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\setminus\{\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+}\}
we have
\ell(\gamma)=|\log\left[{\gamma\xi,\gamma^{-},\xi,\gamma^{+}}\right]|
.

Definition 2.13

(elementary)
.

A subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is
elementary
when it has invariant subset of cardinal
1
in
X\sqcup\partial X
or of cardinal
2
in
\partial X
.

Remark 2.14

(on terminology)
.

Beware that conflicting terminologies appear in the literature.

For example,
[
18
]
and
[
39
]
are not compatible, and we use a mix of them.
Their notions of elementarity agree, but ours is less restrictive (when there is a global fixed point at infinity but the limit set is infinite, these authors do not consider the action to be elementary, whereas we do).
Their notion of parabolicity is different, and we use the same as in
[
18
]
.

The elementary representations partition into the following classes:

elliptic
:

there is a global fixed point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, so
\Gamma
can be conjugated in
\Isom(\mathbf{S}_{o}^{\kappa-1})
.

parabolic
:

there is a global fixed point
\infty\in\partial\mathbf{H}
and its horospheres are all invariant, so
\Gamma
can be conjugated in
\Isom(\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1})
.

focal
:

there is a global fixed point
\infty\in\partial\mathbf{H}
but horospheres are not invariant, so
\Gamma
can be conjugated in
\Isom(\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1})\rtimes\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and is projection in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is non-trivial.

lineal
:

there is an invariant pair of points at infinity but no fixed point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, so
\Gamma
can be conjugated in
\Isom(\mathbf{S}_{o}^{\kappa-2})\rtimes\mathbb{R}^{*}
and its projection in
\mathbb{R}^{*}
is infinite.

Remark 2.15

(elementary and length function)
.

An elementary representation may have zero length function (in which case it is elliptic or parabolic) or non-zero length function (in which case it is focal or lineal).

Lemma 2.16

(Tits alternative)
.

A subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is non-elementary if and only if it contains loxodromic elements
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
whose fixed points
\alpha^{\pm},\beta^{\pm}
are all distinct.

In that case, there exists
p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
such that
a=\alpha^{p}
and
b=\beta^{p}
freely generate a rank-
2
free group, and the restricted length-function
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\ni(m,n)\mapsto\ell(a^{m}b^{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is proper.

Proof.

Since
\Gamma
is non-elementary then it has non-zero length function so it admits loxodromic elements.
If any two loxodromic elements in
\Gamma
have a common fixed point, then it would be lineal or focal, so we may find
\alpha,\beta
with distinct fixed points.
The second statement follows from (the proof) of
Lemma

1.23
.
∎

The following
Corollary

2.17
will serve in
Theorem

6.2
to separate the length functions associated to elementary and non-elementary representations of groups.

Corollary 2.17

(elementary from homomorphisms)
.

A subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is elementary if and only if its length function
\ell\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the absolute value of a homomorphism
\Xi\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

1.23
, but let us spell out the proof for clarity.

Assume
\Gamma
is elementary.
If
\Gamma
is elliptic, then we set
\Xi
to be the trivial homomorphism.
If
\Gamma
has a fixed point
\xi\in\partial X
, then we set
\Xi
to be the Busemann homomorphism
g\mapsto\Bus_{\xi}(gx,x)
for any
x\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
associated to
\xi
(see
[
10
, Chapter II.8]
for details about Busemann functions).
If
\Gamma
is lineal then it leaves invariant some geodesic line
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\subset X
and the translation parameter along this geodesic yields the desired homomorphism
\Xi
.
In all cases, we have
\ell(\gamma)=\lvert\Xi(\gamma)\rvert
.

Conversely, assume that
\Gamma
is non-elementary, then
\Gamma
contains two loxodromic elements
\alpha,\beta
with disjoint fixed point sets at infinity by
Lemma

2.16
so by
Lemma

1.23
there exists
p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
such that the restricted length-function
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\ni(m,n)\mapsto\ell(a^{pm}b^{pn})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is proper, so there cannot be a homomorphism
\Xi\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\ell(\gamma)=\lvert\Xi(\gamma)\rvert
.
∎

When a subgroup is non-elementary, its limit set
\Lambda(\Gamma)
is infinite and is the smallest closed
\Gamma
-invariant subset of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
(see
[
18
, §7.2-§7.3]
).

Lemma 2.18

(convex core)
.

Consider a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
which is non-neutral.

There is a smallest non-empty (closed) invariant convex subspace of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
: that is the convex hull of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
.
We call it the
convex core
, and denote it
\operatorname{Core}(\Gamma)
.

There is a smallest non-empty (closed) totally geodesic invariant subspace: that is the span of its limit set
\Span(\Lambda(\Gamma))\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.

Proof.

If the representation is not elementary, this is
[
18
, §7.2-7.3]
).

If the representation is not neutral, then there is an loxodromic element and thus the limit has at least two points. Any convex subset
C
that is invariant contains
\Lambda(\Gamma)
in its boundary because it does not depend on the chosen base point.
By the continuity of geodesics with respect to their endpoints (
[
18
, Proposition 4.4.4]
), the convex hull
C
contains all geodesics between points in
\Lambda(\Gamma)
.
In particular, the intersection of all invariant closed convex subsets is non-empty and thus this is the unique minimal invariant closed convex subset.

The same argument gives the same result for invariant totally geodesic subspaces.
∎

Recall that
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})=\PO(1,\kappa)
, and an isometric action of a group
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
may be lifted to a linear action on
\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
preserving
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
(or the upper half of
\mathbf{X}_{+1}
).

Definition 2.19

(minimal and irreducible)
.

A representation
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is
minimal
when its action on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
admits no proper closed invariant totally geodesic subspace.

A representation
\Gamma\to\PO(1,\kappa)
is
irreducible
when its corresponding linear action on
\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
does not preserve a non-trivial closed linear subspace.

Remark 2.20

(irreducible = minimal and non-elementary)
.

It follows from the definitions that a representation is irreducible if and only if it is minimal without fixed point at infinity.
Indeed in
\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
, a non-trivial closed linear subspace admits a non-trivial closed orthogonal complement: at least one of these intersects the isotropic cone or the unit hyperboloid
\mathbf{X}_{0}\cup\mathbf{X}_{+1}
, which projectifies to
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\cup\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.

3
From kernels to isometric embeddings

In this section we will consider topological spaces
X,Y
and topological groups
\Gamma,G
acting by homeomorphisms on these spaces, but we will mostly focus on sets and groups with the discrete topology.

Definition 3.1

(kernel)
.

On a topological space
X
, a
kernel
is a continuous function
K\colon X^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
that is symmetric (
\forall x,y\in X\colon K(x,y)=K(y,x)
).
We say that it has diagonal
s\in\mathbb{R}
when
\forall z\in X\colon K(z,z)=s
. We say that it is non-negative when
\forall x,y\in X\colon K(x,y)\geq 0
.

We recall here some topological background concerning the topologies that one may endow the spaces of functions over
X
and the space of representations
\Gamma\to\Isom(X)
.

Definition 3.2

(pointwise topology)
.

For a topological space
X
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of continuous functions
X^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
is endowed with the pointwise convergence topology inherited by the product topology when viewed as a subset of
\prod_{X^{p}}\mathbb{R}
.

It is a locally convex topological vector space.
Its compact convex sets correspond to the products of compact intervals, of the form
\prod_{x\in X^{p}}[min_{x},max_{x}]
.

Lemma 3.3

(closed subspaces)
.

On a space
X
, the subset of continuous functions
K\colon X^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
cut out by any collection of non-strict equalities between continuous functions of the values of
K
at finite number of points at a time is closed.

More precisely, if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have a set
L_{n}
(which may have any cardinal) indexing continuous functions
D^{n}_{l}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n^{p}}\to\mathbb{R}
, then the following formula defines a closed subspace:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\;\forall l\in L_{n},\;\forall x\in X^{n^{p}}\colon D^{n}_{l}(K(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{p}}))\geq 0.

Example 3.4

(group as set)
.

On a topological group
\Gamma
, the subset of functions
K\colon\Gamma^{1+p}\to\mathbb{R}
which are left invariant is closed and identifies with the space of all functions
F\colon\Gamma^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
by
K(\gamma_{0},\dots,\gamma_{n})=F(\gamma_{0}^{-1}\gamma_{1},\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n-1}^{-1}\gamma_{n})
and
F(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=K(1,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{1}\dots\gamma_{n})
.

3.1
Kernels of positive type and of conditionally negative type

We recall some facts about kernels of positive type and of conditionally negative type (see for example
[
2
, Chapter 3]
with slightly different terminology) , since we wish to emphasize some results about linear independence that we will need in the next sections.

Definition 3.5

(positive and conditionally negative type)
.

A kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}

has
positive type

when for all
x\in X^{n}
we have:

\textstyle\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\colon\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K(x_{i},x_{j})\geq 0

(15)

and
strictly positive type
when such inequalities are strict for distinct
x_{k}
.

has
conditionally negative type

when it has diagonal
0
, and for all
x\in X^{n}
we have:

\textstyle\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\colon\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}=0\implies\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K(x_{i},x_{j})\leq 0.

(16)

and
strictly conditionally negative type
when such inequalities are strict for distinct
x_{k}
.

For a cardinal
\kappa
we denote the Hilbert space of that dimension by
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
.
Recall that a subset
Y
of a Hilbert is
total
when there is no closed linear subspace containing
Y
and it is
affinely total
when there is no closed affine subspace containing
Y
.

Theorem 3.6

(linear and affine GNS constructions)
.

Consider a kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
.

If
K
has positive type then there is a cardinal
\kappa
and a continuous
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
with total image such that
\forall x,y\in X\colon K(x,y)=\langle f(x),f(y)\rangle
.
Moreover it is unique: for any two such
f_{i}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{i}}
, there is a linear isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{2}}
such that
f_{2}=\varphi\circ f_{1}
.

If
K
has conditionally negative type then there is a cardinal
\kappa
and a continuous
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
with affinely total image such that
\forall x,y\in X\colon K(x,y)=\|f(x)-f(y)\|^{2}
.
Moreover it is unique: for any two such
f_{i}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{i}}
, there is
an affine isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{2}}
such that
f_{2}=\varphi\circ f_{1}
.

Proof.

For both (positive and conditionally negative) types, the existence and unicity of
f
are explained constructively in
[
2
, Chapter 3]
.
They also follow from the Cayley-Merger theorem and the work of Blumenthal
[
4
, §42]
, as we will explain in more detail for kernels of hyperbolic type in the proof of
Proposition

3.13
.
∎

Remark 3.7

(separation means injectivity)
.

We say that the kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
of positive or conditionally negative type
separates points
(or is
separating
) when, for all distinct
x,y\in X
we have:

\langle\ ,\ \rangle
:

when
K
is of positive type that
K(x,y)<\max\{K(x,x),K(y,y)\}

\|\ \|^{2}
:

when
K
is of conditionally negative type that
K(x,y)\neq 0
.

The fact that
K
separates points is equivalent to the injectivity of the functions
f
in
Theorem

3.6
. The strictness of
K
implies that
K
separates points.

The strict inequalities respectively characterize linear and affine independence of the images under the associated map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
to a Hilbert space.

Lemma 3.8

(strict means independence)
.

Let
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
be a kernel.

If
K
has strictly positive type, then for any continuous
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
such that
K(x,y)=\langle\varphi(x),\varphi(y)\rangle
, the images of points in
X
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
, in particular
f
is an embedding.

If
K
has strictly conditionally negative type, then for any continuous
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
such that
K(x,y)=\|f(x)-f(y)\|^{2}
, the images of points in
X
are affinely independent in
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
, in particular
f
is an embedding.

Proof.

Consider a map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
such that for all
x,y\in X
we have either
K(x,y)=\langle f(x),f(y)\rangle
or
K(x,y)=\|f(x)-f(y)\|^{2}
depending on the type.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in X
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
.

We have
\left\|\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}f(x_{i})\right\|^{2}=\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\langle f(x_{i}),f(x_{j})\rangle
, which must be
>0
when
K
is strictly of positive type, hence
f(X)
is linearly independent and
f
is injective.

Assuming
\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}=0
we have
\|\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}f(x_{i})\|^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\|f(x_{i})-f(x_{j})\|^{2}
, which must be
<0
when
K
is strictly of conditionally negative type, hence
f(X)
is affinely independent and
f
is injective.
∎

The following will serve to prove
Proposition

3.30
.

Lemma 3.9

(power series of kernels of positive type are strict)
.

Consider a separating kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
of positive type such that
\forall x\in X\colon K(x,x)<1
and injective associated map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
.

For a power series
Q(z)=\sum_{m\geq 1}q_{m}z^{m}
with coefficients
q_{m}\geq 0
and convergence radius
1
, the kernel
Q\circ K
is strictly of positive type.

Proof.

Let
x\in X^{n}
and denote
K_{i,j}=K(x_{i},x_{j})
.
Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}Q(K_{i,j})=0
, namely
\sum_{m}q_{m}\left(\sum_{ij}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K^{m}_{i,j}\right)=0
.

By Schur theorem
[
2
, Chapter 3, Theorem 1.12]
, for all
m\geq 1
the kernel
K^{m}
is of positive type, implying that
\left(\sum_{ij}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K^{m}_{i,j}\right)\geq 0
.
Since the convergence radius of
Q
is
1
the inequality
q_{m}\geq 0
is strict for infinitely many
m
.
Thus for such
m\geq 1
we have

\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K^{m}_{i,j}=0
.

Since the kernel
K
is of positive type, it satisfies the Cauchy-Schwartz inequalities, in particular
\forall i,j\colon\lvert K_{i,j}\rvert^{2}\leq K_{i,i}K_{j,j}
. Hence for
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
, by
Remark

3.7
, we have
\lvert K_{i,j}\rvert<\mu:=\max\left\{K_{i,i}\right\}
.

We deduce from all the above that for infinitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\left(K_{i,j}/\mu\right)^{m}=0
and taking the limit on that diverging subsequence of
m
to
+\infty
reveals

\sum_{K_{i,i}=\mu}\lambda_{i}^{2}=0
,
namely
K_{i,i}=\mu\implies\lambda_{i}=0
.
Repeating the argument with
\max\{K_{j,j}\colon K_{j,j}<\mu\}
eventually leads to showing that all
\lambda_{i}=0
.
This proves that
Q\circ K
is strictly of positive type.
∎

The following will serve to prove
Proposition

4.19

Lemma 3.10

(powers of kernels of conditionally negative type are strict)
.

Consider a kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
of conditionally negative type vanishing only on the diagonal.

For all
t\in(0,1)
the kernel
K^{t}
has strictly conditionally negative type.

Proof.

Fix
t\in(0,1)
.
Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be distinct points in
X
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
with
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=0
.
Assume by contradiction that
\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K(x_{i},x_{j})^{t}=0
.
By the inverse Laplace transform, we have for any
k\geq 0
that

k^{t}=\frac{t}{\Upgamma(1-t)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}(1-e^{-uk})\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u^{t+1}}

(17)

Applying this integral representation to the values of
K^{t}
in the finite sum
0=\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K^{t}(x_{i},x_{j})
and using
\sum_{ij}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}=(\sum_{k}\lambda_{k})^{2}=0
we find
0=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left[\sum_{ij}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\exp(-uK(x_{i},x_{j}))\right]\tfrac{du}{u^{1+t}}
.
By Shoenberg’s theorem
[
2
, Chapter 3, Theorem 2.2]
,
\forall u\in(0,+\infty)
the kernel
\exp(-uK)
is of positive type, so the integrand
\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\exp(-uK(x_{i},x_{j}))
is
\geq 0
, hence for almost all
u\in(0,\infty)
it is
=0
. By continuity it vanishes for all
u\in(0,\infty)
.
Taking the limit as
u\to+\infty
reveals
\sum_{K(x_{i},x_{j})\neq 0}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}=0
.
Since
K
only vanishes on the diagonal, this says
\sum_{i\neq j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}=0
.
Substracting this from
0=(\sum_{k}\lambda_{k})^{2}
yields
\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}^{2}=0
hence
\lambda=0
which is a contradiction.
Thus
\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K^{t}(x_{i},x_{j})\neq 0
proving that
K^{t}
is strictly of conditionally negative type.
∎

3.2
Kernels of hyperbolic type and embeddings in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}

We recall the definition of kernels and functions of hyperbolic type and the main results about them, most of which can be found in
[
47
]
.

Definition 3.11

(kernel of hyperbolic type)
.

A kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
is of
hyperbolic type
when it is nonnegative with diagonal
1
, and for any
x_{0}\in X
the
visual kernel

K_{0}\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by
K_{0}\colon(x_{i},x_{j})\mapsto K(x_{0},x_{i})K(x_{0},x_{j})-K(x_{i},x_{j})
is positive type, namely we have the following
hyperbolic Cauchy-Schwartz inequalities
:

\textstyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\;\forall x\in X^{n},\;\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\quad\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K(x_{i},x_{j})\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}K(x_{0},x_{k})\right)^{2}.

(HCS)

(In particular with
n=1
, a kernel of hyperbolic type must take values in
[1,\infty)
.)

Proof.

The condition (
HCS
) rewrites as
\forall\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\colon\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}K_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})\geq 0
, meaning that
K_{0}
yields a nonnegative quadratic form on the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space with basis
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.
∎

Let us relate this to the equivalent characterization in terms of Gram-matrix determinants (see
[
64
, 7.2.4]
) also known as Cayley-Merger determinants introduced by Blumenthal (see
[
4
, §42, §46]
).

Proposition 3.12

(Cayley-Menger determinant)
.

A kernel
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
with diagonal
1
is of hyperbolic type if and only if all its
hyperbolic Cayley Menger determinants
are non-positive:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\;\forall x\in X^{1+n}\colon\quad\det\left(-K(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{0\leq i,j\leq n}\leq 0

(HCM)

Proof.

Recall Sylvester’s law expressing the signature of a real quadratic form in terms of the signs of its diagonal-minors: for any
x_{0}\in X
, the visual kernel
K_{0}
yields a nonnegative quadratic form on the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space with basis
X
if and only if
\forall x\in X^{n}\colon\det\left(K_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\geq 0
.

Besides
\forall x\in X^{1+n}
the determinant of
\left(-K(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{0\leq i,j\leq k}
can be rewritten (by subtracting to each column
j\geq 0
the column
0
times
K(x_{0},x_{j})
and expanding with respect to the line
0
) to equal minus the determinant of
\left(K_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
.
Hence (
HCS
) and (
HCM
) are equivalent.
∎

The notion of kernel of hyperbolic type is for algebraic Minkowski spaces analogous to the notion of kernel of positive type for Hilbert spaces. Its interest lies in
[
47
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Proposition 3.13

(hyperbolic GNS)
.

For a subset
X\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, the function
K(x,y)=\cosh\dH(x,y)
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.

Conversely, for every kernel of hyperbolic type
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
, there is a cardinal
\kappa
and a continuous function
f\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
such that
K(x,y)=\cosh\dH(f(x),f(y))
.
Moreover for any two such
f_{i}\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
with total images, there is an isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
such that
f_{2}=\varphi\circ f_{1}
.

Additionally when
\kappa
is countable, it is the supremum of
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exists a non-vanishing hyperbolic Cayley-Menger determinant of size
1+n
.

Proof.

We sketch proofs to explain some ideas and relate them to past works on the topic.

Let us show that the kernel
(\cosh\circ\dH)
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
satisfies
Definition

3.11
.
It is symmetric and normalized to
1
on the diagonal, and we now show the Hyperbolic Cauchy-Schwartz inequalities.
It lifts in
\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
to the Minkowski form
\langle,\rangle
on the upper-half sheet of the unit hyperboloid
\mathbf{X}_{+1}
. The Minkowski form also restricts to a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form that is negative definite on the tangent space to
\mathbf{X}_{+1}
at
x_{0}
.
The orthogonal projections of
x_{i}\in\mathbf{X}_{+1}
into that tangent space are
v_{i}=x_{i}-\langle x_{0},x_{i},\rangle x_{0}
so the sum
v=\sum\lambda_{i}v_{i}
has Minkowski norm
\langle v,v\rangle\geq 0
.
This implies that the visual kernel
K_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})=\langle x_{0},x_{i}\rangle\langle x_{0},x_{j}\rangle-\langle x_{i},x_{j}\rangle
is non-negative.

Conversely, if a space
X
with a nonnegative kernel
d
normalized to
0
on the diagonal is such that the kernel
K=\cosh\circ d
has non-positive Hyperbolic Cayley-Menger determinants, then it embeds isometrically in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
One may refer to
[
4
, §42]
for a thorough analysis of the Euclidean analogue, and to
[
4
, §106]
for its extension to the hyperbolic case.
Moreover we may characterize when the image lies on some finite dimensional subspace of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and its dimension is the minimal
n
such that all hyperbolic Cayley-Menger determinants with more than
n+1
points vanish. Indeed for any
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathbf{H}^{n}
, the determinant of
\left(-\cosh\dH(x_{i},x_{j})\right)
vanishes if and only if
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}
are included in a proper totally geodesic subspace
[
4
, Theorem 106.1]
and
(X,d)
embeds in
\mathbf{H}^{n}
if and only if it is true for any of its
(n+3)
-tuple
[
4
, Theorem 111.2]
.
∎

Remark 3.14

(from scalar product to chord-length-square)
.

Consider a kernel
C\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
with diagonal
1
.
One may define a related kernel given by the
chord length-squared
, defined by
S=(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\dH)^{2}=(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}(C))^{2}
.
It is shown in
[
31
, Theorem 3]
that
\sign\det\left(S(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{0\leq i,j\leq n}=-\sign\det\left(C(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
.
Thus, one may also rephrase the condition
Equation

HCM
characterizing the fact that
X
embeds into some
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
by saying that
\forall x\in X^{1+n}

\det\left(-S(x_{i},x_{j})\right)\geq 0
.

By
[
31
, Corollary 4]
, these sign conditions on the
4\times 4
determinants on
S
recover the Ptolemaic inequality for hyperbolic spaces, which we
will use in
Section

4.2
:

\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{13}\right)\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{24}\right)\leq\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{12}\right)\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{34}\right)+\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{14}\right)\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{23}\right)

(18)

Hence the sign conditions for the determinants of the chord lengths squared matrices of larger sizes can correspond to generalizations of this Ptolemaic inequality.

Definition 3.15

(strictly of hyperbolic type)
.

We say that a kernel of hyperbolic type
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
is
strictly of hyperbolic type
when for any
x_{0}\in X
, the visual kernel
K_{0}\colon(x,y)\mapsto K(x,x_{0})K(x_{0},y)-K(x,y)
is strictly of positive type.

This strictness condition is again an independence property as explained in the following
Proposition

3.16
.

This independence property appears for the embeddings associated to real trees in
[
18
, Theorem 13.1.1(iv)]
and is stated in
[
16
, Remark 4.3]
for the exotic deformations of hyperbolic spaces
\mathbf{H}^{n}
that we will see in
Section

3.4
.

Proposition 3.16

(strict means independence)
.

A kernel of hyperbolic type
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
is strictly of hyperbolic type if and only if every function
f\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
satisfying
K(x,y)=\cosh(\dH(f(x),f(y))
has the property that for every finite
F\subset X\colon\dim\Span f(F)=\Card(F)
.

Proof.

Let
F
be a finite subset and fix
x_{0}\in F
. The kernel
K_{0}\colon(x,y)\mapsto K(x,x_{0})K(x_{0},y)-K(x,y)
is strictly of positive type. So, by the first part of
Lemma

3.8
, the image of
F
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa}
is linearly independent which proves the result.
∎

Definition 3.17

(spaces of kernels of hyperbolic type)
.

On a space
X
, we denote by the
\mathcal{K}(X)
set of kernels of hyperbolic type endowed with the pointwise convergence topology.

It is a closed subspace of
\prod_{X^{2}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
by
Lemma

3.3
.

For
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
, we denote by
\dim(K)\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
the dimension
\kappa
of the hyperbolic space given by
Proposition

3.13
.
When
X
is finite any kernel
K
has
\dim(K)\leq\Card(X)-1
.

Proposition 3.18

(dimension is lower semi-continuous)
.

The function
K\mapsto\dim(K)
is lower semi-continuous.

Proof.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}
, a kernel of hyperbolic type
K_{0}
has
\dim(K_{0})\geq l
if and only if there exists
x_{0},\dots,x_{l}
such that the Cayley-Menger determinant
\det(-K_{0}(x_{i},x_{j}))_{0\leq i,j\leq l}\neq 0
.
Hence the set of kernels of dimension
\geq l
is
\cup_{x\in X^{l}}\{K\in\mathcal{K}(X)\colon\det(-K(x_{i},x_{j}))_{0\leq i,j\leq l}\neq 0\}
, which is a union of open sets. This gives the lower semi-continuity of the dimension.
∎

3.3
Functions of hyperbolic type and actions on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}

Definition 3.19

(function of hyperbolic type)
.

On a topological group
\Gamma
, a function
F\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
is of
hyperbolic type
when the kernel
\Gamma\times\Gamma\ni(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto F(\alpha^{-1}\beta)\in\mathbb{R}
is of hyperbolic type.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
the space of functions of hyperbolic type on
\Gamma
endowed with the pointwise convergence topology.
It is a closed subspace of
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1})^{\Gamma}
by
Lemma

3.3
.

Example 3.20
.

Given a continuous representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
, the choice of a point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
yields a function of hyperbolic type via the formula
F(g)=\cosh\dH(o,\rho(\gamma)o)
.

Another choice
o^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
yields another function of hyperbolic type
F^{\prime}
and several applications of the triangle inequality for
d
show that
\lvert\cosh^{-1}F-\cosh^{-1}F^{\prime}\rvert\leq 2\dH(o,o^{\prime})
.

The following summarizes
[
47
, Theorem 3.4 and Corollary 3.5]
.

Proposition 3.21

(representing functions of hyperbolic type)
.

On a group
\Gamma
, for every function of hyperbolic type
F\colon\Gamma\times\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
, there is a hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, a point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that
F(\gamma)=\cosh\dH(o,\rho(\gamma)o)
and the orbit of
o
is total.

If
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and
\rho^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}})
are two such representations, then there is a unique isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}}
satisfying
\varphi(\rho(\gamma)x)=\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)\varphi(x)
for all
x\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

The second statement in
Proposition

3.21
implies that one may attach
F\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
any object or attribute associated to the corresponding class of actions of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.

Definition 3.22

(length function)
.

To a function of hyperbolic type
F\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
on a group, we associate a translation
length function

\ell_{F}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by choosing any associated irreducible representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and defining
\ell_{F}(\gamma)=\ell(\rho(\gamma))
.

We say that
F
is
neutral
when
\ell_{F}=0
and we denote
\mathcal{F}_{nn}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
the open subspace of non-neutral functions of hyperbolic type.

The following formula is from
[
47
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Lemma 3.23

(
F
recovers
\ell
)
.

For
F\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
, its translation length function
\ell_{F}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is given for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
by:

\ell_{F}(\gamma)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{n}\cosh^{-1}\left(F(\gamma^{n})\right).

Thus, if functions of hyperbolic type
F_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
satisfy
\|\cosh^{-1}(F_{1})-\cosh^{-1}(F_{2})\|<\infty
or equivalently
\|\log(F_{1}/F_{2})\|<\infty
, then their length functions
\ell_{F_{i}}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
are equal
\ell_{F_{1}}=\ell_{F_{2}}
.

Remark 3.24

(converse to Proposition
3.21
)
.

Proposition
4.23
will imply that
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{nn}(\Gamma)
satisfying
\|\log(F_{1}/F_{2})\|<\infty
(equivalently
\|\cosh^{-1}(F_{1})-\cosh^{-1}(F_{2})\|<\infty
)
must arise from a same representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.

Corollary 3.25

(neutral and elementary)
.

For two functions of hyperbolic type
F_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
such that satisfy
\|\cosh^{-1}(F_{1})-\cosh^{-1}(F_{2})\|<\infty
or equivalently
\|\log(F_{1}/F_{2})\|<\infty
, we have:

Neutral:

F_{1}
is neutral if and only if
F_{2}
is neutral.

Elementary:

F_{1}
is elementary if and only if
F_{2}
is elementary.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.23
, we know that
\ell_{F_{1}}=\ell_{F_{2}}
.
The result follows since
\ell_{F}
determines the neutrality property (by definition), and the elementary property (by Lemmata
2.16
&
1.23
).
∎

Here is another consequence of Lemma
3.23
, which is very close to
[
54
, Theorem 1.9]
.

Lemma 3.26

(continuity of the length spectrum)
.

The function
F\mapsto\ell_{F}
is continuous for the pointwise convergence topology on the real vector space of functions
\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

For fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the function
F\mapsto\tfrac{1}{n}\cosh^{-1}\left(F(\gamma^{n})\right)
is continuous.
We show that the limit is both upper and lower semi-continuous.

On the one hand, by sub-additivity of
n\mapsto\cosh^{-1}\left(F(\gamma^{n})\right)
, the limit is actually an infimum, hence
F\mapsto\ell_{F}(\gamma)
is upper semi-continuous.
On the other hand, applying
[
54
, Theorem 1.1]
to the action of
\Gamma
on the Gromov-
\delta
-hyperbolic space
X
shows that for every
x\in X
and
\gamma\in\Isom(X)
we have

d(\gamma^{2}x,x)\leq d(\gamma x,x)+\ell(\gamma)+2\delta
.
Thus

\ell_{F}(\gamma)\geq\sup\{\tfrac{1}{n}\left(\cosh^{-1}(F(\gamma^{2n}))-\cosh^{-1}\left(F(\gamma^{n})\right)-2\delta\right)\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}

and the reverse inequality follows from the fact that
\ell(\gamma)
is actually the limit of the right-hand side, which proves lower semi-continuity.
∎

Lemma 3.27

(
F
recovers classification)
.

Consider a function of hyperbolic type
F\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
with translation length function
\ell_{F}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

For a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
realizing
F
, a subgroup
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
is such that:

\rho(\Gamma_{0})
is elliptic

if and only if
F(\Gamma_{0})
is bounded

\rho(\Gamma_{0})
is parabolic

if and only if
F(\Gamma_{0})
is unbounded but
\ell_{F}(\Gamma_{0})
is bounded

\rho(\Gamma_{0})
is hyperbolic

if and only if
\ell_{F}(\Gamma_{0})
is unbounded

This applies in particular to the full group
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
or a cyclic subgroup
\Gamma_{0}=\{\gamma^{n}\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

Proof.

The first and third points are clear having in mind that
\ell_{F}(\Gamma_{0})
is unbounded is equivalent to
\ell_{F}(\Gamma_{0})=\{0\}
. For the second point, this follows from the partitioning of elementary representations described in
Remark

2.14
.
∎

3.4
The exotic deformation from powers of kernels

We first recall
[
47
, Theorem I
bis
]
.

Theorem 3.28

(exotic deformation)
.

For
t\in(0,1]
, the function
(\cosh\circ\dH)^{t}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\times\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\mathbb{R}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.
This defines the continuous
exotic deformation

\exoH_{t}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega+\kappa}
, hence the associated (conjugacy class of)
exotic representation
(s)
\chi_{t}\colon\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega+\kappa})
.

We still denote
\exoH_{t}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\sqcup\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\mathbf{H}^{\infty+\kappa}\sqcup\partial\mathbf{H}^{\infty+\kappa}
its extension to the boundary.

This leads to the following
[
47
, Theorem 3.10]
.

Corollary 3.29

(powers of kernels and functions of hyperbolic type)
.

If
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type, then for all
0\leq t\leq 1
,
K^{t}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.

Similarly, if
F\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
is a function of hyperbolic type on a group, then for all
0\leq t\leq 1
,
F^{t}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.
Moreover, the following properties hold:

1.

When
t\neq 0
we have that
F^{t}
is non-elementary if and only if
F
is so.

2.

The translation length functions associated with the
F^{t}
are homothetic:
\ell_{t}=t\ell_{1}
.

Proof.

The statement about elementarity follows from the fact that the equivariant map
\exoH_{t}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\omega}\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
is a
(t,C)
-quasi-isometry, hence induces an
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
-equivariant map
\exoH_{t}\colon\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
The statement about proportionality of length functions is proved in
[
47
, § 4.2]
.
∎

The following proposition is a strengthening of the previous relying on the proof of
[
48
, Theorem 1.4]
where powers of kernels of hyperbolic type are proved directly to be of hyperbolic type.

Proposition 3.30

(strict powers are strict)
.

Let
K
be a kernel of hyperbolic type on
X
that separates points. For every
t\in(0,1)
, the power
K^{t}
is strictly of hyperbolic type.

Proof.

We use the notations in the proof of
[
48
, Theorem 1.12]
for
K=\beta
and
Q=q
.
Monod proves that
\beta^{t}
is of hyperbolic type by proving an inequality.
We do not repeat the proof and only explain how to get a strict inequality using his notations (with kernels
N,M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}
).
The goal is to prove that
N
is strictly of positive type which is equivalent to
M^{\prime\prime}=q(M^{\prime})
is strictly of positive type. This is the case since
M^{\prime}
is a separating kernel of positive type and
Q
satisfies the hypotheses of
Lemma

3.9
.
∎

Taking powers of functions of hyperbolic type affects the algebraic cross-ratio accordingly.

Lemma 3.31

(powers of cross-ratio)
.

For
t\in(0,1]
, and for any
x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}\in\partial^{4}{\mathbf{H}^{\omega}}
,

\left[{\exoH_{t}(x^{-}),\exoH_{t}(x^{+}),\exoH_{t}(y^{-}),\exoH_{t}(y^{+})}\right]=\left[{x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}}\right]^{t}.

Proof.

Recall Equations (

=

[
⋅
]

0

) and (

=

[
⋅
]

∞

‣
1.2
) characterizing when the cross-ratio equals
0
and
\infty
.
If
\left[{x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}}\right]=0
then
x^{-}=x^{+}
or
y^{-}=y^{+}
so
\exoH_{t}(x^{-})=\exoH_{t}(x^{+})
or
\exoH_{t}(y^{-})=\exoH_{t}(y^{+})
hence
\left[{\exoH_{t}(x^{-}),\exoH_{t}(x^{+}),\exoH_{t}(y^{-}),\exoH_{t}(y^{+})}\right]=0
as desired.
If
\left[{x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}}\right]=\infty
then
x^{-}=y^{+}
or
y^{-}=x^{+}
so
\exoH_{t}(x^{-})=\exoH_{t}(y^{+})
or
\exoH_{t}(y^{-})=\exoH_{t}(x^{+})
hence
\left[{\exoH_{t}(x^{-}),\exoH_{t}(x^{+}),\exoH_{t}(y^{-}),\exoH_{t}(y^{+})}\right]=\infty
as desired.

Now, assume that
\left[{x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}}\right]\in(0,+\infty)
. There exists a hyperbolic isometry
\gamma
with repulsive point
x^{+}
, attractive point
y^{+}
and such that
\gamma(y^{-})=y^{+}
.
Since
\exoH_{t}
is
\chi_{t}
-equivariant, the isometry
\chi_{t}(\gamma)
is a hyperbolic translation with axis
(\chi_{t}(\gamma)^{-},\chi_{t}(\gamma)^{+})=(\exoH_{t}(x^{+}),\exoH_{t}(y^{+}))
such that
\chi_{t}(\gamma)(\exoH_{t}(y^{-}))=\exoH_{t}(x^{-})
.
By construction,
\chi_{t}
has translation length
\ell(\chi_{t}(\gamma))=t\ell(\gamma)
.
Thus, by Equation (
9
):
\left[{\exoH_{t}(x^{-}),\exoH_{t}(x^{+}),\exoH_{t}(y^{-}),\exoH_{t}(y^{+})}\right]=\left[{\chi_{t}(\gamma)\exoH_{t}(y^{-}),\chi_{t}(\gamma)^{-},\exoH_{t}(y^{-}),\chi_{t}(\gamma)^{+}}\right]
is equal to
\exp\left(-\ell(\chi_{t}(\gamma))\right)=\exp\left(-t\ell(\gamma)\right)
hence to
\left[{x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}}\right]^{t}
.
∎

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
(X,d)
be a strongly hyperbolic space and
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(x,y)\mapsto\exp(td(x,y))
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.
Let
f_{t}\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
be a map such that
\cosh(\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y))=\exp(td(x,y))
and
\chi_{t}\colon\Isom(X)\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
be the associated representation.

For all
\gamma\in\Isom(X)

we have
\ell(\chi_{t}(\gamma))=t\ell(\gamma)
.

For all
x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}\in(\partial X)^{(4)}
we have

\left[{f_{t}(x^{-}),f_{t}(x^{+}),f_{t}(y^{-}),f_{t}(y^{+})}\right]=\left[{x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}}\right]^{t}
.

Proof.

By taking the logarithm of the relation
\cosh(\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y))=\exp(td(x,y))
, we find that

\lvert\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y))-td(x,y)\rvert\leq\ln(2)
so as
d(x,y)\to\infty
we have
\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y))-td(x,y)\to\ln(2)
so the results follow by definition of the translation length and continuity of the cross-ratio.
∎

Example 3.33
.

On the algebraic hyperbolic space
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa},\dH)
, if
t\in(0,1]
then the function
\exp(t\dH)
is a kernel of hyperbolic type (
[
48
, Theorem 8.5]
).

On a real tree
(T,d)
as in
Definition

1.4
, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
the function
\exp(td)
is a kernel of hyperbolic type (
[
48
, Proposition 1.5]
).

4
Abstract and algebraic cross-ratios: rigidity properties

In this section we consider a topological space
Y
(often a set with the discrete topology).
Let us recall that by
Y^{(4)}\subset Y^{4}
we mean the space of quadruples of points of which at least three are distinct.

4.1
Strongly hyperbolic spaces with unique cross-ratio up to powers

The goal of this subsection is to show that for a strongly hyperbolic space
(X,d)
, the cross-ratio defined by
d
from
Equation

5
and its powers are the unique continuous functions
(\partial X)^{(4)}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the usual identities ((
Invariance
), (
Inversion
), (
Cocycle
), (

=

[
⋅
]

0

), (

=

[
⋅
]

∞

‣
1.2
)) and that is invariant under any subgroup
\Gamma\leq\Isom(X)
acting sufficiently transitively on
\partial X
.

This generalizes the similar statement for algebraic hyperbolic spaces in
Lemma

2.5
.

Definition 4.1

(abstract cross-ratio)
.

On a topological space
Y
, an
abstract cross-ratio
is a continuous function
B\colon Y^{(4)}\to[0,\infty]
satisfying the (
Invariance
), (
Inversion
) and (
Cocycle
) relations as well as the characterizations (

=

[
⋅
]

0

) and (

=

[
⋅
]

∞

‣
1.2
) of the fibers above
0
and
\infty
.

We say that an abstract cross-ratio to
generate the topology
when for very triple of distinct points
x,y,z\in Y
, the collection of subsets
\{w\in Y\colon B(w,x,y,z)<\varepsilon\}
for
\varepsilon>0
is a basis of neighborhoods of
x
.

Remark 4.2

(
B(y,x,y,z)=1=B(x,y,z,y)
)
.

For an abstract cross-ratio
B
on
Y
, if
(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\in Y^{(4)}
satisfies
x=y
or
x^{\prime}=y^{\prime}
then by
Equation

Inversion
we have
B(x,x^{\prime}y,y^{\prime})=1
.
However the converse may fail drastically, as it is the case for the usual cross-ratio on
\mathbf{H}^{3}
.

Remark 4.3

(compact metrizable)
.

If
Y
is a compact metrizable space, then any abstract cross-ratio necessarily generates the topology. Indeed, if for fixed distinct
x,y,z\in Y
and a sequence
(w_{n})\in Y{N}
we have
B(w_{n},x,y,z)\to 0
, then
(w_{n})
has an extraction converging to some
x^{\prime}
which satisfies
B(x^{\prime},x,y,z)=0
and as
y\neq z
, we deduce from
Equation

=

[
⋅
]

0

that
x=x^{\prime}
.

Example 4.4

(abstract cross-ratios from pairings)
.

If a continuous function
\It\colon Y\times Y\to\mathbb{R}
is symmetric (
\It(x,y)=\It(y,x)
) and separates points (
\It(x,y)=0\implies x=y
), then it yields an abstract cross-ratio by the formula
B(u,v,x,y)=\tfrac{\It(u,v)\It(x,y)}{\It(u,y)\It(x,v)}
.
Indeed, the (
Invariance
), (
Invariance
) and (
Cocycle
) relations follow from the symmetry of
\It
and the formula for
B
; while the equations (

=

[
⋅
]

0

) and (

=

[
⋅
]

∞

‣
1.2
) follow from the separation of
\It
.
Moreover, if
\It
generates the topology of
Y
(in the sense that for all
x\in Y
the collection of subsets
\{x\in Y\colon\It(x,y)<\varepsilon\}
for
\varepsilon>0
is a basis of neighborhoods of
x
), then
B
generates the topology of
Y
.

Example 4.5

(strongly hyperbolic)
.

If a metric space
(X,d)
is strongly hyperbolic, then the cross-ratio introduced in
Section

2.3
defines an abstract cross-ratio on
\partial X
that generates the topology induced by any visual distance
d_{o}
.

Recall that a group
\Gamma
acts
3
-transitively on a space
X
when for any two triples of distinct points
(x_{i})
and
(y_{i})
, there is
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma(x_{i})=y_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Proposition 4.6

(abstract cross-ratios on
3
-full strongly hyperbolic spaces)
.

Consider a strongly hyperbolic space
(X,d)
with
\Card(\partial X)\geq 3
and a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X)
acting
3
-transitively on
\partial X
.
If an abstract cross-ratio
B
on
\partial X
generating the topology is
\Gamma
-invariant, then there exists
t>0
such that for all
(w,x,y,z)\in(\partial X)^{(4)}
we have
B(w,x,y,z)=\left[{w,x;y,z}\right]^{t}
.

Proof.

For distinct points
0,\infty\in\partial X
, consider the subgroup
H\subset\Gamma
of elements fixing both of them.
Choose
1\in\partial X\setminus\{0,\infty\}
, and define continuous functions
\vec{\ell}_{B},\vec{\ell}_{C}\colon H\to(\mathbb{R},+)
by
\vec{\ell}_{B}\colon\mapsto\log B(\gamma 1,0,1,\infty)
and
\vec{\ell}_{C}\colon\gamma\mapsto\log\left[{\gamma 1,0;1,\infty}\right]
.
They are group homomorphisms by the (
Cocycle
) relation.
Moreover every
\gamma\in H
has translation length
\ell(\gamma)=\lvert\vec{\ell}_{C}(\gamma)\rvert
by
Equation

9
.
Observe that for any abstract
\Gamma
-invariant cross-ratio
B
, for any
\xi\in\partial X\setminus\{0,\infty\}
and
\gamma\in H
, applying the (
Cocycle
) relation, the
H
-invariance of
B
, and the (
Inversion
) relation:

\displaystyle B(\gamma\xi,0,\xi,\infty)

\displaystyle=B(\gamma\xi,0,\gamma 1,\infty)B(\gamma 1,0,1,\infty)B(1,0,\xi,\infty)

\displaystyle=B(\gamma 1,0,1,\infty)

This implies that the homomorphisms
\vec{\ell}_{B},\vec{\ell}_{C}
do not depend on the choice of
1\in\partial X\setminus\{0,\infty\}
.
We aim to prove that these homomorphisms are positively proportional.

If
\gamma\in H
satisfies
\vec{\ell}_{C}(\gamma)>0
then
\gamma^{n}1\to\infty
hence by continuity of
B
we also have
\log B(\gamma^{n}1,0,1,\infty)\to+\infty
thus
\log B(\gamma 1,0,1,\infty)>0
: this proves that
\ker\vec{\ell}_{B}\subset\ker\vec{\ell}_{C}
.
A similar argument using the fact that
B
generates the topology implies the converse inclusion
\vec{\ell}_{B}\supset\ker\vec{\ell}_{C}
.
Hence
\vec{\ell}_{B}=\ker\vec{\ell}_{C}
consist of all non-loxodromic elements in
H
, and for all
\gamma\in H
there exists
t_{\gamma}>0
such that
\vec{\ell}_{B}(\gamma)=t_{\gamma}\vec{\ell}_{C}(\gamma)
, which is unique when
\gamma
is loxodromic.

Consider loxodromic
\alpha,\beta\in H
.
We may find
m_{k},n_{k}\to\infty
such
\vec{\ell}_{C}(\alpha^{m_{k}})-\vec{\ell}_{C}(\beta^{n_{k}})\to 0
, hence
\alpha^{m_{k}}\beta^{-n_{k}}1
does not converge to
0
or
\infty
.
However if
t_{\alpha}>t_{\beta}
, then
\vec{\ell}_{B}(\alpha^{m_{k}}\beta^{-n_{k}})\to\infty
thus
\alpha^{m_{k}}\beta^{-n_{k}}1
diverges to the attractive fixed point of
\alpha
in
\{0,\infty\}
which is a contradiction.
By symmetry we deduce
t_{\alpha}=t_{\beta}
.
This proves that there is
t>0
such that
\vec{\ell}_{B}=t\vec{\ell}_{C}
.

To conclude the proof, it remains to observe that for any distinct
w,x,y,z\in\partial X
, there is
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma(x,y,z)=(0,1,\infty)
, so
B(w,x,y,z)=B(\xi,0,1,\infty)
for some
\xi\in\partial X
and by triple transitivity, there is
\gamma\in\Gamma
fixing
0,\infty
such that
\xi=\gamma(1)
, thus
B(\xi,0,1,\infty)=\left[{\xi,0,1,\infty}\right]^{t}
and finally
B(w,x,y,z)=\left[{w,x,y,z}\right]^{t}
.
The cases where two points are equal follow from
Equation

=

[
⋅
]

0

or
Equation

=

[
⋅
]

∞

, or from (
Inversion
) that for distinct
x,y,z\in\partial X
we have
B(y,x,y,z)=B(x,y,z,y)=1
.
∎

Corollary 4.7

(cross-ratios preserved by
3
-equivariant boundary maps)
.

Consider for
i\in\{1,2\}
strongly hyperbolic spaces
(X_{i},d_{i})
and an embedding
f\colon\partial X_{1}\to\partial X_{2}
that is a homeomorphism on its image.
Assume that there is a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(X_{1})
acting
3
-transitively on
\partial X_{1}
and that
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

There is
t>0
such that for all
(w,x,y,z)\in(\partial X_{1})^{(4)}
we have

\left[{f(w),f(x);f(y),f(z)}\right]_{\partial{X_{2}}}=\left[{w,x;y,z}\right]_{\partial X_{1}}^{t}

Proof.

Define
B\colon(\partial X_{1})^{(4)}\to[0,\infty]
by
B(w,x,y,z)=\left[{f(w),f(x);f(y),f(z)}\right]_{\partial X_{2}}
. Since
f
is a homeomorphism onto its image, the abstract cross-ratio
B
is covered by
Proposition

4.6
.
∎

4.2
From kernels of hyperbolic type to strong hyperbolicity

For trees and exotic deformations of hyperbolic spaces, we have metric spaces
(X,d)
admitting
\varepsilon>0
such that kernel
\exp(\varepsilon d)
of hyperbolic type.
This raises the following.

Question 4.8
.

For which metric spaces
(X,d)
is there
\varepsilon>0
such that the kernel
\exp(\varepsilon d)
is of hyperbolic type?

Recall that (
3
) characterizes strong-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolicty by the Ptolemy inequality for
\exp(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon d)
.
The following is a coarse asymptotic weakening of this notion.

Definition 4.9

(coarse strong hyperbolicity)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
C\in\mathbb{R}
.
A metric space
(X,d)
is called
C
-coarsely strongly-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolic
when for all
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in X
, their distances
d_{ij}=d(x_{i},x_{j})
and their maximum
M=\max\{d_{ij}\colon 1\leq i,j\leq 4\}
satisfy:

\exp\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}(d_{13}+d_{24})\leq\exp\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}(d_{12}+d_{34})+\exp\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}(d_{14}+d_{23})+C\exp(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}M)

(19)

Remark 4.10

(terminology)
.

The spaces defined in
4.9
were introduced by
Miao–Schroeder in
[
45
, Definition 1]
under the name of
asymptotically
\operatorname{PT}_{-1}
spaces
.
Their preprint posted on arXiv in 2012 seems to remain unpublished.
Since then, the notion of strong hyperbolicity has been defined by Nica–Spakula in
[
52
]
and widely used since its introduction.
This is why we prefer to rename
\operatorname{PT}_{-1}
spaces as asymptotically strongly hyperbolic spaces.

The following
[
45
, Theorem 1.2]
shows that coarse strong hyperbolicity retains many of the properties of strong hyperbolicity.

Theorem 4.11

(coarsely strongly-hyperbolic implies regularly Gromov-hyperbolic)
.

If a metric space
(X,d)
is coarsely strongly hyperbolic, then
X
is Gromov-hyperbolic, the Gromov product extends continuously to the boundary, and all visual metametrics are metrics on
\partial X
and they are all Möbius equivalent (in the sense that they induce the same metric cross-ratio).

In algebraic hyperbolic spaces
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa},\dH)
we have the following Ptolemy inequality for
\sinh(\tfrac{1}{2}\dH)
, which is
[
31
, Corollary 4]
recovering
[
73
, Corollary of Theorem 3.2]
.

Lemma 4.12

(Ptolemy inequality in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
)
.

For all
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
with mutual distances denoted
\dH(x_{i},x_{j})=\delta_{ij}
, we have the Ptolemaic inequality:

\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{13}\right)\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{24}\right)\leq\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{12}\right)\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{34}\right)+\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{14}\right)\left(\sinh\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{23}\right)

(20)

which may be written in terms of the kernel of hyperbolic type using
\sqrt{2}\sinh(\tfrac{1}{2}\delta)=\sqrt{\cosh(\delta)-1}
.

Proposition 4.13

(kernel
\exp(d)
of hyperbolic type implies coarse strong-hyperbolicity)
.

If a metric space
(X,d)
admits an
\varepsilon>0
such that
\exp(\varepsilon d)
is a kernel of hyperbolic type then
(X,d)
is
3
-coarsely strongly-
\varepsilon
-hyperbolic.
In particular it satisfies the conclusions of
theorem

4.11
.

Proof.

After replacing
(X,d)
by
(X,\varepsilon d)
, it suffices to prove it for
\varepsilon=1
.

Suppose that
\exp(d)
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.
There is a cardinal
\kappa
and a continuous function
f\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
such that
\exp d(x,y)=\cosh\dH(f(x),f(y))
.
Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{4}\in X
. Denote
d_{ij}=d(x_{i},x_{j})
and
\delta_{ij}=\dH(f(x_{i}),f(x_{j}))
so that
\exp d_{ij}=\cosh\delta_{ij}
, and
M=\max_{ij}d_{ij}
.
Developing the Ptolemy inequality for
\sqrt{2}\sinh(\delta_{ij}/2)=\sqrt{\cosh(\delta_{ij})-1}=\sqrt{\exp(d_{ij})-1}
and using
\sqrt{x}-1\leq\sqrt{x-1}\leq\sqrt{x}
yields:

\exp\tfrac{1}{2}(d_{13}+d_{24})\leq\exp\tfrac{1}{2}(d_{12}+d_{34})+\exp\tfrac{1}{2}(d_{14}+d_{23})+3\exp(M/2)

proving the desired coarse strong-
1
-hyperbolicity with
C=3
.
∎

Remark 4.14

(not sufficient)
.

The necessary condition of
Proposition

4.13
is not sufficient.
Indeed, a quaternionic hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbb{H}}^{n}
is
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
thus (coarsely) strongly hyperbolic; however for
\varepsilon>0
the kernel
\exp(\varepsilon d)
cannot be of hyperbolic type, since all kernels of hyperbolic type on
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbb{H}}^{n}
that are
\Isom(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
-invariant must be bounded as we now explain.
On a real hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\kappa}
the kernel
\log\cosh d
is of conditionally negative type by
[
26
, Corollary 8.2]
.
However a kernel on
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbb{H}}^{n}
that is
\Isom(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
invariant and of conditionally negative type must be bounded, since
\Isom(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
has Property
\operatorname{FH}
by
[
1
, §3.3]
.

4.3
Algebraic cross-ratios

In this section we propose a GNS construction from infinity for real hyperbolic spaces.

Definition 4.15

(algebraic cross-ratio)
.

Let
Y
be a topological space. We define an
algebraic cross-ratio
on
Y
to be an abstract cross-ratio
such that the function on
(Y\setminus\{\infty\})^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined on the diagonal by
(y,y)\mapsto 0
and outside the diagonal by

(x,y)\mapsto\left(R(x,y,0,\infty)R(y,0,1,\infty)\right)^{2}

(21)

is a kernel conditionally of negative type on
Y\setminus\{\infty\}
.

Remark 4.16

(indeterminate value)
.

Note that when
y=0\neq x
, we can determine the value of
R(x,y,0,\infty)R(y,0,1,\infty)=\infty\times 0
using the cocycle relation to find
R(x,0,1,\infty)\in(0,\infty)
.

Example 4.17

(algebraic model)
.

Let us explain why, for an algebraic hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, the cross-ratio on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is an algebraic cross-ratio.
In the upper half space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}=\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}\times(0,+\infty)
, the boundary at infinity is identified with
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}\cup\{\infty\}
and for any
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}
, we have

\left[{x,y;z,\infty}\right]=\tfrac{\|y-x\|}{\|y-z\|}

(22)

because any quadruple is contained in an isometric copy of
\mathbf{H}^{3}
so we may use Corollary
2.7
.

Thus for any fixed distinct
0,1\in\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}
,

\left[{x,y;0,\infty}\right]\left[{y,0;1,\infty}\right]=\tfrac{\|y-x\|}{\|y-0\|}\cdot\tfrac{\|0-y\|}{\|0-1\|}=\tfrac{\|y-x\|}{\|0-1\|}

which is the square root of a kernel of conditionally negative type.

Theorem 4.18

(GNS for algebraic cross-ratios)
.

Consider a space
Y
having at least four points with an algebraic cross-ratio
R\colon Y^{(4)}\to[0,+\infty)
.

There is a cardinal
\kappa
and a continuous map
f\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
with total image such that for all
x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}\in Y^{(4)}
we have
\left[{f(x^{-}),f(x^{+}),f(y^{-}),f(y^{+})}\right]=R(x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+})
.

Moreover, for any two such maps
f_{1}\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}
and
f_{2}\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
, there exists a unique isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
such that
f_{2}=\varphi\circ f_{1}
, in particular
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
.

Consequently, if a group
\Gamma
acts on
Y
preserving
R
then there is a representation
\rho\colon G\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that
f
is
\rho
-equivariant.

Proof.

Choose a point
\infty\in\partial X
.
Fix two distinct points
0,1\in Y\setminus\{\infty\}
, and perform the GNS construction
3.6
to obtain a Hilbert space
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}
and a map
f\colon Y\setminus\{\infty\}\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}
with total image such that

\|f(y)-f(x)\|=R(x,y,0,\infty)R(y,0,1,\infty).

Consider the upper half-space model
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\kappa-1}\times(0,+\infty)
and extend
f\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
by sending
\infty
to
\infty
.
For all

x,y,z\in Y\setminus\{\infty\}
we have, using (
Inversion
) and (
Cocycle
) for
R
, that:

\left[{f(x),f(y);f(z),f(\infty)}\right]=\tfrac{\|f(y)-f(x)\|}{\|f(y)-f(z)\|}=\tfrac{R(x,y,0,\infty)}{R(z,y,0,\infty)}=R(x,y,z,\infty)

Using again (
Cocycle
), (
Invariance
) and (
Inversion
) we get for all
(x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+})\in Y^{(4)}
that:

\displaystyle[f(x^{-}),f(x^{+});f(y^{-}),f(y^{+})]

\displaystyle=[f(x^{-}),f(x^{+});f(\infty),f(y^{+})][f(\infty),f(x^{+});f(y^{-}),f(y^{+})]

\displaystyle=[f(x^{-}),f(x^{+});f(y^{+}),f(\infty)]^{-1}[f(y^{-}),f(y^{+});f(x^{+}),f(\infty)]^{-1}

\displaystyle=R(x^{-},x^{+},y^{+},\infty)^{-1}R(y^{-},y^{+},x^{+},\infty)^{-1}=R(x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}).

Thus
f\colon Y\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
preserves the cross-ratio.

Now, consider two such functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. Choose
\infty\in A
and let
\infty_{i}=f_{i}(\infty)
. Consider the upper half-space model of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{i}-1}\times(0,+\infty)
so that
\infty_{i}
is the point at infinity in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
and
||\ ||_{i}
is the Hilbert norm on
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{i}-1}
.
By construction, there exists a unique
\lambda>0
such that all
x,y\in Y\setminus\{\infty\}
satisfy

\|f_{2}(x)-f_{2}(y)\|_{2}=\lambda\|f_{1}(x)-f_{1}(y)\|_{1}.

Since the images of
f_{i}
in
\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{i}-1}
are total, there is a unique isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{1}-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{2}-1}
such that
f_{2}=\lambda.\varphi\circ f_{1}
on
Y\setminus\{\infty\}
.
The function
f^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
defined by
f^{\prime}(x,t)=(\lambda f(x),\lambda t)
yields an isometry with the desired properties, and its unicity follows from that of
f
.
∎

Proposition 4.19

(independence of exotic embeddings)
.

For
t\in(0,1)
, the exotic deformation
\exoH_{t}\colon\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa+\infty}
has the property that if
F
is a finite subset of
\partial\mathbf{\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}}
has
\Card(F)\geq 2
, then the minimal totally geodesic subspace of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa+\infty}
whose boundary contains
\exoH_{t}(F)
has dimension
\Card(F)-1
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.31
on powers of cross-ratios, for any quadruple of distinct
w,x,y,z\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, we have
\left[{\exoH_{t}(w),\exoH_{t}(x),\exoH_{t}(y),\exoH_{t}(z)}\right]=\left[{w,x,y,z}\right]^{t}
.

Fix a point
\infty\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
Using the upper half-space model described in
Example

4.17
, the set
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\setminus\{\infty\}
has a structure of Hilbert space, and we have a kernel
K=R^{2}
of conditionally negative type on
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\setminus\{\infty\}
defined by
R(x,y)=\|x-y\|=\frac{\left[{x,y,0,\infty}\right]}{\left[{y,0,1,\infty}\right]}
.
The same is true for
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa+\infty}\setminus\{\exoH_{t}(\infty)\}
and we have
R^{t}(x,y)=\frac{\left[{\exoH_{t}(x),\exoH_{t}(y),\exoH_{t}(0),\exoH_{t}(\infty)}\right]}{\left[{\exoH_{t}(y),\exoH_{t}(0),\exoH_{t}(1),\exoH_{t}(\infty)}\right]}=\frac{\|\exoH_{t}(x)-\exoH_{t}(y)\|}{\|\exoH_{t}(0)-\exoH_{t}(1)\|}
.

The affine subspaces of dimension
n
in the Hilbert space
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa+\infty}\setminus\{\exoH_{t}(\infty)\}
correspond to the boundaries of totally geodesic subspaces of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa+\infty}
of dimension
n+1
containing
\exoH_{t}(\infty)
in their boundary.
Since
K=R^{2}
is conditionally of negative type, by
Lemma

3.10
,
K^{t}=R^{2t}
is strictly of conditionally negative type, thus
\exoH_{t}
has affinely independent image on finite subsets of
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\setminus\{\infty\}
.
∎

4.4
Rigidity results from algebraic cross-ratios

We now apply
Theorem

4.18
to obtain rigidity results for actions on algebraic hyperbolic spaces.

Corollary 4.20

(Poincaré extension)
.

Consider, for
i=1,2
, total subsets
L_{i}\subset\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
.

For every bijection
f\colon L_{1}\to L_{2}
preserving the cross-ratio, there exists a unique isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
such that
\varphi\colon\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
coincides with
f
on
L_{1}
.

The unicity implies in particular that if
f
is equivariant for some group action then so is
\varphi
.

Proof.

When
n=\Card L_{i}<4
, this follows from the fact
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{1+n})
acts transitively on
n
-tuples of distinct points in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{1+n}
.
When
\Card L_{i}\geq 4
, we apply
Theorem

4.18
with
Y=L_{1}
to the identity
\mathbf{1}\colon L_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}
and
f\colon L_{1}\to L_{2}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
.
There exists a unique isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
whose restriction to
L_{1}
is
f
.
∎
