Let us illustrate the usefulness of algebraic cross-ratios to simplify one step in the proof of
[
39
, Theorem A]
about the rigidity of marked length spectrum, at least in the real case.

Theorem 4.21

(rigidity of marked-length spectrum)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group and
\rho_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
be two Zariski dense hyperbolic representations with finite dimensions
\kappa_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
i=1,2
.
If the marked length spectrum of the two representations are proportional then the representations are conjugated.
In particular,
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
and the marked length spectrum are equal.

Proof.

Let
\ell_{i}
be the length function associated with
\rho_{i}
. Up to permuting the two representations, there is
0<t\leq 1
such that
\ell_{1}=t\ell_{2}
.
Let
\rho_{2}^{\prime}=\chi_{t}\circ\rho_{2}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
where
\kappa=\kappa_{2}
if
t=1
and
\kappa=\infty
if
t<1
. The length function
\ell_{2}^{\prime}
associated with
\rho_{2}^{\prime}
satisfies
\ell_{2}^{\prime}=\ell_{1}
.
Hence denoting by
L_{i}
the set of attractive points at infinity of loxodromic elements of
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}^{\prime}
, it follows from
[
39
, Lemma 3]
that there is a bijection
f\colon L_{1}\to L_{2}
that preserves the cross-ratio.
By Corollary
4.20
, there is an equivariant isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. In particular,
\kappa=\kappa_{1}
and thus
t=1
which implies that
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
.
∎

The following generalizes
[
39
, Lemma 3]
to infinite dimensions.

Lemma 4.22

(translation length recovers cross-ratio on limit set)
.

Consider for
i\in\{1,2\}
, representations
\rho_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})

whose translation length functions are non-zero and equal.
There exists an equivariant map between their limit sets
\varphi\colon\Lambda_{1}\to\Lambda_{2}
preserving the cross-ratio such that every
\rho_{i}
-loxodromic
\gamma\in\Gamma
has its fixed points
\rho_{i}(\gamma)^{\pm}
satisfying
\varphi(\rho_{1}(\gamma)^{\pm})=\rho_{2}(\gamma)^{\pm}
.

Proof.

Since
\ell_{i}
is non-zero, the subset of fixed points of its loxodromic elements
L_{i}\subset\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
has
\Card L_{i}\in\{2,\infty\}
, and it is dense by
[
18
, Propositions 7.4.6]
in its limit set
\Lambda_{i}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
.

Since the translation length functions coincide, the subsets of elements in
\Gamma
that act loxodromically with respect to
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
coincide.
For two such loxodromic elements
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
, it follows from
Lemma

1.23
that
\rho_{1}(\alpha)^{+}=\rho_{1}(\beta)^{+}\iff\rho_{2}(\alpha)^{+}=\rho_{2}(\beta)^{+}
.
Thus we have a bijection
\varphi\colon L_{1}\to L_{2}
defined by the formula
\rho_{1}(\gamma)^{+}\mapsto\rho_{2}(\gamma)^{+}
.
Let us show that
\varphi
preserves the cross-ratio. Actually, for any elements
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
that are hyperbolic for
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
, by
Equation

10
we have

\left[{\rho_{1}(\alpha)^{+},\rho_{1}(\alpha)^{-},\rho_{1}(\beta)^{+},\rho_{1}(\beta)^{-}}\right]=\left[{\rho_{1}(\alpha)^{+},\rho_{1}(\alpha)^{-},\rho_{1}(\beta)^{+},\rho_{1}(\beta)^{-}}\right].

Moreover for any group elements
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
that are hyperbolic for
\rho_{i}
, with
\rho_{i}(\alpha)^{+}\neq\rho_{i}(\beta)^{+}
and
\rho_{i}(\gamma)^{+}\neq\rho_{i}(\delta)^{+}
, we have
\rho_{i}(\alpha^{n}\beta^{-n})^{+}\to\rho_{i}(\alpha)^{+}
,
\rho_{i}(\alpha^{n}\beta^{-n})^{-}\to\rho_{i}(\beta)^{+}
,
\rho_{i}(\gamma^{n}\delta^{-n})^{+}\to\rho_{i}(\gamma)^{+}
and
\rho_{i}(\gamma^{n}\delta^{-n})^{-}\to\rho_{i}(\delta)^{+}
. So

\displaystyle\left[{\rho_{1}(\alpha)^{+},\rho_{1}(\beta)^{+};\rho_{1}(\gamma)^{+},\rho_{1}(\delta)^{+}}\right]=

\displaystyle\lim_{n}\left[{\rho_{i}(\alpha^{n}\beta^{-n})^{+},\rho_{i}(\alpha^{n}\beta^{-n})^{-};\rho_{i}(\gamma^{n}\delta^{-n})^{+},\rho_{i}(\gamma^{n}\delta^{-n})^{-}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[{\rho_{2}(\alpha)^{+},\rho_{2}(\beta)^{+};\rho_{2}(\gamma)^{+},\rho_{2}(\delta)^{+}}\right]

which proves that
\varphi\colon L_{1}\to L_{2}
preserves the cross-ratio. It extends by continuity to a cross-ratio preserving map between the limit sets
\Lambda_{1}\to\Lambda_{2}
.

Observe that if
\Card L_{i}=2
then the condition about the cross-ratio is empty.
∎

Recall from
Remark

2.20
that an irreducible representation is a minimal representation without fixed point at infinity.
The following proposition shows that functions of hyperbolic type at uniformly bounded distance from one another correspond to irreducible representations up to conjugacy.

Proposition 4.23

(rigidity of marked length spectrum)
.

Consider a group
\Gamma
with minimal representations
\rho_{i}\colon G\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
for
i=1,2
.
If
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
have non-trivial and equal length functions
\ell_{\rho_{1}}=\ell_{\rho_{2}}
, then
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
and
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
are conjugated by an isometry
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
.

Proof.

The Lemma
4.22
provides a
\rho_{i}
-equivariant cross-ratio preserving map
\varphi\colon\Lambda_{1}\to\Lambda_{2}
, and we have
\Card\Lambda_{i}
is
2
or uncountable.
If
\Card L_{i}=\infty
is uncountable, by minimality of
\rho_{i}
, the set
\Lambda_{i}
is total in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
, so the Corollary
4.20
yields a
\rho_{i}
-equivariant isometry
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
.
If
\Card L_{i}=2
, then we have two isometric actions of
\Gamma
on the real line, and it is an exercise to show that two such actions with the same (non-zero) marked length spectrum are conjugated.
∎

Corollary 4.24

(length equivalent)
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, consider non-neutral
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{nn}(\Gamma)
yielding non-zero length functions
\ell_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
For any minimal representations
\rho_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
realizing
F_{i}
, the following are equivalent:

F
:

The functions
\log(F_{1}/F_{2})
or equivalently
\cosh^{-1}F_{1}-\cosh^{-1}F_{2}
are bounded.

\ell_{F}
:

The length functions
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are equal.

\rho_{F}
:

The representations
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are conjugate.

Proof.

If
\log(F_{1}/F_{2})
is bounded then the Lemma
3.23
recovering
\ell_{F}
from
F
implies
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
.

In that case, the representations
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
realizing
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are non-neutral and minimal so by Proposition
4.23
they are conjugated by an isometry
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
.

In that case, choosing
o_{i}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
with
o_{2}=\varphi(o_{1})
, we have
\dH(o_{1},\rho_{1}(\gamma)o_{1})=\dH(o_{2},\rho_{2}(\gamma)o_{2})
so
\|\cosh^{-1}F_{1}(\gamma)-\cosh^{-1}F_{2}(\gamma)\|
is bounded, or equivalently
\log(F_{1}/F_{2})
is bounded.
∎

Remark 4.25

(neutral exceptions)
.

Corollary

4.24
does not generalize to the neutral case.
For example, recall from
Section

2.4
that elliptic or parabolic representations have zero length functions, but by Lemma
3.27
their associated functions of hyperbolic type are respectively bounded and unbounded.

Corollary 4.26

(homothetic marked length spectra)
.

Consider functions of hyperbolic type
F_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
yielding non-trivial length functions
\ell_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and any minimal representations
\rho_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
realizing
F_{i}
.
For every
t\in(0,1]
the following are equivalent:

F
:

The
F_{i}
are
t
-equivalent
, namely
\log(F_{1}/F_{2}^{t})
is bounded.

\ell_{F}
:

The
\ell_{i}
are
t
-homothetic
, namely
\ell_{1}=t\ell_{2}
.

\rho_{F}
:

The representation
\rho_{1}
is conjugate to the
t
-deformed representation
\chi_{t}\circ\rho_{2}
.

In that case the
F_{i}
share the same objects and attributes associated to the homothety class of
\ell_{i}
, such as elementarity and neutrality (yielding a
t
-variant of
Corollary

3.25
).

Proof.

If
\log(F_{1}/F_{2}^{t})
is bounded then the Lemma
3.23
recovering
\ell_{F}
from
F
implies
\ell_{1}=t\ell_{2}
.

In that case, the representations
\rho_{1},\chi_{t}\circ\rho_{2}
realizing
F_{1}
and
F_{2}^{t}
are non-neutral and minimal so by Proposition
4.23
they are conjugated by
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{2}}
.

In that case, choosing
o_{i}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}}
with
o_{2}=\varphi(o_{1})
, we have
\dH(o_{1},\rho_{1}(\gamma)o_{1})=\dH(o_{2},(\rho_{t}\circ\rho_{2})(\gamma)o_{2})
so
\|\cosh^{-1}F_{1}(\gamma)-\cosh^{-1}F_{2}^{t}(\gamma)\|
is bounded, or equivalently
\log(F_{1}/F_{2}^{t})
is bounded.
∎

5
Deformations and degenerations to actions on trees

5.1
Critical exponent, dimension, representative

Definition 5.1

(critical exponent of a kernel of hyperbolic type)
.

For a kernel of hyperbolic type
K\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
we define its
critical exponent

t_{K}\in[1,\infty]
as the supremum of
t\in[1,\infty)
such that
K^{t}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.

When
t_{K}<\infty
we call
K^{t_{K}}
the
critical representative
of
K
, and thus
Proposition

3.13
defines the associated
critical dimension

\kappa_{K}
for which there exists an essentially unique
critical embedding

f_{K^{t_{K}}}\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{K}}
with total image satisfying
K^{t_{K}}=\cosh\circ\dH
.

Definition 5.2

(arboreal)
.

A kernel of hyperbolic type
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
is
arboreal
when there is a real tree
(T,d)
, a function
f\colon X\to T
such that
K(x,y)=\exp d(f(x),f(y))
.

Proposition 5.3

(arboreal = infinite critical exponent)
.

A kernel of hyperbolic type
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
is arboreal if and only if it has critical exponent
t_{K}=\infty
.

Proof.

Assume that
X
is arboreal, and consider a function to a real tree
f\colon X\to T
such that
K(x,y)=\exp(d(f(x),f(y)))
.
For all
t>0
, we have
\exp(td)\in\mathcal{K}(T)
hence composing with
f
shows that
K^{t}\in\mathcal{K}(X)
.

Conversely assume that
t_{K}=+\infty
, namely for all
t>0
the power
K^{t}\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.
By
Proposition

3.13
, there is an embedding
f_{t}\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{t}}
with total image such that
K^{t}(x,y)=\cosh(\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y)))
.
Thus for all
t>1
, we have:

\log K(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{t}\log\cosh\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y)).

This implies that
\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y))\to+\infty
as
t\to\infty
, and since
\log\cosh(u)\sim u
as
u\to+\infty
we have:

\log K(x,y)\sim\tfrac{1}{t}\dH(f_{t}(x),f_{t}(y)).

By taking the limit we find that
\log K
satisfies the triangle inequality, and as it is symmetric it defines a semi-metric on
X
.
Hence on
X
the relation
\log K(x,x^{\prime})=0
is an equivalence relation and the quotient
X\bmod{K=1}
is endowed with metric
\log K
.
Moreover we deduce from the asymptotic for
\log K
and the fact
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{t}},\dH)
is Gromov-
\log(2)
-hyperbolic, that the metric space
(X\bmod{K=1},\log K)
is
0
-hyperbolic.
The result now follows from
Proposition

1.6
.
∎

Remark 5.4

(infinitely divisible)
.

There is a similar notion to being arboreal for kernels of positive type: to be infinitely divisible.
A kernel of positive type
K
that takes nonnegative values is
infinitely divisible
when for all
t>0
the kernel
K^{t}
is of positive type (see
[
2
, Definition 2.6 and Proposition 2.7]
).

This is more than an analogy: if
K
is a kernel hyperbolic type that is arboreal then
K^{-1}
is a kernel of positive type that is infinitely divisible. Indeed if
K
is arboreal, then there is
f\colon X\to T
and
d
as in
Definition

5.2
, such that
K=\exp(D_{T})
where
D_{T}\colon(x,y)\mapsto d(f(x),f(y))
.
The kernel
D_{T}
is conditionally of negative type (for trees, this can be found in
[
37
]
; for real trees, it follows by approximating the convex hull of finitely many points by a finite tree with arbitrarily small edges).
Hence the kernel
K^{-1}=\exp(-D_{T})
takes nonnegative values and satisfies condition (ii) in
[
2
, Proposition 2.7]
, hence it is infinitely divisible.

The converse does not hold (namely
K^{-1}
could be infinitely divisible and
K
not arboreal), see for example
[
2
, Exercise 2.14]
.

Proposition 5.5

(critical exponent is upper-semi-continuous)
.

The function sending a kernel of hyperbolic type
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
to its critical exponent
t_{K}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\sqcup\{\infty\}
is upper semi-continuous.
Moreover if
t_{K}<\infty
then
K^{t_{K}}\in\mathcal{K}(X)
.

Proof.

For the upper semi-continuity, we will show that for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\sqcup\{\infty\}
the preimage of
[1,t)
, namely the subset
\mathcal{V}^{t}
of
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
such that
K^{t}\notin\mathcal{K}(X)
, is open.
For fixed
x=(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\in X^{1+n}
, the subset
\mathcal{V}^{t}_{x}
of
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
such that
K^{t}
violates the hyperbolic Cayler-Menger determinant inequality (
HCM
) at
x
, namely such that
\det\left(-K^{t}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{0\leq i,j\leq n}>0
, is open for the pointwise convergence.
By definition
\mathcal{V}^{t}
is the union of
\mathcal{V}^{t}_{x}
over all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in X^{1+n}
, so it is open.

Now assuming that
t_{K}<\infty
, suppose by contradiction that
K^{t_{K}}\notin\mathcal{K}
.
By definition, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in X^{1+n}
such that
\det\left(-K^{t_{K}}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{0\leq i,j\leq n}>0
.
Since for every
x
the function
t\mapsto\det\left(-K^{t}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{0\leq i,j\leq n}
is continuous, there exists
t_{x}<t_{K}
such that
K\in\mathcal{V}^{t}_{x}
, which would imply
t_{K}\leq t_{x}
, a contradiction.
Hence
K^{t_{K}}\in\mathcal{K}(X)
as desired.
∎

Applying upper-semi-continuity of the critical exponent at
t_{K}=\infty
yields the following.

Corollary 5.6

(arboreal are closed)
.

In the space of kernels of hyperbolic type, those which are arboreal form a closed set.

Definition 5.7

(critical dimension and representative)
.

Let
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
.

When
t_{K}<\infty
, we define the
critical representative
of
K
as
K^{t_{K}}\in\mathcal{K}(X)
, and
Proposition

3.13
defines the associated
critical dimension

\kappa_{K}
for which there exists an essentially unique
critical map

f_{K^{t_{K}}}\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{K}}
with total image satisfying
K^{t_{K}}=\cosh\circ\dH
.

When
K
is arboreal, we define the
critical dimension

\kappa
such that for all
t>0
, we have a map
f_{K^{t}}\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
with total image satisfying
K^{t}=\cosh\circ\dH
as in
Proposition

3.13
.

Proposition 5.8
.

For
K_{0}\in\mathcal{K}(X)
with
\kappa_{K_{0}}<\infty
, if
t_{K_{0}}<\infty
then the critical dimension function
K\mapsto\kappa_{K}
is lower semi-continuous at
K_{0}
.

Remark 5.9

(variant assumption)
.

When
X
is infinite, if
\kappa_{K_{0}}<\infty
and
K_{0}
separates points (namely
K_{0}(x,y)=1\implies x=y
) then
t_{K_{0}}<\infty
(since otherwise we would have
\dim(K_{0}^{t_{K_{0}}})=\Card(X)-1=\infty
by
Proposition

3.30
contradicting
\kappa_{0}<\infty
), so
Proposition

5.8
applies.

Proof.

Choose any ultrafilter
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathcal{K}(X)
that converges to
K_{0}
.

By
Proposition

5.5
, the limit
t_{\mathcal{F}}=\lim_{\mathcal{F}}t_{K}
satisfies
t_{\mathcal{F}}\leq t_{K_{0}}<\infty
.
Moreover, we have
\lim_{\mathcal{F}}K^{t_{K}}=K_{0}^{t_{\mathcal{F}}}
so by
Proposition

3.18
on lower semi-continuity of the dimension we have

\lim_{\mathcal{F}}\kappa_{K}\geq\dim(K_{0}^{t_{\mathcal{F}}})
.

Finally, the linear independence proved in
Proposition

3.30
shows that for any
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
and all
t\leq 1
, we have
\dim(K^{t})\geq\dim(K)
, so
\dim(K_{0}^{t_{\mathcal{F}}})\geq\kappa_{K_{0}}
.

Chaining up the inequalities yields

\lim_{\mathcal{F}}\kappa_{K}\geq\dim(K_{0}^{t_{\mathcal{F}}})\geq\kappa_{K_{0}}
, proving the desired lower semi-continuity of
K\mapsto\kappa_{K}
at
K_{0}
.

Let us note that when
X
is countable, the space of kernels of hyperbolic type is metrizable, so one may simplify this proof replacing the ultrafilter by a converging sequence up to extracting two successive subsequences.
∎

Remark 5.10

(
Proposition

5.8
may fail at arboreal)
.

When
X
has at least four elements
o,x,y,z
, if
K_{0}
is arboreal then
Proposition

5.8
can fail as we now show.

In
\mathbf{H}^{2}
consider for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
an equilateral triangle with vertices
x_{n},y_{n},z_{n}
and incenter
o_{n}
such that
d(o_{n},x_{n})=n
.
The map
f_{n}\colon X\to\mathbf{H}^{2}
sending
(x,y,z)\mapsto(x_{n},y_{n},z_{n})
and sending all other points to
o_{n}
yields a kernel of hyperbolic type on
X
defined by
K_{n}(u,v)=\cosh\dH(f_{n}(u),f_{n}(v))
; satisfying in particular
K_{n}(o,x)=K(o,y)=K(o,z)=\cosh(n)
.
The sequence of kernels
K_{n}^{1/n}
converges to the arboreal kernel of hyperbolic type
K_{0}
associated to the star with three edges of length
e
.
For all
n
,
\kappa_{K_{n}^{1/n}}=2
but
\kappa_{K_{0}}=3
, so this contradicts lower semi-continuity. The issue is that
t_{K_{n}^{1/n}}=n\to\infty
.

Such counter examples cannot occur when
X
is infinite and
K_{0}
with
\kappa_{0}<\infty
separates points (as mentioned in
Remark

5.9
).

5.2
Equivariant embeddings of real trees into
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}

After
[
50
,
3
,
62
]
, the spaces of actions of groups containing non-abelian free group on a finite dimensional hyperbolic space have been compactified by spaces of actions on real trees.

In fact, the action of a group
\Gamma
on a real tree
T
also yields an action on
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
via the following embedding theorem, proved for simplicial trees in
[
12
]
, and extended to real trees in
[
18
, Theorem 13.1.1]
and
[
48
, Proposition 1.5]
.

Theorem 5.11

(equivariant isometric embedding of trees into hyperbolic space)
.

Let
T
be a real tree. For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>1}
there exists a cardinal
\kappa
and a representation
\pi_{\lambda}\colon\Isom(T)\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and an
\Isom(T)
-equivariant continuous injective function
\exoT_{\lambda}\colon T\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
such that

\lambda^{d(x,y)}=\cosh\dH(\exoT_{\lambda}(x),\exoT_{\lambda}(y)).

(23)

Moreover, the embedding
\exoT_{\lambda}
extends to an
\Isom(T)
-equivariant embedding
\partial\exoT_{\lambda}\colon\partial T\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
which is a homeomorphism on its image.

Remark 5.12

(unicity)
.

It follows from the unicity statement for kernels of hyperbolic type (
Proposition

3.13
) if any function
f\colon T\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}}
with total image satisfies
Equation

23
, then
\kappa=\kappa^{\prime}
and there exists
\varphi\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that
f=\varphi\circ\exoT_{\lambda}
.

Remark 5.13

(maybe not irreducible)
.

The representation constructed in
Theorem

5.11
is often not irreducible, even when the action on
T
is minimal (namely has no invariant convex subspace. For example, it
T
is a simplicial tree, the closed span of the images of the vertices will be an invariant subspace which does not contain any midpoint of an edge. Actually, to see that, one can consider the barycentric subdivision of the tree and apply the explicit construction in
[
12
, §8]
to see that the image of the vertices lie in the Hilbert space spanned by the Dirac functions
\delta_{v}
where
v
is a vertex and for the midpoint of an edge
m
, its image has non zero component with respect to
\delta_{m}
which is a unit vector orthogonal to all
\delta_{v}
’s.

Remark 5.14

(homothetic)
.

It follows that for a tree
T
with a fixed base point
o\in T
, for
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}>1
the functions of hyperbolic type
P_{i}\colon\gamma\in\Isom(T)\mapsto\cosh\dH(\exoT_{\lambda_{i}}(o),\pi_{\lambda_{i}}(\gamma)\cdot\exoT_{\lambda_{i}}(o))\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
on
\Isom(T)
associated with
\exoT_{\lambda_{i}}(o),\pi_{\lambda_{i}}
satisfy
P_{\lambda_{1}}=P_{\lambda_{0}}^{t}
where
t=\tfrac{\log(\lambda_{1})}{\log(\lambda_{0})}
, hence the
P_{i}
are homothetic.

Remark 5.15

(homothety at large scale but non-rectifiable at small scale)
.

The embedding
\exoT_{\lambda}
in Theorem
5.11
scales the metric according to different regimes.

At large scale
\exoT_{\lambda}
behaves like a homothety of ratio
\log(\lambda)
since
\dH(\exoT_{\lambda}(x),\exoT_{\lambda}(y))
is asymptotic to
\log(\lambda)\cdot d(x,y)
when
d(x,y)\to\infty
(see Lemma
5.18
).
At infinitesimal scale, the embedding is much more complicated. For example, the image of a geodesic in
T
is not a rectifiable curve since by Equation (
23
), we have
\dH(\exoT_{\lambda}(x),\exoT_{\lambda}(y))=H(d(x,y))
where
H\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a continuous function that is differentiable on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
\sinh(H(d))H^{\prime}(d)=\log(\lambda)\lambda^{d}
.
Thus
\lim_{d\to 0}H^{\prime}(d)=+\infty
which implies that the image of any geodesic has infinite length.

We may now characterise when a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
comes from an action on a real tree as in
Theorem

5.11
.

Corollary 5.16

(arboreal)
.

For
F\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
, the following are equivalent:

•

The kernel
K\in\mathcal{K}(\Gamma)
defined by
K(\alpha,\beta)=F(\alpha^{-1}\beta)
is arboreal (
Definition

5.2
).

•

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the function
F^{t}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
is of hyperbolic type.

•

For any corresponding representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
, there is a real tree
(T,d)
and a representation
\rho_{T}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(T)
such that
\rho=\pi_{e}\circ\rho_{T}
where
\pi_{e}
is given by
Theorem

5.11
.

Proof.

The proof follows from
Proposition

5.3
using the equivariance of the kernels and embeddings in
Theorem

5.11
.
∎

Remark 5.17

(bounded from arboreal does not imply arboreal)
.

On a group
\Gamma
, there may exist functions of hyperbolic type at bounded distance from an arboreal one but that are not arboreal.

For instance with
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, consider a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\mathbf{H}^{2}
sending the generator to a hyperbolic translation, and choose any point
o
off its axis to obtain such a function of hyperbolic type
F=F_{\rho,o}
.

More generally, consider any minimal action of a group
\Gamma
on an infinite real tree
(T,d)
.
We obtain from the embedding
\exoT_{\lambda}\colon T\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
an irreducible representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.
Choosing a base point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
yields a function of hyperbolic type
F_{\rho,o}
: it is arboreal if and only if
o\in\exoT_{\lambda}(T)
.
Those are all at bounded distance from one another.

We may apply Lemma
3.32
in this context to find how the representation
\pi_{\lambda}
modifies the length function on
\Isom(T)
and the cross-ratio on
\partial T
. The statement about length function already appears in
[
18
, Remark 13.1.6]
.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
T
be a real tree and consider an
\Isom(T)
-equivariant embedding
\pi_{\lambda}\colon\Isom(T)\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
satisfying Equation (
23
) for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
.

For
\gamma\in\Isom(T)
we have
\ell_{\mathbf{H}^{\omega}}(\pi_{\lambda}(\gamma))=\log(\lambda)\ell_{T}(\gamma)
.

For
x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}\in\partial T
, we have

\left[{\exoT_{\lambda}(x^{-}),\exoT_{\lambda}(x^{+});\exoT_{\lambda}(y^{-}),\exoT_{\lambda}(y^{+})}\right]=\left[{x^{-},x^{+},y^{-},y^{+}}\right]^{\log(\lambda)}
.

Remark 5.19

(deforming
\exoH_{t}
versus
\exoT_{\lambda}
)
.

For the equivariant embeddings of hyperbolic spaces
\exoH_{t}\colon\mathbf{H}^{n}\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
given by the exotic deformation
Theorem

3.28
, the length spectrum can only be compressed by
t^{\prime}\leq 1
.
For the equivariant embeddings of real trees
\exoT_{\lambda}\colon T\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
in
Theorem

5.11
the length spectrum can be compressed or dilated depending on whether
\lambda
is smaller or larger than
\exp(1)
.

Remark 5.20

(characterising
\exoT_{\lambda}
from the cross-ratio on dense subsets of
\partial T
)
.

Consider a real tree
(T,d)
and a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Aut(T)
.
Assume that
\Gamma
acts minimally on
T
(so that it has no strict non-empty closed convex
\Gamma
-invariant subset).
In that case
\exoT_{\lambda}
can be characterized as the unique irreducible representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that every
\gamma\in\Gamma
satisfies
\ell_{\rho}(\gamma)=\lambda\ell_{T}(\gamma)
.
Equivalently, this is the unique irreducible representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that the image of any loxodromic element is loxodromic, and for any loxodromic
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
we have
[\rho(\alpha)^{-},\rho(\alpha)^{+};\rho(\beta)^{-},\rho(\beta)^{+}]=[\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+};\beta^{-},\beta^{+}]^{\lambda}
. This follows from
Proposition

4.23
and
Corollary

4.20
.

5.3
Geometric limits of actions on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
to actions on a real tree
T

We endow
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
with its Polish topology (induced by the pseudo-metrics
(g,h)\mapsto d(gx,hx)
for
x\in\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
, see
[
23
]
).
For a countable group
\Gamma
, the product topology on
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})^{\Gamma}
is Polish as well, so this restricts to a Polish topology on the closed subset of representations
\Hom(\Gamma,\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega}))
.
More concretely, a sequence of representations
\rho_{m}
converges to a representation
\rho
if and only if for all
(\gamma,x)\in\Gamma\times\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
we have convergence
\rho_{m}(\gamma)(x)\to\rho(\gamma)(x)
(it suffices to consider
x
in a total subset of
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
).

Definition 5.21

(infimum displacement length)
.

For a finite subset
S\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
, we define the infimum
S
-displacement length as

\Delta(S)=\inf\{\max\{\dH(\gamma o,o)\colon\gamma\in S\}\colon o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\}.

Lemma 5.22

(infimum displacement achieved)
.

If a finite set
S
generates a subgroup
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
with no fixed points in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, then the infimum defining
\Delta(S)
is achieved at some point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.

Proof.

The function
f\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
defined by
f(x)=\max\{\dH(\gamma x,x)\colon\gamma\in S\}
is a maximum of continuous and convex functions, hence it is continuous and convex. For
l\in(\Delta(S),+\infty)
, the set
C_{l}=f^{-1}([\Delta(S),l])
is a closed convex subset of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, and we obtain decreasing intersections
C_{\cap}=\bigcap_{l}C_{l}
and
\overline{C}_{\cap}=\bigcap_{l}(C_{l}\cup\partial C_{l})
.
By
[
22
, Proposition 4.4]
, either
C_{\cap}\neq\emptyset
, in which case
f
has a minimum in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
as desired; or else
\overline{C}_{\cap}
consists of single point
\xi\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, which must be fixed by
S
(since every element in
S
displaces
C_{l}
by at most
l
), a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 5.23

(geometric convergence to an action on a tree)
.

Consider a group
\Gamma
with a finite generating set
S
, a sequence of cardinals
\kappa_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
and a sequence of representations
\rho_{m}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}})
.

If no
\rho_{m}
has a fixed point at infinity, but the infimum displacement length
\Delta(\rho_{m}(S))
is unbounded as
m\to\infty
, then up to extracting a subsequence of
(\rho_{m})
there are:

1.

a real tree
(T,d)
and an isometric action
\rho_{T}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(T)

2.

an embedding
\exoT_{\lambda}\colon T\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
equivariant for
\pi_{\lambda}\colon\Isom(T)\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
with
\lambda=e
,

3.

an isometric action
\rho_{\omega}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
preserving
\exoT_{\lambda}(T)
,

4.

a sequence of representations
\rho_{m}^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
such that
F_{\rho_{m}^{\prime}}\sim F_{\rho_{m}}
and

5.

a sequence of
(\rho_{m},\rho_{m}^{\prime})
-equivariant exotic embeddings
\exoH_{t_{m}}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

such that
(\rho^{\prime}_{m})_{m}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
converges to
\rho_{\omega}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
and
\rho_{\omega}=\pi_{\lambda}\circ\rho_{T}
.

Moreover, there exist points
o_{m}\in\operatorname{Core}(\rho_{m})
and
o\in T
such that for every finite set
K\subset\Gamma
, we have Hausdorff convergence of
\exoH_{t_{m}}(\Conv(\rho_{m}(K)\cdot o_{m}))
to
\exoT_{\lambda}(\Conv(\rho_{\omega}(K)\cdot o))
.

Remark 5.24

(recovering previous results)
.

Let us first emphasize that one of our new contributions lies in the freedom to consider actions
\rho_{m}
on hyperbolic spaces whose dimensions
\kappa_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
form a non constant sequence of countable cardinals, whereas all past works are in the setting where
\kappa_{m}
is bounded by or equal to a fixed finite integer
n
.

If we specialize our Theorem
5.23
to the case of a group
\Gamma
which is not virtually Abelian and of faithful discrete representations
\rho_{m}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
with a constant sequence of finite dimensions
\kappa_{m}=n
, then the tree that we obtain is isometric to one of the trees obtained by Morgan
[
51
, Theorem 3.6]
(see also
[
56
]
for a survey of similar results by Culler, Morgan, Shalen and Otal) and later recovered independently by Bestvina
[
3
, Theorem A]
and Paulin
[
62
]
.
Under these additional assumptions, they deduce that the limiting action of
\Gamma
on
T
has small edge stabilizers.
Our proof does not use their results and reconstructs the tree by different methods, but still relies on the idea to renormalize the metric.

The actions on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
once embedded inside
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
converge in a precise geometric sense, as explained by the Hausdorff convergence of the convex hull of finite portion of orbits.
The proof in
Step 7
is to be compared with those in
[
16
, §1.3 and Proposition 1.9]
which deals with the case where the sequence of dimensions
\kappa_{m}
is a constant integer
\kappa_{m}=n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Assume that the finite generating set
S
is symmetric and contains
1
, so that the ball of radius
k\in\mathbb{N}
is
S^{k}\subset\Gamma
.

Step 1:
Position the convex cores
\operatorname{Core}(\rho_{m})
around a fixed point
o
.

Since
\rho_{m}
has no fixed points at infinity, we may find by
Lemma

5.22
a base point
o_{m}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
minimizing the
\rho_{m}(S)
-displacement function
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\omega}\mapsto\max\{d(\rho_{m}(\gamma)(o),o)\colon\gamma\in S\}
. Up to embedding isometrically each
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
extending trivially
\rho_{m}
on the orthogonal of the span of the image of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
, we may and shall assume that
\kappa_{m}=\omega
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
After conjugating each
\rho_{m}
, we may assume that each
o_{m}
coincide with a single base point
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

Since
\rho_{m}
has no fixed points at infinity there is a smallest non-empty closed convex invariant set
\operatorname{Core}(\rho_{m})\subset\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.
Since projections on closed convex subsets contract distances, we must have
o\in\operatorname{Core}(\rho_{m})
.
Let
C_{m}^{k}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
be the closed convex hull of
\rho_{m}(S^{k})(o)
.
Observe that
\overline{\cup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C_{m}^{k}}
is a non-empty invariant closed convex subset of
\operatorname{Core}(\rho_{m})
, hence these subsets coincide.

Step 2:
Rescale by exotic deformations
\exoH_{t_{m}}
to focus on the convex cores.

Let
d_{m}=\max\{\dH(\rho_{m}(\gamma)(o),o)\colon\gamma\in S\}
and
t_{m}=d_{m}^{-1}
.
Consider the equivariant exotic deformation
\exoH_{t_{m}}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\omega}\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
from Theorem
3.28
, so that:

\forall x,y\in\mathbf{H}^{\omega}\colon\quad\cosh\dH(\exoH_{t_{m}}(x),\exoH_{t_{m}}(y))=\left(\cosh\dH(x,y)\right)^{t_{m}}.

(24)

and assume (after composing
\exoH_{t_{m}}
with an isometry) that
\exoH_{t_{m}}(o)=o
.
Let
\rho_{m}^{\prime}=\chi_{t_{m}}\circ\rho_{m}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
.
Triangle inequalities show that every
\gamma\in S^{k}
satisfies
\dH(\rho_{m}(\gamma)(o),o)\leq kd_{m}
, thus taking
\cosh^{-1}\log
of (
24
) and using for
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the inequalities
\cosh^{-1}\exp(x)\leq x+\log(2)
and
\log\cosh(x)\leq x
yields:

\dH(\rho_{m}^{\prime}(\gamma)(o),o)\leq\cosh^{-1}\exp(t_{m}\log\cosh(kd_{m}))\leq(t_{m}\log\cosh(kd_{m}))+\log(2)\leq k+\log(2)

hence
\exoH_{t_{m}}(C_{m}^{k})\subset B(o,k+\log(2))
.

Step 3:
Construct dyadic subdivisions
X_{m}^{k,i}
of
C_{m}^{k}
and
reposition
Y_{m}^{k,i}=\exoH_{t_{m}}(X_{m}^{k,i})
in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

For
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
and
\delta\in[0,1]
, let
x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)
be the point on
[\rho_{m}(\gamma_{0})(o),\rho_{m}(\gamma_{1})(o)]
at distance
\delta\times\dH(\rho_{m}(\gamma_{0})(o),\rho_{m}(\gamma_{0})(o))
from
\rho_{m}(\gamma_{0})(o)
, and let
y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)=\exoH_{t_{m}}(x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta))
.

Denote, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
the depth-
i
dyadic cube
D_{i}=[0,1]\cap\tfrac{1}{2^{i}}\mathbb{Z}
, and
D_{\infty}=\bigcup_{i}D_{i}=[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Z}[1/2]
.
Define the depth-
i
dyadic subdivision
X_{m}^{k,i}=\{x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)\colon\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in S^{k},\,\delta\in D_{i}\}
of the
1
-skeleton of
C_{m}^{k}
, and its exotic rescale
Y_{m}^{k,i}=\exoH_{t_{m}}(X_{m}^{k,i})\subset\exoH_{t_{m}}(C_{m}^{k})
.
Note that if
k\leq k^{\prime}
and
i\leq i^{\prime}
then
Y_{m}^{k,i}\subset Y_{m}^{k^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
.

The subset
Y_{m}^{k,i}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
has cardinal at most
1+\kappa_{k,i}=(1+2^{i})\times\Card(S^{k})^{2}
so it lies in a totally geodesic subspace of dimension
\kappa_{k,i}
containing
o
.
Since the stabilizer of
o
in
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
acts transitively on flags of totally geodesic subspaces containing
o
of dimension sequence
(\kappa_{n,n})
, we may assume (up to conjugating
\rho^{\prime}_{m}
) that there exists such a flag of subspaces
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{n}})
of dimensions
\kappa_{n,n}
in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
such that for all
m,k,i
we have
Y_{m}^{k,i}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{n,n}}
where
n=\max\{k,i\}
.

Step 4:
Extracting a convergent subsequence of
Y_{m}^{k,i}
as
m,k,i\to\infty
to find
Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

For all
m
we have
Y_{m}^{k,i}\subset B(o,k+\log(2))\cap\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{n,n}}
where
n=\max\{k,i\}
. So by compactness of closed balls of finite dimensional hyperbolic spaces, we may assume (up to extracting a subsequence) that each sequence
y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)\in Y_{m}^{k,i}
converges to some point in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.
Taking a diagonal extraction over
m
, we may assume that for all
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
and
\delta\in D_{\infty}
the sequence
y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)
converges to some
y(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)\in\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
: let
Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty}=\{y(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)\colon\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma,\,\delta\in D_{\infty}\}
.
Denote
T\subset\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
the topological closure of
Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty}
in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

Step 5
: Defining a distance
d
on
T
which is
0
-hyperbolic.

For
y,y^{\prime}\in T
, define
d(y,y^{\prime})=\log\cosh\dH(y,y^{\prime})
.
We compute for
y=\lim y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)
and
y^{\prime}=\lim y_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime})
, using (
24
) and the asymptotic
(\log\cosh x)/x\to 1
as
x\to\infty
, that

\displaystyle d(y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\log\left(\cosh\left(\dH(y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),y_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime}))\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}\log\left(\cosh\left(\dH(x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),x_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime}))\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}\dH(x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),x_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime})).

Since the hyperbolic distance on
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
is Gromov-hyperbolic and
t_{m}\to 0
, we have that
(Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty},d)
is a
0
-hyperbolic metric space. Moreover a Cauchy sequence in
(Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty},d)
must be a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathbf{H}^{\omega},\dH)
which is complete, thus converges to a point in its closure
T
.
Hence
(T,d)
coincides with the completion of
(Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty},d)
.
Moreover, since
Y^{\infty,\infty}_{\infty}
admits approximate midpoints (for any
\varepsilon>0
and
x,y\in Y^{\infty,\infty}_{\infty}
, there is
m\in Y^{\infty,\infty}_{\infty}
such that
d(x,m),d(m,y)\leq d(x,y)/2+\varepsilon
), its completion
(T,d)
is a real tree by
Definition

1.4
.
By construction:

\forall y,y^{\prime}\in T\colon\quad\cosh(\dH(y,y^{\prime}))=e^{d(y,y^{\prime})}

hence the inclusion
T\hookrightarrow\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
corresponds to the embedding
\exoT_{\lambda}
of Theorem
5.11
for
\lambda=e
.

Step 6
: The non-isometric embedding
T\hookrightarrow\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant and
\Gamma
acts isometrically.

For
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
and
\delta\in D_{\infty}
and
\alpha\in\Gamma
, we have
y_{m}(\alpha\gamma_{0},\alpha\gamma_{1};\delta)=\rho_{m}(\alpha)y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta)
.
Thus we obtain an action
\rho_{\omega}
of
\Gamma
on
Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty}
defined by
\rho(\alpha)(y(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta))=y(\alpha\gamma_{0},\alpha\gamma_{1};\delta)
, which is isometric since for all
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in D_{\infty}
and
\alpha\in\Gamma
, the limit as
m\to\infty
of

\dH(\rho_{m}(\alpha)y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),\rho_{m}(\alpha)y_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime}))=\dH(y_{m}(\alpha\gamma_{0},\alpha\gamma_{1};\delta),y_{m}(\alpha\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\alpha\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime}))

yields

\dH\rho(\alpha)y(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),\rho(\alpha)y(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime}))=\dH(y(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),y(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime})).

The isometric action of
\Gamma
on
Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty}
extends uniquely to an isometric action of
\Gamma
on its topological closure
(T,d)
and to the smallest closed totally geodesic subspace containing them which we may still denote
(\mathbf{H}^{\omega},\dH)
.
The embedding
\exoT_{e}\colon(T,d)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

Step 7:
Hausdorff convergence
\exoH_{t_{m}}(\Conv(\rho_{m}(S^{k})\cdot o_{m}))\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}\exoT_{\lambda}(\Conv(\pi_{\lambda}(S^{k})\cdot o))
in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

Let us begin by clarifying the objective and notations.
Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Recall that the convex hull
C_{m}^{k}
of
X_{m}^{k,0}=\rho_{m}(S^{k})\cdot o_{m}
is also the convex hull of its
1
-skeleton, or of its depth-
i
dyadic subdivision
X_{m}^{k,i}
.
Note that for finite
i
, the set
\exoH_{t_{m}}(X_{m}^{k,i})=Y_{m}^{k,i}
pointwise converges as
m\to\infty
to
Y_{\infty}^{k,i}
.
Moreover, in the real tree
T
(the closure of
Y_{\infty}^{\infty,\infty}
), the convex hull of the finitely many points
\pi_{\lambda}(S^{k})\cdot o=Y_{\infty}^{k,0}
is the union of the geodesic segments between them, namely the closure of
Y_{\infty}^{k,\infty}=\{y(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),\ \gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in S^{k},\delta\in D_{\infty}\}
.
That being said, we aim to prove that
\exoH_{t_{m}}(C_{m}^{k})\subset\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
Hausdorff converges to the closure of
Y_{\infty}^{k,\infty}
.

Let us now outline the steps of the strategy.
By triangle inequalities, for all
m,i
we have:

\dH_{H}(\overline{Y_{\infty}^{k,\infty}},\exoH_{t_{m}}(C^{k}_{m}))\leq\dH_{H}(\overline{Y_{\infty}^{k,\infty}},Y_{\infty}^{k,\infty})+\dH_{H}(Y_{\infty}^{k,\infty},Y_{\infty}^{k,i})+\dH_{H}(Y_{\infty}^{k,i},Y_{m}^{k,i})+\dH_{H}(Y_{m}^{k,i},\exoH_{t_{m}}(C^{k}_{m}))

The first term vanishes.
For all finite
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the finite set
Y_{m}^{k,i}
converges (pointwise hence Hausdorff) to
Y_{\infty}^{k,i}
as
m\to\infty
so this deals with the third term.
We must therefore bound the second and fourth terms.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
.
Let
M^{k}=\sup\{t_{m}\operatorname{diam}(C_{m}^{k})\colon m\in\mathbb{N}\}\leq t_{m}2kd_{m}=2k
.
Suppose that
i\in\mathbb{N}
is large enough so that
\exp(M^{k}/2^{i})<\cosh(\varepsilon)
.
For any
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\in\Gamma
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in[0,1]
and any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, applying equation (
24
) and for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
the inequality
(\cosh d)^{t}\leq\exp(td)
, we have:

\cosh\dH(y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),y_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime}))\leq\exp\left(t_{m}\dH(x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),x_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime};\delta^{\prime}))\right).

For
\delta\in[0,1]
we may chose
\delta^{\prime}\in D_{i}
such that
\lvert\delta-\delta^{\prime}\rvert<1/2^{i}
, thus for all
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in S^{k}
we have:

\exp(t_{m}\dH(x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta^{\prime})))\leq\exp(M^{k}/2^{i})

hence
\dH(y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta),y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1};\delta^{\prime}))\leq\cosh^{-1}\exp(M^{k}/2^{i})<\varepsilon
.
Thus
\dH_{H}(Y_{m}^{k,\infty},Y_{m}^{k,i})\leq\varepsilon
.

Next, assume that
i
is at least as large as above.
By Corollary
2.1
, for any
m
the Hausdorff distance between the polytope
C_{m}^{k}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
and its
1
-skeleton is at most
\sinh^{-1}(1)
.
Thus for
i
large enough we have
\dH_{H}(C_{m}^{k},X_{m}^{k,i})<2\sinh^{-1}(1)
.
Now if
m
is large enough so that
(\cosh 2\sinh^{-1}(1))^{t_{m}}\leq\exp(M^{k}/2^{i})
, their images by
\exoH_{t_{m}}
satisfy
\dH_{H}(\exoH_{t_{m}}(C^{k}_{m}),Y_{m}^{k,i})<\varepsilon
.

Finally, observe that one may rewrite Step 7 replacing
S^{k}
by any finite subset
K\subset\Gamma
to prove the claim as anounced in the Theorem.
∎

5.4
Ultraproducts of hyperbolic spaces and actions on real trees

The goal of this subsection is to give a slightly different point of view on the construction of the real
T
inside
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
obtained in
Theorem

5.23
using ultraproducts.
For background on ultrafilters and ultraproducts (or ultralimits), we refer to
[
10
, Chapter I.5]
.
One key point is that an ultraproduct of algebraic hyperbolic spaces is still a algebraic hyperbolic space.
Let
\beta\mathbb{N}
denote the Stone–Čech compactification of
\mathbb{N}
, that is the space of ultrafilters on
\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 5.25

(ultraproduct of hyperbolic spaces)
.

For a sequence of cardinals
(\kappa_{m})
indexed by
\mathbb{N}
and an ultrafilter
\mathfrak{u}\in\beta\mathbb{N}
, there exists a cardinal
\kappa
such that the ultraproduct
X=\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
with distance
d_{\mathfrak{u}}(x,y)=\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}d(x_{,}y_{m})
is isometric to
\mathbf{H}^{\mathbf{\kappa}}
.

When each
\kappa_{m}
is countable and the sequence is not bounded by a finite cardinal, the cardinal
\kappa
is equal to that
\mathfrak{c}=\Card(\mathbb{R})
of the continuum.

Proof.

Let
\dH_{m}
be the distance function on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
. The function
\cosh\circ\dH_{m}
is a kernel of hyperbolic type.
Hence the distance
d_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
X
yields a kernel of hyperbolic type by
\cosh\circ d_{\mathfrak{u}}=\lim\cosh\circ\dH_{m}
, so there exists a minimal cardinal
\kappa
such that
X
is isometric to a subset of
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
Since each
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
is geodesic and each geodesic segment lies in a bi-infinite geodesic,
X
enjoys the same property.
Moreover
X
is complete (this holds for all ultraproducts over non-principal filters
\mathfrak{u}
).
Consequently,
X
is complete and totally geodesic, so it is isometric to
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
∎

Let us now recover
Theorem

5.23
using ultralimits.

Consider a group
\Gamma
generated by a finite
S
, and a sequence of cardinals
\kappa_{m}
and of representations
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}})
with no fixed points at infinity.

By
Lemma

5.22
we may find, a base point
o_{m}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}
realising the minimum of the
\rho_{m}(S)
-displacement function
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\omega}\mapsto\max\{\dH(\rho_{m}(\gamma)(o),o)\colon\gamma\in S\}
, which we abbreviate as
\Delta_{m}=\Delta(\rho_{m}(S))
.

Assume that
\Delta_{m}
is unbounded, and consider an ultrafilter
\mathfrak{u}
on
\mathbb{N}
such that
\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}\Delta_{m}=+\infty
.

The ultraproduct
\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\left(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}},\dH_{m}/\Delta_{m},o_{m}\right)
is a pointed real tree
(T_{\mathfrak{u}},d,o_{\mathfrak{u}})
because it is a complete metric space which is path connected and
0
-hyperbolic (since
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}},d_{m}/\Delta_{m})
is Gromov
\log(2)/\Delta_{m}
-hyperbolic and
\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}\Delta_{m}=+\infty
).
It is endowed with the isometric
\Gamma
-action given by
\rho_{T}=\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\rho_{m}
.

Moreover, applying
Theorem

3.28
with
t_{m}=1/\Delta_{m}
we have exotic embeddings
\exoH_{t_{m}}\colon\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}^{\prime}}
yielding exotically-rescaled representations
\rho_{m}^{\prime}=\chi_{t_{m}}\circ\rho_{m}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}_{m}})
.

The ultraproduct
\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\left(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{m}^{\prime}},\dH_{m}^{\prime},\exoH_{t_{m}}(o_{m})\right)
is a pointed algebraic hyperbolic space
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{\mathfrak{u}}},\dH_{\mathfrak{u}},\exoH_{\mathfrak{u}}(o_{\mathfrak{u}}))
By
Lemma

5.25
.
It is endowed with the isometric diagonal
\Gamma
-action
\rho^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{u}}:=\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\rho_{m}^{\prime}
.

The ultraproduct map
\exoH_{\mathfrak{u}}
induced by the product map
\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\exoH_{t_{m}}
is an embedding of the pointed real tree
(T_{\mathfrak{u}},d,o_{\mathfrak{u}})
into the pointed algebraic hyperbolic space
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{\mathfrak{u}}},\dH_{\mathfrak{u}},\exoH_{\mathfrak{u}}(o_{T}))
which is equivariant with respect to the isometric
\Gamma
-actions
\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\rho_{m}
and
\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\rho_{m}^{\prime}
.

Moreover distances between points in
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
and their images in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{\mathfrak{u}}}
satisfy the following relation: If
x,y\in T_{\mathfrak{u}}
are given by
x=\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}x_{m}
and
y=\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}y_{m}
then
d(x,y)=\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}t_{m}\dH_{m}(x_{m},y_{m})
This implies that
d(x_{m},y_{m})\to\infty
.
Since, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the equality
\cosh(\dH(\exoH_{t_{m}}(x_{m}),\exoH_{t_{m}}(y_{m}))=\cosh(\dH_{m}(x_{m},y_{m}))^{t_{m}}
and because of
\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}t_{m}=0
we have

\cosh\left(\dH_{\mathfrak{u}}(\exoH_{\mathfrak{u}}(x),\exoH_{\mathfrak{u}}(y))\right)=e^{d(x,y)}.

Theorem 5.26

(geometric and ultrafliter limits coincide)
.

Consider a group
\Gamma
generated by a finite
S
, and a sequence of cardinals
\kappa_{m}
and of representations
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
with no fixed points at infinity such that
\Delta(\rho_{m}(S))\to+\infty
.
By
Theorem

5.23
, one can extract from
(\rho_{m})
a limiting action of
\Gamma
by isometries on a real tree
(T,d)
with an equivariant embedding in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
which is
\Gamma
-equivariant for the isometric actions
\rho_{T},\rho_{\omega}
.

There is an ultrafilter
\mathfrak{u}\in\beta\mathbb{N}
such that, up to passing to the unique minimal invariant real subtree, the real trees
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
T
are isometric in a
\Gamma
-equivariant way and up to passing to the unique minimal totally geodesic invariant subspace that contain the embeddings of
T
and
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
, the actions
\rho^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\rho_{\omega}
of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
are conjugated.

Proof.

We explain the isometry between
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
T
.
For
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
and
\delta\in[0,1]
, we denote by
z_{\mathfrak{u}}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\delta)\in T_{\mathfrak{u}}
the unique point on the geodesic segment
[\gamma_{0}o_{\mathfrak{u}},\gamma_{1}o_{\mathfrak{u}}]
at distance
\delta d_{\mathfrak{u}}(\gamma_{0}o_{\mathfrak{u}},\gamma_{1}o_{\mathfrak{u}})
from
\gamma_{0}o_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

We first perform all the successive extractions done in Step 1 of the proof of
Theorem

5.23
and choose an ultrafilter
\mathfrak{u}
on
\mathbb{N}
that contains all the images of these extractions.
Such
\mathfrak{u}
exists by compactness of
\beta\mathbb{N}
. In particular, with the notations of
Theorem

5.23
and
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
dyadic numbers in
[0,1]
, for
y=\lim y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\delta),y^{\prime}=\lim y_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
and
z=z_{\mathfrak{u}}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\delta),z^{\prime}=z_{\mathfrak{u}}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle d_{T}(y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\log\cosh\dH(y_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\delta),y_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}\dH(x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\delta),x_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathfrak{u}}t_{m}\dH(x_{m}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\delta),x_{m}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=d_{T_{\mathfrak{u}}}(z,z^{\prime}).

Thus the map
f_{\mathfrak{u}}
from
T
to the closed convex hull of the orbit of
o_{\mathfrak{u}}
in
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
which sends
y
to
z
is an equivariant isometry.
The real tree
T
is minimal, since it is equal to the closure of the union of the axes of elements in
\Gamma
. Up to replacing
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
by the image of
f_{\mathfrak{u}}
,
T
and
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
are isometric in a
\Gamma
-equivariant way.

Moreover, both trees
T
and
T_{\mathfrak{u}}
are embedded via maps
\exoH\colon T\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
and
\exoH_{\mathfrak{u}}\colon T_{\mathfrak{u}}\to\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
in
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
according to
Theorem

5.11
for the same parameter
\lambda=e
.

Now,
Remark

5.12
shows that the two representations
\rho_{\omega}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{u}}
are conjugated.
∎

6
Equivalence classes of functions of hyperbolic type

Given a topological group
\Gamma
and a cardinal
\kappa\leq\Card(\Gamma)
, we wish to consider the space of continuous representation
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
up to the action of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
at the target, namely inner automorphisms and exotic representations
\rho_{t}\colon\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.

For this purpose, we will consider functions of hyperbolic type up to bounded pertubations (which arise by conjugacy) and powers (which arise from the exotic deformation).

Recall that on a (topological) group
\Gamma
, the space of functions of hyperbolic type
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
is endowed with the pointwise convergence topology.

Definition 6.1

(comparability and homothety)
.

On a group
\Gamma
, we say that
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
are

comparable

when
\log(F_{1}/F_{2})
or equivalently
\cosh^{-1}F_{1}-\cosh^{-1}F_{2}
is bounded.

homothetic

when there exists
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,1]
such that
F_{1}^{t_{1}}
and
F_{2}^{t_{2}}
are comparable.

These define equivalence relations on
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
, the latter coarser than the former, and endow the quotient spaces with the quotient topologies so as to obtain successive continuous quotient maps denoted
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)\to\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
sending
F\mapsto\bar{F}\mapsto\left[{F}\right]
.

Proof.

The comparability relation is of course reflexive and symmetric, and its transitivity follows from the triangular inequality on sup-norms.
The homothety relation coarsens comparability; its reflexivity follows by setting
t_{1}=1=t_{2}
, symmetry follows from that for the comparability relation by inversion
t\mapsto 1/t
, and transitivity follows from that for comparability and the existence quantifier on
t
.
∎

In
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
, the comparability class of the constant function equal to
1
is the set of all bounded functions.
The set of unbounded comparability classes is denoted
\mathcal{C}^{*}(\Gamma)=\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\setminus\bar{\mathbf{1}}
and the corresponding homothety classes is denoted
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\Gamma)=\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\setminus\left[{\mathbf{1}}\right]
.

The space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is not Hausdorff because the class
\left[{\mathbf{1}}\right]
is in the closure of any other class: if
F
is a function of hyperbolic type then
F^{t}\to\mathbf{1}
as
t\to 0
.

6.1
Topology of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
and length functions

The notions of elementarity and neutrality are invariant by comparability (
Corollary

3.25
) and homothety (
Corollary

4.26
).
We denote the corresponding spaces of comparable classes by
\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{C}^{*}(\Gamma)
and of homothety classes by
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\Gamma)
.
The aim of this subsection is to show that the topology on these subspaces is quite well behaved.

Recall that in restriction to the subset of non-neutral functions
\mathcal{F}_{nn}(\Gamma)
, the comparability and homothety relations are characterized by
Corollary

4.24
and
Corollary

4.26
in terms of the length spectrum and the associated minimal representations.

Let
\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
be the product vector space with the product topology, and
\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
be the associated projective space with the quotient topology.
After removing the trivial element
\{0\}
which is its own class, we obtain a space
\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
that is metrizable when
\Gamma
is countable.

Theorem 6.2

(length function on
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group.

The length function
\ell\colon\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
defined by
F\mapsto\left(\ell_{F}(\gamma)\right)_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
quotients to a function
\ell\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
which is continuous and injective in restriction to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
; in particular the sets
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
are Hausdorff and open in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.

Remark 6.3
.

This can be compared with representations of discrete subgroups in Lie groups of rank
1
: the conjugacy classes of irreducible discrete faithful representations some countable groups
\Gamma
form a nice subspace of in their variety of representations into
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
, parametrized by length functions. See for example
[
57
]
for an original result and
[
39
]
for a more comprehensive one.

Proof.

The function
\ell\colon\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
defined by
F\mapsto\left(\ell_{F}(\gamma)\right)_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
is continuous by Lemma
3.26
.
By Corollary
4.26
, this descends to a function
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
defined by
\left[{F}\right]\mapsto[(\ell_{F}(\gamma))_{\gamma}]
, which is continuous and injective.
Since
\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
is open and Hausdorff, so is its preimage
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
.
Let us show that
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
is open in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
by providing an open cover.
For
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
, let
\mathcal{U}(\alpha,\beta)
be the subset of
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
such that

\ell_{F}(\alpha),\ell_{F}(\beta),\ell_{F}(\alpha\beta)>0
and
\ell_{F}(\alpha\beta)\neq\lvert\ell_{F}(\alpha)\pm\ell_{F}(\beta)\rvert
.
This well-defined subset of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is open.
If
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathcal{U}(\alpha,\beta)
then
\ell_{F}
is not the absolute value of a homomorphism on the subgroup generated by
\alpha
and
\beta
, so by
Corollary

2.17
,
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
.
Conversely if
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
then
F
it is not the absolute value of a homomorphism there exist
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
with
\ell_{F}(\alpha),\ell_{F}(\beta),\ell_{F}(\alpha\beta)>0
and
\ell_{F}(\alpha\beta)\neq\lvert\ell_{F}(\alpha)+\ell_{F}(\beta)\rvert
hence
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathcal{U}(\alpha,\beta)
.
∎

For the rest of the section we assume that
\Gamma
is finitely generated: the aim is to prove the next
Theorem

6.4
giving more information about the topology of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
.

Theorem 6.4

(topology of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
for finitely generated
\Gamma
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group.

1.

The space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is compact.

2.

The subspace
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
is a filtering union of compact metrizable subspaces
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;2,\varepsilon)
so the restriction
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;2,\varepsilon)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

3.

The subspace
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
is a filtering union of compact metrizable subspaces
\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;2,\varepsilon)
so the restriction
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;2,\varepsilon)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

Proof.

Lemma

6.5
proves
Item

1
.

Lemma

6.6
and
Lemma

6.7
prove that
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}
and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}
are filtering unions of compact subsets.
Let us explain how to deduce the metrizability and the homeomorphism properties. The restriction of
\mathbf{P}\ell\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
is continuous injective by
theorem

6.2
, hence in restriction to any compact subset, it is a homeomorphism onto its image.
The space
\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}\setminus\{0\}
has a countable basis for its topology (since
\Gamma
is countable), and it is Hausdorff separated, so its compact subsets are metrizable.
∎

Lemma 6.5

(compactness of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
)
.

For a finite generating set
S
of
\Gamma
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we define the subset
\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)=\{F\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)\colon\max F(S)\leq\cosh(\mu)\}
of
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
.

For generating
S\subset\Gamma
and
\mu>0
, the subset
\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
is compact, and the continuous quotient map
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is surjective in restriction to
\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
.
In particular
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is compact.

Proof.

Assume for notational simplicity that the generating set
S
is symmetric
S=S^{\pm 1}
and contains the identity element
1
, so that if
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{S}\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{N}
denotes its associated word-length function, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
S^{n}=\{\gamma\in\Gamma\colon\lvert\gamma\rvert_{S}\leq n\}
.

We will show that the growth of a function of hyperbolic type is bounded above by some absolute function depending only on its values on a generating set.
Fix
F\in\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\cosh\mu_{n}=\max F(S^{n})
.
For
\gamma\in S^{n+1}
written as
\gamma=\alpha\beta
with
\alpha\in S^{1}
and
\beta\in S^{n}
:

\displaystyle\cosh^{-1}(F(\gamma))

\displaystyle\leq\dH(\alpha\beta o,\alpha o)+\dH(\alpha o,o)+\dH(o,\beta o)+\dH(\beta o,o)

\displaystyle\leq\dH(\beta o,o)+\dH(\alpha o,o)+\dH(o,\beta o)+\dH(\beta o,o)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{1}+3\mu_{n}.

Hence
\mu_{n+1}\leq\mu_{1}+3\mu_{n}
, so
\mu_{n}\leq 4^{n}\mu_{1}
so every
\gamma\in\Gamma
satisfies
1\leq F(\gamma)\leq\cosh(4^{\lvert\gamma\rvert_{S}}\mu_{1})
.

Thus for
\mu>0
, the subset of
F\in\mathcal{F}
with
\max F(S)\leq\cosh\mu
is closed and contained in the compact subset
\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}[1,\cosh(4^{\lvert\gamma\rvert_{S}}\mu)]
, so it is a compact subset of
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)
.

Let
\mu>0
.
For
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
one may choose any representative
F
and find
t\in(0,1)
such that
(\max F(S))^{t}\leq\cosh(\mu)
, thus
F^{t}\in\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
.
This proves surjectivity of
\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.
∎

Lemma 6.6

(filtering union of compacta to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}
)
.

For a finite generating
S\subset\Gamma
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we define the subset
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
of
F\in\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
such that
\max\ell_{F}(S)\geq\varepsilon
.

It is compact and contained in
\mathcal{F}_{nn}(\Gamma)
, so it quotients to a compact
\mathbf{P}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{nn}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
.
Moreover for all
\mu>0
, the space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
is the filtering union of the
\mathbf{P}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{nn}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
over increasing finite generating sets
S\subset\Gamma
and decreasing
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

The subset
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
of
\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
is closed hence compact by
Lemma

6.5
.
Moreover by definition
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
is contained in
\mathcal{F}_{nn}(\Gamma)
, hence it quotients by continuity to a compact
\mathbf{P}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{nn}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Let
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
, and choose any representative
F\in\mathcal{F}_{nn}(\Gamma)
.
Since
F
is non-neutral we may find
\alpha\in\Gamma
such that
\ell_{F}(\alpha)>0
.
Choose any finite generating set
S
containing
\alpha
(and one may enlarge to so that it is symmetric and contains the identity).
Let
t\in(0,1]
such that
\max F^{t}(S)\leq\cosh(\mu)
so that
F^{t}\in\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
.
Choose
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
\varepsilon\leq\max\ell_{F^{t}}(S)
.
We found
S,\varepsilon
as desired such that
\left[{F}\right]
admits a representative
F^{t}
in
\mathcal{A}_{nn}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
.
∎

Lemma 6.7

(filtering union of compacta to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}
)
.

For a finite generating
S\subset\Gamma
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we define the subset
\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
of
F\in\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
for which there exists
\alpha,\beta\in S
satisfying
\ell_{F}(\alpha),\ell_{F}(\beta)\geq\varepsilon
and

\ell_{F}(\alpha\beta)\geq\ell_{F}(\alpha)+\ell_{F}(\beta)+\varepsilon
.

It is compact and contained in
\mathcal{F}_{ne}(\Gamma)
, so quotients to a compact subset
\mathbf{P}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{ne}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
.
Moreover for all
\mu>0
, the space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
is the filtering union of the
\mathbf{P}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{ne}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
over increasing finite generating sets
S\subset\Gamma
and decreasing
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

The subset
\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
of
\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
is closed, hence compact by
Lemma

6.5
.
Moreover
\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
is by definition and
Corollary

2.17
contained in
\mathcal{F}_{ne}(\Gamma)
, hence it quotients by continuity to a compact subset
\mathbf{P}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{ne}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)\subset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Fix
\left[{F}\right]\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
, and choose any representative
F\in\mathcal{F}_{ne}(\Gamma)
, with an associated representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.

Since
\rho
is non-elementary, we may find
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
with
\ell_{F}(\alpha),\ell_{F}(\beta)\geq 1
whose
\rho
-fixed points have
L:=2\log\left[{\rho(\beta)^{-},\rho(\alpha)^{+};\rho(\alpha)^{-},\rho(\beta)^{+}}\right]>0
.
Note that the quantity
L
is invariant under replacing
\alpha,\beta
by any of their positive powers, and by
Lemma

1.19
we have as
m,n\to\infty
the limit
\ell_{F}(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})-\ell_{F}(\alpha^{m})-\ell_{F}(\beta^{n})\to L
.
Thus after replacing
\alpha,\beta
by powers, we may assume that
\ell_{F}(\alpha),\ell_{F}(\beta)\geq 1
and for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
we have
\ell_{F}(\alpha^{m}\beta^{n})-\ell_{F}(\alpha^{m})-\ell_{F}(\beta^{n})\geq L/2
.

Choose any finite generating
S\subset\Gamma
containing
\alpha,\beta
.
Let
t\in(0,1]
such that
F^{t}\in\mathcal{B}(S;\mu)
.
We have
\ell_{F^{t}}(\alpha),\ell_{F^{t}}(\beta)\geq t
and

\ell_{F^{t}}(\alpha\beta)-\ell_{F^{t}}(\alpha)-\ell_{F^{t}}(\beta)\geq tL/2
so for
\varepsilon=t\min\{1,L/2\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we have
F^{t}\in\mathcal{A}_{ne}(S;\mu,\varepsilon)
as desired.
∎

Remark 6.8

(not closed)
.

Let us compare the compactness of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
with classical results in finite dimension.

In finite dimension
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for a finitely generated non-virtually abelian group
\Gamma
, the space of discrete and faithful representations
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
is closed by
[
74
, Theorem 3]
. One reason is the Margulis lemma which tells that there is
\varepsilon_{n}>0
such for any such
\rho
, the subgroup generated by
\{\gamma\in\Gamma\colon d(\rho(\gamma)x,x)<\varepsilon\}
is virtually Abelian.

In infinite dimension, the situation is different. There is no Margulis constant for representations
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
when
\kappa
is infinite. Indeed
[
18
, Observation 11.1.4]
shows that for all
\varepsilon>0
, one can find
\alpha,\beta\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
with
\Delta(\{\alpha,\beta\})<\varepsilon
that freely generate a rank-
2
free subgroup
\Gamma\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
(with a fixed point at infinity) that is strongly discrete (for every bounded subset
B\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
the set
\{\gamma\in\Gamma\colon\gamma B\cap B\neq\emptyset\}
is finite, see
[
18
, Definition 5.2.1]
).

6.2
Background on the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology

The goal for the rest of this section is to compare our compact space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
of classes of function of hyperbolic type on a finitely generated group
\Gamma
with previous compactifications of character varieties with respect to the
equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology
formalized by Paulin in
[
61
,
62
,
63
]
, which we first recall.

Fix a group
\Gamma
, and consider a subset
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
of its isometric actions: an element of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
consists of a metric space
(X,d)
together with a homomorphism
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(X,d)
.

A basis of neighborhoods of
((X,d),\rho)\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
is defined by the following subsets
V(P,K,\varepsilon)\subset\mathcal{X}
, indexed by finite
P\subset\Gamma
and compact
K\subset X
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
The subset
V(P,K,\varepsilon)
consists of all
((X^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
such that there exists a compact
K^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
and a closed
P
-equivariant
\varepsilon
-approximation between
K
and
K^{\prime}
.
An
\varepsilon
-approximation
between
K
and
K^{\prime}
is a closed subset
R\subset K\times K^{\prime}
which projects surjectively onto
K
and
K^{\prime}
, such that for all
(x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime})\in R
, we have
\lvert d(x,y)-d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\rvert<\varepsilon
.
It is
P
-equivariant when for all
\gamma\in P
and
(z,z^{\prime})\in R
we have
\rho(\gamma)z\in K\implies(\rho(\gamma)z,\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)z^{\prime})\in R
.

This defines the
equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology
on
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
.
For
i\in\{1,2\}
, the elements
((X_{i},d_{i}),\rho_{i})
of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}

belong to the same class
when there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isometry
X_{1}\to X_{2}
: we denote the topological quotient space
\mathcal{X}
; such a space is called a
Gromov-Hausdorff space
.

Remark 6.9

(Gromov-Hausdorff inseparability)
.

In a topological space, two elements are called
separable
when they admit disjoint open neighborhoods (called distinguishable in
[
62
]
).

Let us give examples of non-isometric actions that are inseparable in any Gromov-Hausdorff space that contains them.

Fixed points:

If a
\Gamma
-action by isometries has a fixed point then it is inseparable from the trivial action on a singleton.
In general, a
\Gamma
-isometric action
((X,d),\rho)
is inseparable from the trivial action on a singleton if and only if for all finite
P\subset\Gamma
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact
K\subset X
of diameter
\max\{d(x,y)\colon x,y\in K\}<\varepsilon
which is
P
-invariant. In particular, if an action of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is parabolic, then it is inseparable from the trivial action on a singleton.

Invariant subspaces:

A
\Gamma
-action by isometries is Gromov-Hausdorff inseparable from its restriction to any
\Gamma
-invariant subspace.
In particular, for two
\Gamma
-actions by isometries
((X_{i},d_{i}),\rho_{i})
, the Cartesian product
\Gamma
-action
\prod_{i}\rho_{i}
of
\Gamma
on
\prod_{i}X_{i}
preserves any product metric (such as the
L^{p}
combination of metrics with
p\in[1,\infty]
).
If
\rho_{0}
has a fixed point then these product actions are all Gromov-Hausdorff inseparable from
\rho_{1}
.

For a Gromov-Hausdorff space
\mathcal{X}(\Gamma)
, we denote
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{X}(\Gamma)
its quotient under the following equivalence relation
((X,d_{X}),\rho_{X})\sim((Y,d_{Y}),\rho_{Y})
when there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection
f\colon X\to Y
and
\lambda>0
such that for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,
d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))=\lambda d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})
.

6.3
Embedding Gromov-Hausdorff spaces in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)

Consider the set
\mathcal{H}(\Gamma)
of all conjugacy classes of
\Gamma
-isometric actions on some algebraic hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
for some cardinal
\kappa
with the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology, and its subset
\mathcal{H}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
of minimal non-neutral actions.
We may wish restrict to actions on an algebraic hyperbolic space of a fixed dimension
\kappa
, and we denote the corresponding space with
\kappa
in exponent
\mathcal{H}^{\kappa}(\Gamma)
.

Remark 6.10

(convex cores)
.

A representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
with no fixed points at infinity admits a unique minimal closed convex
\Gamma
-invariant subspace
X\subset\mathbf{H}^{n}
(called its convex core). In this case, any Gromov-Hausdorff neighborhood of the action of
\Gamma\to\Isom(X,d)
contains the full action
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n},d)
, hence these two actions are inseparable from one another.

When
\Gamma
is countable, it follows from
[
61
, Chapitre 4, Théorème 2.6]
that for
i\in\{1,2\}
two representations
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{\kappa_{i}}(\Gamma)
without fixed points at infinity are Gromov-Hausdorff inseparable if and only if the actions on their associated convex cores are conjugated.
In particular, if
\rho_{i}
are irreducible and
\kappa_{i}\geq 2
, then these actions are Gromov-Hausdorff inseparable if and only if
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
and the representations are conjugated.
Indeed, in the linear models
\mathbb{R}^{1+\kappa_{i}}
, the linear span of the convex cores equal the whole space, and the actions are linear.

Proposition 6.11
.

For a group
\Gamma
, the function

\mathcal{H}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
defined by
\left[{\rho}\right]\mapsto\left[{F_{\rho}}\right]
is continuous and its restriction to
\mathcal{H}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
is injective.

Proof.

Let us first prove continuity of
\mathcal{H}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
at
\rho\in\mathcal{H}^{\kappa}(\Gamma)
.
Fix
o\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
For all
\eta>0
and
F\subset\Gamma
finite, we must construct a finite
P\subset\Gamma
, compact
K\subset\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and real
\varepsilon>0
such that if
\rho^{\prime}\in V_{\rho}(P,K,\varepsilon)
then there exists some cardinal
\kappa^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}}
such that
\forall\gamma\in F\colon\lvert F_{\rho,o}(\gamma)-F_{\rho^{\prime},o^{\prime}}(\gamma)\rvert<\eta
.
Set
P=F
,
K=\rho(P)\cdot o
, and
\varepsilon=\eta/\sinh(M)
where
M=\operatorname{diam}(K)
.
It follows from the definition of the Gromov-Hausdorff neighborhoods that if
\rho^{\prime}\in V_{\rho}(P,K,\varepsilon)
then there exists
o^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa^{\prime}}
such that
\forall\gamma\in P\colon\lvert\dH(\rho(\gamma)o,o)-\dH(\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)o^{\prime},o^{\prime})\rvert<\varepsilon
.
This implies, using the fact that an
\varepsilon
-modulus of continuity for
\cosh
on
[0,M]
is given by
\varepsilon\sinh(M)
, we have that
\forall\gamma\in F\colon\lvert F_{\rho,o}(\gamma)-F_{\rho^{\prime},o^{\prime}}(\gamma)\rvert<\varepsilon\times\sinh(M)=\eta
, as desired.

Now let us show that the restriction
\mathcal{H}_{mnn}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is injective.
Consider for
i=\{1,2\}
minimal non-neutral representations
\rho_{i}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa_{i}})
whose functions of hyperbolic type are homothetic, by
Corollary

4.26
we have
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
, and there is a parameter
t\in(0,1]
for the exotic deformation
\chi_{t}
from
Theorem

3.28
such that
\rho_{1}
conjugated to
\chi_{t}\circ\rho_{2}
or
\rho_{2}
conjugated to
\chi_{t}\circ\rho_{1}
, hence
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
define the same point in
\mathcal{H}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
.
∎

Consider the set
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
of all conjugacy classes of
\Gamma
-isometric actions on complete real trees. An isometric action on a real tree is called
minimal
when there is no closed invariant real sub tree (that is a closed invariant convex subspace in this situation).
The set
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
contains the subset
\mathcal{T}_{m}(\Gamma)
of minimal actions, which contains the subsets
\mathcal{T}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
of those which are, moreover, non-neutral and
\mathcal{T}_{mne}(\Gamma)
of those which are minimal and non-elementary.
We endow all these spaces with the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology.

Remark 6.12

(minimal representative in Gromov-Hausdorff neighbourhood)
.

Any action on a real tree has a minimal invariant closed subtree, unless there is a unique fixed point at infinity and all elements are elliptic.
In particular every non-neutral action on a real tree is Gromov-Hausdorff-inseparable from a minimal non-neutral action on a complete real tree.

All neutral actions on a real tree are Gromov-Hausdorff-inseparable from the trivial action on a point.

Let
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\supset\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{m}(\Gamma),\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mnn}(\Gamma),\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mne}(\Gamma)
be their quotient under the action of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
by homotheties where
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
acts on the class of
((T,d),\rho)\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
by
\lambda\cdot(T,d)=(T,td)
.

Proposition 6.13

(compact metrizable space of actions on trees)
.

If
\Gamma
is finitely generated, the Gromov-Hausdorff space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
is compact metrizable.

Proof.

Under the assumption that
\Gamma
contains a free subgroup on
2
generators, this result was proved for the subspace
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
of classes of isometric actions on real trees with small edge stabilizers by Paulin in
[
61
, Chapitre 2, Théorème 6.2]
.
Since
\Gamma
is assumed to be finitely generated, the topology on
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
is second countable, so it suffices to prove that it is sequentially compact, and we explain below how to adapt Paulin’s proof to our situation.

Let
(T_{n},\rho_{n})
be a sequence of minimal non-neutral
\Gamma
-actions on the complete real tree
T_{n}
.
Since there is no fixed point in
T_{n}
, there are at most
2
fixed point at infinity. Up to extraction, we may assume that for all
n
, there is the same number
\in\{0,1,2\}
of fixed points in
\partial T_{n}
, and we now distinguish those three cases.

If every
\rho_{n}
has no fixed points, then the actions are minimal and irreducible: Paulin’s proof shows that one can extract a limit and that the limit representation has a loxodromic element (the assumption about small stabilizers is only used to ensure that the limiting representation has no fixed point at infinity and to prove that the limit representation has small stabilizers has well. Thus, it is unnecessary for us).

If every
\rho_{n}
has a unique fixed point, then for a finite set of generators, the intersection of their minimal subsets (axis or fixed point set) is non-empty, so we may choose a base point
o_{n}
in this intersection and apply Paulin’s construction to obtain a limit representation with one generator that is hyperbolic, hence non-neutral.

If every
\rho_{n}
has a
2
fixed points, then the minimality assumption implies that the tree
T_{n}
is the real line and the image of
\rho_{n}
consists only of translations (possibly trivial), thus choosing any base point
o_{n}\in T_{n}\simeq\mathbb{R}
, rescaling by the maximum of translations lengths of the generators, and extracting a subsequence such that for any
n
this maximum is realized by the same generator, we obtain a limit representation on
\mathbb{R}
with a generator acting by translation by
\pm 1
.
∎

Remark 6.14

(minimal representatives)
.

Consider a finitely generated group
\Gamma
.
The space
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
of isometric actions on separable real trees has a maximal Hausdorff separated quotient
\overline{\mathcal{T}}(\Gamma)
: denote
\mathbf{P}\overline{\mathcal{T}}(\Gamma)
its set of homothety classes with the quotient topology.
Proposition

6.13
shows that
\mathbf{P}\overline{\mathcal{T}}(\Gamma)
is compact metrizable homeomorphic to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mnn}(\Gamma)\sqcup\{\left[{\bar{1}}\right]\}
where the isolated point
\left[{\bar{1}}\right]
corresponds to the class of the trivial action.
This can be compared with
Remark

6.10
.

Proposition 6.15

(comparing topologies for actions on trees)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group.

The map
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
associating to the class of
((T,d),\rho)
the class of functions of hyperbolic type
\gamma\mapsto\exp{d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)}
for
o\in T
is continuous.

Consequently, if
\Gamma
is finitely generated the restriction
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mnn}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
is a homeomorphism on its image.

Proof.

The map which to an isometric
\Gamma
-action on a tree
((T,d),\rho)\in\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\cdot}(\Gamma)
and a point
o\in T
associates the class of the function of hyperbolic type
\gamma\mapsto F(\gamma)=\exp{d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)}
is independent of
o
and invariant by homothety.
Moreover it is constant across the Gromov-Hausdorff class of
((T,d),\rho)
. The proof of continuity is very similar to the one in the proof of
Proposition

6.11

Fix some
((T,d),\rho)
and
o\in T
. For a finite set
P\subset\Gamma
, we set
K=\rho(P)\cdot o
, and
\varepsilon=\eta e^{-M}
where
M=\operatorname{diam}(K)
.
It follows from the definition of the Gromov-Hausdorff neighborhoods that if
((T^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})\in V_{\rho}(P,K,\varepsilon)
then there exists
o^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}
such that
\lvert d(\rho(\gamma)o,o)-d^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime}(\gamma)o^{\prime},o^{\prime})\rvert<\varepsilon
for all
\gamma\in P
.
This implies, using the fact that an
\varepsilon
-modulus of continuity for the exponential map on
[0,M]
is given by
\varepsilon e^{-M}
, that
\lvert F_{\rho,o}(\gamma)-F_{\rho^{\prime},o^{\prime}}(\gamma)\rvert<\varepsilon\times e^{M}=\eta
for all
\gamma\in P
as desired.

Hence the map
((T,d),\rho)\mapsto F_{\rho}
descends to a map
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.
This map is constant across the Gromov-Hausdorff class of
((T,d),\rho)
and invariant by homothety, so it descends to a continuous map
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.

Now consider its restriction
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mnn}\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.
To show that this map is injective, we show how a limiting isometric action on a tree
T
can be recovered from the cross-ratio at infinity.
By minimality, the tree
T
must be the union of axes of loxodromic elements for
\rho(\Gamma)
hence the function of hyperbolic type determines the cross-ratio on
\partial T
. Consequently, the reconstruction procedure from
\partial T
to
T
via the cross-ratio in
Proposition

6.17
below gives the injectivity.

The map restricted to
