\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
, which is compact and metrizable by
Proposition

6.13
, is continuous and injective so it is a homeomorphism on its image.
∎

Remark 6.16
.

The subspace of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
corresponding to the space of functions of hyperbolic type which are arboreal actions is closed by
Corollary

5.6
.
For finitely generated
\Gamma
, the
Proposition

6.15
and
Remark

6.14
shows that it corresponds to the compact metrizable space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
with the class of the trivial function being isolated.

Moreover, it is known by
[
61
, Théorèmes 5.1 & 5.3]
, that the topology on
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mne}(\Gamma)
is the one given by the projectivized marked length spectrum.

Proposition 6.17

(reconstring tree from cross-ratio)
.

Consider for
i\in\{1,2\}
two (complete) real trees
(T_{i},d_{i})
such that the convex hull of
\partial T_{i}
is
T_{i}
. If
\varphi\colon\partial T_{1}\to\partial T_{2}
is a bijection preserving the boundary cross-ratio then there is an isometry
f\colon T_{1}\to T_{2}
whose boundary extension coincides with
\varphi\colon\partial T_{1}\to T_{2}
.

Moreover when
\Gamma
acts by isometries on the
T_{i}
, if
\varphi
is
\Gamma
-equivariant then
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

Proof.

First, observe that if
\partial T_{i}
consists of less than 4 points, then the trees
T_{i}
are both a line or an infinite tripod, and the result follows. Now assume
\partial T_{i}
has at least
4
points.

In
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]
, the author considers the case of affine buildings of rank
1
(namely simplicial trees with no leaves). Her reconstruction procedure gives the tree
T
as a quotient of the tangent space
\mathfrak{u}(T)
defined as the set of triples
((\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}),t)\in(\partial T)^{(3)}\times\mathbb{R}
of at infinity together with a real parameter
t
. The point
((\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}),t)
corresponds to the point on the oriented geodesics
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
with parameter
t
for the arc-length parametrization with origin at the projection of
\xi_{3}
on
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
. The same construction works for a real tree
T
that is the union of bi-infinite geodesics, or equivalently that is the convex hull of its boundary
\partial T
.

The equivariant statement follows from the construction.
∎

6.4
Recognition of previous compactifications

For a finitely generated torsion-free group
\Gamma
containing a free group of rank
2
and an for integer
n\geq 2
,
following
[
62
]
, let
\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
be the subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\Gamma)
corresponding to faithful and discrete representations
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
: it is endowed the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology, and let
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
be its quotient by the homothety relation with the quotient topology.
Note that for
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, if
n_{1}<n_{2}
then
\mathcal{H}^{n_{1}}_{fd}\subset\mathcal{H}^{n_{2}}_{fd}
.
The quotient map
\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
is an homeomorphism.

Recall that the Gromov-Hausdorff space of minimal non-elementary actions on trees
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}_{mne}(\Gamma)
contains the subspace
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
of those having small stabilizers.
Note that
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is compact.
Paulin proved in
[
62
, Théorème 6.4]
that the space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is compact.
Thus
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
contains the
equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff compactification of the space of faithful and discrete representations of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{H}^{n}
.

We will show that this compactification also holds in the corresponding spaces of homothety classes of functions of hyperbolic type.
For this, denote
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
the space of homothety classes of functions of hyperbolic type associated to faithful and discrete actions of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{H}^{n}
, namely the image of
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{H}_{mnn}(\Gamma)
by the (injective and continuous) function
\mathcal{H}_{mnn}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
defined by
\rho\mapsto\left[{F_{\rho}}\right]
obtained in
Proposition

6.11
.

Theorem 6.18

(recovering compactification of faithful and discrete)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated torsion-free group containing a free group of rank
2
.
The Gromov-Hausdorff space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is homeomorphic to its image
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

6.11
we have a continuous injective function
\mathcal{H}_{mnn}^{n}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
given by
\rho\mapsto\left[{F_{\rho}}\right]
.
Its restriction to
\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
and then quotients to a function
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{mnn}^{n}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
which by
Corollary

4.26
is still injective.
Its image denoted
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
consists of the homothety classes of functions of hyperbolic type associated to faithful and discrete actions of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{H}^{n}
.
We thus obtain a continuous and bijective function
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
.

By Lemmas
6.11
and
6.15
, the well-defined map
\Psi\colon\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}(\Gamma)
is continuous in restriction to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
and to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
, and we wish to show that
\Psi
is continuous in restriction to
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
. Since
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is compact and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\cup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is metrizable, it remains to show that if a sequence of
\alpha_{m}\in\mathcal{H}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
has homothety classes converging to some
T_{1}\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
, then the same is true for the associated equivalence classes of functions of hyperbolic type. But this follows from
Theorem

5.11
: it actually constructs a real tree
T_{2}
with a point, and a sequence of functions of hyperbolic type
F_{\rho_{m}}
equivalent those associated to
\alpha_{m}
such that
F_{\rho_{m}}
converges to the function of hyperbolic associated to the action on
T_{2}
.
By continuity of the length spectrum for the equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff topology
[
63
, Theorem 4.2]
, the homothety classes of the length spectrum for
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the same and thus by
Corollary

4.26
the classes of functions of hyperbolic type are the same. So the map
\Psi
is continuous. It is injective by rigidity of the length spectrum for actions on
\mathbf{H}^{n}
and on real trees
[
63
, Remark 4.6]
. Since
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is compact and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{nn}(\Gamma)
is Hausdorff,
\Psi
is a homeomorphism on its image.
∎

Remark 6.19

(recovering previous compactifications)
.

It follows from
Theorem

6.18
, that the compactification of the character variety
\mathcal{H}_{fd}^{n}(\Gamma)
in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
is homeomorphic to the one obtained by Culler, Morgan, Otal and Shalen in various works
[
50
,
51
,
17
,
49
]
.
Hence, our compactification encompasses these compactifications for all values of
n
.

Remark 6.20

(fundamental groups of hyperbolic manifolds)
.

When
\Gamma
is the fundamental group of a closed surface of genus
\geq 2
, it follows from
[
71
, Theorem 3.2]
that for all
n\geq 2
, the closure of
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
in this compactification is all of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
, and this coincides with Thurston’s compactification of the Teichmüller space by measured laminations.

Example 6.21

(small actions trees not in the closure)
.

There are finitely generated groups
\Gamma
for which the inclusion
\bigcup_{n}\overline{\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)}\subset\bigcup_{n}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is strict.

In fact, we can find such groups for which the set of faithful (maybe non-discrete) classes of actions
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)
is empty whereas
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is not.

For this, consider two finitely generated groups
A,B
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
they do not admit a faithful action on
\mathbf{H}^{n}
(for instance any non-
\mathbb{R}
-linear group, or any group having property-
T
such as
\SL_{k}(\mathbb{Z})
for
k\geq 3
).
The amalgam
\Gamma=A\star B
also has
\bigcup_{n}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
, but it acts on its Bass-Serre tree with trivial (hence small) edge stabilizers so
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset
.

Remark 6.22

(when
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}\subsetneq\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}
)
.

Note that
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is closed in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma
, so any point in the difference
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\setminus\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)
is isolated from the compactification
\bigcup_{n}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}_{fd}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}_{n}(\Gamma)
.

Examples of
\Gamma
for which
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)\setminus\mathbf{P}\mathcal{TS}(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset
include free groups on
\geq 2
generators and automorphism groups of simplicial trees that are
k
-regular with valency
k\geq 3
.

The following example suggests that
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
can be much larger than
\bigcup_{n}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}^{n}(\Gamma)\sqcup\mathbf{P}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
.

Example 6.23

(bendings)
.

Let
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
be the fundamental group of a closed orientable surface of genus
\geq 2
. In
[
75
, Theorem 1.3]
, Xu exhibits a deformation space of representations of
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
of infinite dimension (parametrized by some infinite dimensional Lie group).
These representations are not arboreal and we expect that most of them have critical exponent
1
(namely they are not exotic deformations of representations into some
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{n})
for finite
n\in\mathbb{N}
, nor limits of such (since this would yield an arboreal one)).

In short, these representations are obtained from an initial representation
\rho
by the following bending procedure.

First, consider a simple closed curve
\gamma\subset\Sigma
separating the surface
\Sigma
to obtain (by the Van Kampen theorem) a presentation of its fundamental group as an amalgam
\Gamma=A*{Z}B
where
A,B
are the fundamental groups of the holed surfaces with genus
\geq 1
on each side of
\gamma
.
Choose a representation
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
which is an exotic deformation of a representation
\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{2})
obtained from a hyperbolic metric on
\Sigma
.

[
75
, Proposition 4.11]
shows that
\rho(A)
is irreducible.

Next, for
g\in\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
centralizing
\rho(\mathbb{Z})
, define the bending
\rho^{g}_{A}\colon\Gamma\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
by
g
-conjugating the values on
\beta\in B
as
\rho^{g}_{A}(\alpha)=g\rho(\beta)g^{-1}
while unchanging the values of
\alpha\in A
as
\rho^{g}_{A}(\alpha)=\rho(\alpha)
.
For all
g
the representation
\rho^{g}_{A}
remains non-elementary, and choosing a path of such elements from
g
to the identity (for instance varying in a one-parameter subgroup) we obtain a continuous path of non-elementary representations from
\rho
to
\rho^{g}_{A}
.

Finally, if
\ell(g_{1})\neq\ell(g_{2})
then the bendings
\rho_{g_{1}}
and
\rho_{g_{2}}
yield distinct points in
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
, as we now explain.
On the one hand, by
[
75
, Proposition 4.19]

\rho_{A}^{g_{1}}
and
\rho_{A}^{g_{2}}
cannot be conjugated since
g_{1}\neq g_{2}
.
On the other hand, the
\rho_{A}^{g_{i}}
are non-elementary and coincide on
A
which contains a loxodromic element, so if two such representations yield functions of hyperbolic type that are homothetic, then the homothety factor
t>0
must be
1
, hence both representations must have the same the length spectrum, so by Proposition
4.23
they are conjugated, contradicting the previous observation.

7
Global rigidity for certain group actions on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}

In this section, we apply the unicity of abstract cross-ratio (up to powers) obtained in
Section

4.1
to prove that for some groups, the space of homothety classes of non-elementary functions of hyperbolic type
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(\Gamma)
is reduced to a point, namely that there is a unique non-elementary representation in some
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
up to conjugacy and exotic deformations.

7.1
Boundedness properties of groups

To deal with the groups we have in mind, we will first need to extend our scope beyond locally compact groups and rely on the coarse geometry of topological groups developed by Rosendal
[
67
]
.
We briefly recall the few notions we use and refer to the previous reference for a general treatment or to
[
68
]
for a shorter introduction.

The main idea is to have a well-defined notion of boundedness to extend geometric group theory outside the class of locally compact groups. Following Roe, Rosendal introduces coarse structures
\mathcal{E}
on groups in
[
67
, Definition 2.2]
.
For a topological group
G
, we will only use two such coarse structures: the left coarse structure
\mathcal{E}_{L}
, and the coarse structure
\mathcal{E}_{d}
coming from a continuous isometric group action of
G
on
(X,d)
. Actually, we will only need and recall the notions of bounded subsets relative to these coarse structures.

Definition 7.1

(
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subsets)
.

Let
G
be topological group.
Consider a continuous isometric action on a metric space
(X,d)
.
A subset
B\subset G
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-
bounded
when for any
x\in X
we have
\sup\{d(gx,hx)\colon g,h\in B\}<\infty
.

Proof.

The triangle inequality shows that the condition is independent of
x
(justifying the use of “any”), and amounts to saying that
B
has bounded image under any orbit map
g\mapsto gx
.
∎

Remark 7.2

(inverse and products)
.

The collection of
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subsets in
G
is stable under inverse and finite products: if
A,B\subset G
are
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded then
A^{-1}
and
AB
are
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded.

Definition 7.3

(bornologous and coarsely proper actions)
.

Let
G
be a subgroup of some
\Isom(X,d)
where
(X,d)
is metric space. An action of
G
on another metric space
(X^{\prime},d^{\prime})
by continuous isometries is:

\mathcal{E}_{d}
-
bornologuous

when every
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset is
\mathcal{E}_{d^{\prime}}
-bounded.

\mathcal{E}_{d}
-
coarsely proper

when every
\mathcal{E}_{d^{\prime}}
-bounded subset is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded.

Definition 7.4

(coarsely bounded subset)
.

In a topological group
G
, a subset
B
is
coarsely bounded
when it is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded for every continuous isometric action of
G
on a metric space
(X,d)
.
In particular, the topological group
G
itself is coarsely bounded when every continuous isometric action on a metric space has bounded orbits.

Remark 7.5

(groups with an action witnessing coarse boundedness)
.

For some topological groups
G
, there is a faithful isometric action on a metric space
(X,d)
so that coarsely bounded subsets coincide with
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subsets.
In this case, by definition of coarse boundedness, every continuous isometric action of
G
on a metric space will be
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous.
This holds for the action of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
on its symmetric space
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
as we will see in
Proposition

7.10
.

Example 7.6

(second countable locally compact groups)
.

A second countable locally compact group has a left invariant proper metric
d
, so its coarsely bounded subsets are precisely its
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subsets and correspond to its relatively compact subsets.

Definition 7.7

(locally bounded group)
.

A topological group
G
is
locally bounded
when it has a neighborhood of the identity that is coarsely bounded.
This encompasses local compactness.

Definition 7.8

(cobounded action)
.

For a metric space
(X,d)
and a subgroup
G\subset\Isom(X,d)
, the action of
G
on
X
is
cobounded
when there exists
o\in X
and
M\geq 0
such that for every
x\in X
we have
d(x,Go)\leq M
.

Remark 7.9

(quasi-isometric)
.

Let us note that when an action of
G
on a geodesic space
X
is both coarsely proper and cobounded, the spaces
G
and
X
are
quasi-isometric
in the terminology of Rosendal (see
[
67
, Theorem 2.57]
).

Our first example for this coarse geometry is given by
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and its action on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. The following solves in particular
[
67
, Problem A.11]
.

Proposition 7.10

(bounded properties of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
)
.

For a countable cardinal
\kappa
, the Polish group
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is generated by a coarsely bounded neighborhood of the identity and it acts on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
coarsely properly coboundedly.
In particular, a subset of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is coarsely bounded if and only if it is
\mathcal{E}_{\dH}
-bounded for its action on
(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa},\dH)
.

Proof.

Recall that
G=\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})=\operatorname{PO}(1,\kappa)
contains the subgroup
K=\operatorname{PO}(\kappa)
, and acts by left multiplication on the homogeneous space
X=\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}=G/K
: the stabilizer of
o=\operatorname{1}K
is the group
K
which is coarsely bounded in
G
(when
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
it is compact and when
\kappa=\infty
is countably infinite one may find this in
[
65
, §6]
or
[
23
, §3]
for details).

The Cartan decomposition
G=KA(\mathbb{R})K
(where
A\colon\mathbb{R}\to G
is a one parameter hyperbolic group of transformations
A(t)
acting by translations of
\lvert\ell(A(t))\rvert=t
along the common axis) shows that
G
is generated by
KA([0,1])K
.
The subset
A([0,1])
is coarsely bounded since it is compact.
This proves that
G
is generated by a coarsely bounded neighborhood of the identity
K\cdot A([0,1])\cdot K
, in particular it is locally bounded.
Moreover this shows that a subset
B\subset G
is coarsely bounded if and only if there is
R\geq 0
such that
B\subset K\cdot A([0,R])\cdot K
.

The action of
G
on
X=G/K
is transitive (hence cobounded) and the orbit map of any point is isometrically conjugate to that of the point
o
.
Since
K
is coarsely bounded, a subset of
X=G/K
is bounded if and only if its preimage in
G
by the orbit map of
o
is coarsely bounded, hence the action is coarsely proper.
∎

7.2
Rigidity of groups acting fully on
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
spaces

The main
Theorem

7.14
in this section is a general rigidity result: certain groups can admit at most one continuous isometric action on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
up to conjugacy and exotic deformations.

Definition 7.11

(
3
-full actions on
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
spaces)
.

Consider a topological group
G
acting on a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-space
(X,d)
continuously by isometries.

We say that the action of
G
on
(X,d)
is
3
-full
when it is non-elementary and such that:

Boundary action is
3
-transitive
:

the action of
G
on
\partial X
is
3
-transitive,

Boundary stabilizers are amenable
:

for every
\xi\in\partial X
, its stabilizer
G_{\xi}
is amenable,

Coarse Cartan decompositions of rank-
1
:

for every loxodromic
a\in G
, there is a
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset
B\subset G
such that
G=BAB
where
A
is the subgroup generated by
a
.

Remark 7.12
.

The transitivity of the
G
action on
\partial X
implies that for all
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\partial X
the groups
G_{\xi}
and
G_{\xi^{\prime}}
are conjugated in
G
, thus every
G_{\xi}
contains an element acting loxodromically on
X
(since otherwise none of them would, contradicting non-elementarity of the action).

Moreover, the
3
-transitivity of the
G
action on
\partial X
implies that for all
\xi\in\partial X
the action of
G_{\xi}
on
\partial X\setminus\{\xi\}
is transitive, so for any triple of distinct points
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\partial X
we have
G=G_{\xi_{1}}G_{\xi_{2}}\cup G_{\xi_{3}}G_{\xi_{2}}
.

Remark 7.13

(geometry of
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-spaces)
.

Let us briefly recall some properties that we will use about a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-space
(X,d)
.

As a strongly-
1
-hyperbolic space, its geometry satisfies all results covered in the previous sections.
In particular, its cross-ratio generates the topology in the sense of
Definition

4.1
.

For every pair of distinct boundary points
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\partial X
, there is a unique geodesic connecting them
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})=\{p\in X\colon(\xi,\xi^{\prime})_{p}=0\}
, and any two points
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in X\sqcup\partial X
admit a unique projections
p,p^{\prime}\in(\xi^{\prime},\xi)
, and those satisfy
\lvert\log\left[{\xi,\eta,\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}\right]\rvert=d(p,p^{\prime})
(see
[
9
, §1]
).

Theorem 7.14

(rigidity of groups acting fully on
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
spaces)
.

Consider a topological group
G
acting
3
-fully on a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-space
(X,d)
.

If every non-elementary continuous action of
G
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous, then
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(G)
has at most one point.

The strategy of the proof is to find a
G
-equivariant boundary map
\partial X\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
that is a homeomorphism on its image and pull back the cross-ratio from
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
to
\partial X
.
We may then conclude using the unicity (up to taking powers) of the cross-ratio proved in
Corollary

4.7
, and the fact that the cross-ratio determines the length spectrum of the representation
Lemma

1.18
, hence the conjugacy class of the representation by
Corollary

4.26
.

Lemma 7.15

(boundary map)
.

Consider a topological group
G
acting
3
-fully on a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
-space
(X,d)
as in
Definition

7.11
.

Consider a non-elementary
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous continuous representation
\rho\colon G\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.
For every
\xi\in\partial X
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
fixes a unique point
\varphi(\xi)\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, and for every loxodromic
a\in G
with
a^{+}=\xi
its image
\rho(a)
is hyperbolic with
\rho(a)^{+}=\varphi(\xi)
.

Moreover, the action of
G
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-coarsely proper.

Proof.

The continuous actions of the amenable groups
\rho(G_{\xi})
(which are all conjugate) on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
must, by
[
23
, Proposition 5.1]
, simultaneously fall into (at least) one of the following cases:

1.

\forall\xi\in\partial X
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
fixes a point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
,

2.

\forall\xi\in\partial X
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
stabilizes a geodesic line,

3.

\forall\xi\in\partial X
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
fixes a single point in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}

and we will show that neither of the cases 1 nor 2 can occur, so that only case 3 occurs.

Suppose that case 1 occurs: for any triple of distinct points
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\partial X
the group
\rho(G_{\xi_{i}})
has a fixed point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, so
G=G_{\xi_{1}}G_{\xi_{2}}\cup G_{\xi_{3}}G_{\xi_{2}}
has bounded orbits (by the triangle inequality), hence a fixed point, contradicting non-elementarity.

Suppose now that only case 2 occurs: for every
\xi\in\partial X
the action of
\rho(G_{\xi})
does not fix a point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
but stabilizes a geodesic line
L
.
We may choose
a\in G_{\xi}
which is loxodromic for the action on
X
with
a^{+}=\xi
, and denote
\xi^{\prime}=a^{-}
. Since
\rho(a)
preserves
L
, it is either loxodromic or elliptic. If it were elliptic, then by the property that
G=BAB
for some
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset
B\subset G
and
A
the group generated by
a
, the image
\rho(G)
would have a bounded orbit, contradicting non-elementarity of its action on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
Thus
\rho(a)
is loxodromic and since
a\in G_{\xi}\cap G_{\xi^{\prime}}
, the geodesic line
L
invariant by
\rho(G_{\xi^{\prime}})
is the axis of
\rho(a)
. Finally, since
G_{\xi}
acts transitively on
\partial X\setminus\{\xi\}
while fixing
L
, for every
\eta\in\partial X
the group
\rho(G_{\eta})
stabilizes
L
, thus
L
is
G
-invariant, contradicting non-elementarity.

Now we know that every
\rho(G_{\xi})
fix a single point in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.

Consider a loxodromic
a\in G_{\xi}
with
a^{+}=\xi
, and denote
a^{-}=\xi^{\prime}\in\partial X
its repulsive fixed point. The same reasoning as before shows that
\rho(a)
cannot be elliptic. If
\rho(a)
is parabolic, then it has unique fixed point
\zeta\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, hence
G_{\xi^{\prime}}
also fixes
\zeta
, and as before the transitivity of
G_{\xi}
on
\partial X\setminus\{\xi\}
shows that for
\eta\in\partial X
the group
\rho(G_{\eta})
fixes
\zeta
, hence
\zeta
is fixed by
\rho(G)
, contradicting non-elementarity. Thus
\rho(a)
must be loxodromic.
Let us now show that the fixed point of
\rho(G_{\xi})
is
\rho(a)^{+}
and not
\rho(a)^{-}
.
Choose a point
x\in X
on the axis of
a
and
b\in G_{\xi}\setminus G_{\xi^{\prime}}
.
A comparison inequality in the triangle
(x,bx,\xi)
shows that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
d(a^{-n}ba^{n}x,x)=d(b(a^{n}x),a^{n}x)\leq d(bx,x)
. In particular, there is a
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset
B\subset G
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
a^{-n}ba^{n}\in B
. However
d(a^{n}ba^{-n}x,x)=d(b(a^{-n}x),a^{-n}x)\to\infty
since
\xi^{\prime}\neq b\xi^{\prime}
. The same inequalities hold for
\rho(a)
and
\rho(b)
and their axes and fixed points: since the representation is bornologous, we deduce that
\rho(a)^{+}
is the fixed point of
\rho(G_{\xi})
.

To show that the action is coarsely proper, consider a loxodromic
a
, and let
G=BAB
the coarse Cartan decomposition given by
Coarse Cartan decompositions of rank-

1

:
.
We know from the previous that
\rho(a)
is loxodromic: choose a point
y\in\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
on its axis. The supremum
M=\sup\{\dH(\rho(b)y,y)\colon b\in B\}
is finite since
B
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded and the action of
G
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous. For an element
g=b_{1}a^{n}b_{2}\in BAB
, two triangle inequalities show that
\dH(\rho(g)y,y)\geq\lvert n\rvert\ell(\rho(a))-2M
.
Two more triangular inequalities show that for every bounded set
Y
, a similar inequality holds for all
y\in Y
, hence the action of
G
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-coarsely proper.
∎

The following lemma will serve to deduce bicontinuity of the boundary map
\varphi
in
Lemma

7.15
from its (un)boundedness.

Lemma 7.16
.

Consider a topological group
G
acting continuously by isometries on a
\operatorname{CAT}(-1)
space
(Y,d)
, triply transitively on
\partial Y
. Let
a\in G
be a loxodromic element. Consider a sequence of boundary points
\xi_{n}\in\partial Y\setminus\{a^{\pm}\}
.
Denote
A_{n}(\xi)=\{g\in G_{a^{-}}\cap G_{a^{+}}\colon g(\xi_{0})=\xi_{n}\}
.

We have
\xi_{n}\to a^{+}
if and only if both of the following hold:

•

the sets
A_{n}(\xi)
leave any
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded set in
G
as
n\to\pm\infty

•

there is an
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded set
B\subset G
such that for
\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\,\exists k_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\colon a^{-k_{n}}A_{n}(\xi)\subset B
.

Proof.

Recall the definition and properties of the unique projection
p_{n}
of
\xi_{n}
on
(a^{-},a^{+})
. We have
\xi_{n}\to a_{+}\iff p_{n}\to a_{+}
. Moreover every
g\in A_{n}(\xi)
satisfies
g(p_{0})=p_{n}
.

If
\xi_{n}\to a^{+}
then for any
g_{n}\in A_{n}(\xi)
we have
d(g_{n}p_{0},p_{0})\to\infty
so for any
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset
B\subset G
and for
n
large enough,
A_{n}(\xi)\cap B=\emptyset
. Moreover, for any
n
, we may find
k_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a^{-k_{n}}p_{n}\in B(p_{0},\ell(a))
and thus
a^{-k_{n}}A_{n}(\xi)
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded.

Conversely if
A_{n}(\xi)
escapes any
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset of
G
then
d(p_{0},p_{n})\to\infty
so up to extraction
p_{n}
converges to
a^{\pm}
, but since the distance from
\{a^{-k_{n}}p_{n}\colon k\in\mathbb{N}\}
to
p_{0}
is bounded the limit must actually be
a^{+}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.14
.

Consider a non-elementary continuous representation
\rho\colon G\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.
By
Lemma

7.15
, we have a boundary function
\varphi\colon\partial X\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
sending
\xi\in\partial X
to the unique fixed point of
\rho(G_{\xi})
.
Let us show that
\varphi
is a homeomorphism on its image.

Since the topology on
\partial X
and
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
are metrizable, we will prove that a sequence
\xi_{n}\in\partial X
converges if and only if
\varphi(\xi_{n})\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
converges.
Fix distinct
\xi^{\pm}\in\partial X
and choose a loxodromic
a\in G
such that
a^{\pm}=\xi^{\pm}
.
We now apply
Lemma

7.16
with
Y=X
: a sequence
(\xi_{n})
in
\partial X\setminus\{\xi^{+}\}
converges to
\xi^{+}
if and only the subsets
A_{n}(\xi)=\{g\in G_{a^{+}}\cap G_{a^{-}}\colon g(\xi_{0})=g(\xi_{n})\}
leave any
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset of
G
and there is a
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset
B\subset G
such that
\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\exists k_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\colon a^{-k_{n}}A_{n}(\xi)\subset B
.
Since the action of
G
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-coarsely proper (by
Lemma

7.15
) and
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous, this is equivalent to saying that the image under
\rho
of

A_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)=\{g\in G\colon\rho(g)(\varphi(\xi^{\pm}))=\varphi(\xi^{\pm})\textrm{ and }\rho(g)(\varphi(\xi_{0}))=\rho(g)(\varphi(\xi_{n}))\}

escapes as
n\to+\infty
from any
\mathcal{E}_{\dH}
-bounded subset of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and there is a
\mathcal{E}_{\dH}
-bounded subset
B\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is
k_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\rho(a)^{-k_{n}}A_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)\subset B
.

By
Lemma

7.16
for
Y=\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
this is equivalent to the convergence of
\varphi(\xi_{n})
to
\varphi(\xi^{+})
.
The proves that
\varphi\colon\partial X\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is a homeomorphism on its image.

To complete the proof consider for
i\in\{1,2\}
two non-elementary representations
\rho_{i}\colon G\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
with boundary maps
\varphi_{i}\colon\partial X\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. By
Corollary

4.7
there is
t>0
such that for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}\in(\partial X)^{(4)}
,

\left[{\varphi_{1}(\xi_{1}),\varphi_{1}(\xi_{2});\varphi_{1}(\xi_{3}),\varphi_{1}(\xi_{4})}\right]=\left[{\varphi_{2}(\xi_{1}),\varphi_{2}(\xi_{2});\varphi_{2}(\xi_{3}),\varphi_{2}(\xi_{4})}\right]^{t}

In particular, up to composing
\rho_{1}
with the exotic representation
\rho_{t}
or
\rho_{2}
with
\rho_{1/t}
(depending on whether
t\leq 1
or
t>1
), we may reduce to the case
t=1
and deduce by
Corollary

4.20
that the restriction of the
\rho_{i}
to their minimal invariant totally geodesic subspaces are conjugated, hence their associated functions of hyperbolic type belong to the same class.
∎

7.3
Rigidity for
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}),\Aut(T_{\kappa}),\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})

Corollary 7.17

(rigidity of classical groups)
.

If
G
is one the following topological groups:

1.

the isometry group
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
of the real hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
of countable dimension
\kappa
,

2.

the isometry group
\Aut(T_{\kappa})
of the
\kappa
-regular simplicial tree
T_{\kappa}
where
\kappa\geq 3
is countable,

3.

the group
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
where
\mathbb{K}
a complete non-Archimedean valued field

then the space
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(G)
has exactly one point.

Remark 7.18

(recovering previous results)
.

Corollary

7.17
recovers (with a different proof) the following cases.
The case of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
(in particular of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
for
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
) and of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
are treated respectively in
[
46
]
and
[
47
]
.
The case of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
where
\mathbb{K}
is a non-Archimedean local field is treated in
[
12
, Theorem C]
.

Remark 7.19

(new examples for
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
)
.

In

Corollary

7.17
, the case of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is new when the field
\mathbb{K}
is nonlocal, so it provides in particular the following sources of new examples.

We emphasize that when
\mathbb{K}
is Polish, the group
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is Polish for the topology induced by the uniform topology (as we will see in
Section

7.3.3
).

•

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}_{p}
, the completion of the algebraic closure of a
p
-adic field
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, which is Polish.

•

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{k}((t))
, the field of Laurent series over any field
\mathbb{k}
, which is Polish if
\mathbb{k}
is countable.

•

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{k}((t^{\Lambda}))
, the field of Hahn series over a field
\mathbb{k}
with exponents in a subgroup
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
(the previous case being
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}
), which is Polish if
\mathbb{k}
is countable and
\Lambda
is discrete.

Remark 7.20

(from
\Aut(T_{k})
to Burger-Mozes groups)
.

The case of the automorphism group
\Aut(T_{k})
of the regular tree with infinite countable valency should be extendable to the following.

Consider the infinite regular tree
T{N}
whose vertex links are in bijection with
\mathbb{N}
.
The group of all permutations of a countable set
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{N})
is a non-Archimedean Polish group.
To a closed subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{N})
is associated a universal Burger-Mozes groups
U(\Gamma)
consisting of those automorphisms of
T{N}
whose local actions on vertex links belongs to
\Gamma
.

If the action of
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{N}
is
3
-transitive then the same is true for the action of
U(\Gamma)
on
\partial T{N}
.
If the action of
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{N}
is oligomorphic then, by the argument in the proof of
[
21
, Proposition 5.2]
, the action
U(\Gamma)
on
T{N}
has coarsely bounded vertex stabilizers. The coarse Cartan decompositions in
Coarse Cartan decompositions of rank-

1

:
follows from this coarse boundedness of vertex stabilizers and the fact that the triple transitivity at infinity implies that the stabilizer of a vertex acts transitively on
\partial T_{\omega}
.
Under these assumptions, the only remaining condition to check in
Boundary stabilizers are amenable:
is the amenability of stabilizers of points at infinity: it is less straightforward, but may follow from additional properties for the action of
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{N}
such as amenability of its point stabilizers.
When it holds, we may apply our
Theorem

7.14
to encompass such new examples, including the homeomorphism groups of the Basilica Julia set and other rabbits Julia sets
[
21
]
.

Remark 7.21

(dependence on the topology)
.

Corollary

7.17
is stated for topological groups.

In some cases, the continuity of the representation is not a restriction. For example, the group
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\omega})
has the automatic continuity property, so that when
\kappa
is countable any group representation in
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is automatically continuous (see
[
47
, Theorem III]
for these specific representations and
[
23
, Theorem 1.4]
for the general automatic continuity property).

For other groups, the topology is a key ingredient.
The field
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}_{p}
are algebraically isomorphic (being the unique algebraically closed field with characteristic zero and continuum cardinality) but not homeomorphic since
\mathbb{C}
is locally compact and Archimedean whereas
\mathbb{C}_{p}
is not locally compact and non-Archimedean.
Hence the groups
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C}_{p})
are algebraically isomorphic but not homeomorphic.

Corollary

7.17
shows that both topological groups have a unique homothety class of non-elementary functions of hyperbolic type. However their associated representations are different.
Indeed for
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
the restrictions of these representations to
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
have unbounded orbits whereas for
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C}_{p})
the restrictions to
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
are bounded.

Remark 7.22

(Gelfand pairs)
.

The proof of
[
12
, Theorem C]
relies on Gelfand pairs
(G,K)
, which are well defined only when
G
is locally compact (so that it has a Haar measure).

The notion of Gelfand pair has been extended to topological groups acting by isometries by Rosendal in
[
66
]
. One can check that the hypotheses and in particular the triple transitivity at infinity in
Theorem

7.14
implies that the action of
G
on
X
yields a geometric Gelfand pair according to the terminology of Rosendal. Our proof does not use this notion.

In the remaining subsections, we prove
Corollary

7.17
case by case. Observe that the non-emptiness of
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{ne}(G)
follows, in each case, from the existence of a non-elementary action on some
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
or on some real tree.

7.3.1
The case of
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})

Proof of
Corollary

7.17
for
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
with its Polish topology.

We check the conditions in
Definition

7.11
and
Theorem

7.14
for
G=\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
and
X=\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
with
\kappa
countable.

We saw that
G
acts triply transitively on
\partial X
in
2.1
.
The stabilizer of a point at infinity is amenable by
[
23
, Lemma 5.4]
.
The coarse Cartan decompositions of rank-
1
in
Coarse Cartan decompositions of rank-

1

:
follow from the Cartan decompositions of
\operatorname{PO}(1,\infty)
recalled in
2.1
and the transitivity of the action of
\operatorname{PO}(1,\infty)
on
\mathbf{H}^{\omega}
.

Finally, the action of
G
on
(X,d)
is coarsely proper by
Proposition

7.10
, so any
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subset is coarsely proper, hence any continuous isometric action is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous.
∎

7.3.2
The case of
\Aut(T_{\kappa})

Let
T_{\kappa}
be the simplicial
\kappa
-regular tree with countable valency
\kappa\geq 3
.
Its automorphism group
G=\Aut(T_{\kappa})
endowed with the pointwise convergence on the set of vertices of
T_{\kappa}
is a non-Archimedean Polish group.

For a finite integer
\kappa\geq 3
, the group
\Aut(T_{\kappa})
is locally compact (see for example
[
28
]
) and it is well known that its action on
T_{\kappa}
satisfies
Definition

7.11
.
Hence we will focus on the case where
\kappa=\omega
is the infinite countable degree, even though all our arguments can be adapted for finite cardinals.

Proof of
Corollary

7.17
for
\Aut(T_{\omega})
.

We first check the conditions in
Definition

7.11
.
The fact that the action on
G
on
\partial T_{\omega}
is
3
-transitive follows from a standard back and forth argument.
We will prove that points in
\partial T_{\omega}
have amenable stabilizers in
Proposition

7.23
below.
Let us show now the coarse Cartan decomposition.
First let us note that the stabilizer of a point is coarsely bounded by
[
68
, Example 3.6]
, hence for a segment
[x,x^{\prime}]\subset T_{\omega}
the subset
B(x,x^{\prime})=\{g\in\Aut(T_{\omega})\colon gx\in[x,x^{\prime}]\}
is a finite union of translates of a coarsely bounded subset, hence coarsely bounded.
Now consider a loxodromic
a\in G
with translation length
\ell(a)=l\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Choose a point
x
on its axis
L
and let
B=\{g\in G\colon gx\in[x,ax]\}
.
For
g\in G
, there is
s\in\Stab(x)
such that
s^{-1}gx\in L
, hence
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
a^{m}s^{-1}g(x)\in[x,x^{\prime}]
hence
a^{m}s^{-1}gx\in B
, that is
g\in BAB
.

Finally, the action of
G
on
T_{\omega}
is coarsely proper by
[
68
, Example 3.6]
, in particular
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded subsets coincide with coarsely bounded subsets so every continuous isometric action is
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bornologous.
∎

For
\xi\in\partial T_{\omega}
, its stabilizer
G_{\xi}
is endowed with its Busemann character
\Bus_{\xi}\colon G_{\xi}\to\mathbb{Z}
, whose kernel
G_{\xi}^{0}\subset G_{\xi}
consists of elements which stabilize each horosphere centred at
\xi
.
Note that by regularity, the Busemann character is surjective so we have an extension
G_{\xi}^{0}\to G_{\xi}\to\mathbb{Z}
.

Proposition 7.23

(amenability of
G_{\xi}
)
.

There is an increasing sequence of finite subgroups
G_{\xi}^{0}(n)\subset G_{\xi}^{0}
indexed by
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
g\in G_{\xi}^{0}
and every finite subset
F
of vertices in
T_{\omega}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
h\in G_{\xi}^{0}(n)
such that
g
and
h
coincide on
F
.

Consequently,
G_{\xi}^{0}
is amenable, hence
G_{\xi}
is amenable.

Proof.

Fix a base vertex
w_{0}
and denote
(w_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
its path to
\xi
.
Note that for
g\in G_{\xi}
, there is
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
[w_{0},\xi)\cap[gw_{0},\xi)=[w_{m},\xi)
and if
g\in G_{\xi}^{0}
then
i\geq m\implies g(w_{i})=w_{i}
.
Moreover for every finite subset
F
of vertices, we may consider the subtree
\Conv(w_{m},F)
induced by the vertices in
F
and
w_{m}
.
We will see how to construct a finite order element
h\in\Aut(T_{\omega})
whose action in restriction to
\Conv(w_{m},F)
coincides with that of
f
, and, moreover, how to exhaust all such finite order elements by a sequence of finite groups.

For each vertex
v
of
T_{\omega}
, we denote by
E_{v}
be the set of edges incident to
v
with
e_{v,\xi}\in E_{v}
the unique one on the path from
v
to
\xi
, and choose a bijection (called a colouring)
C_{v}\colon E_{v}\setminus\{e_{v,\xi}\}\to\mathbb{N}
with the condition that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have
C_{w_{i+1}}(w_{i+1}w_{i})=0
, as in the following Figure.
w_{0}
w_{1}
w_{2}
w_{3}
w_{4}
0
0
0
0
0
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
1\;\;\;\;2\,\ldots
\xi

For
g\in G_{\xi}
, its local action at a vertex
v
is the bijection
E_{v}\setminus\{e_{v,\xi}\}\to E_{gv}\setminus\{e_{gv,\xi}\}
encoded as the permutation
\sigma(g,v)\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
defined by
C_{gv}\circ g_{\mid E_{v}\setminus\{e_{v,\xi}\}}\circ C_{v}^{-1}
.
The local actions satisfy the cocycle relation
\sigma(gh,v)=\sigma(g,hv)\circ\sigma(h,v)
.

For a vertex
v
and
k,n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, we define the finite subtree
S_{k,n}(v)\subset T_{\omega}
which is the union of all paths
(v=v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
of length at most
n
with outgoing edges having labels
C_{v_{i}}((v_{i},v_{i+1}))\in\mathbb{N}_{<k}
.
Thus
S_{k,n}(v)
is a
k
-regular rooted tree at
v
of depth
n
.

Now let
G_{k,n}(v)
be the subset of
g\in G_{\xi}
such that for very vertex
u\in S_{k,n}(v)
that is not a leaf the permutation
\sigma(g,u)
fixes pointwise
\mathbb{N}_{\geq k}
, and for every other vertex
u\in T_{\omega}
we have
\sigma(g,u)=\mathbf{1}
.
Thus
G_{k,n}(v)
is the subgroup of
G_{\xi}
preserving
S_{k,n}(v)
and acting rigidly on all edges outside
S_{k,n}(v)
(namely preserving colours of edges not in
E(S_{k,n}(v)
).
In particular an element of
G_{k,n}(v)
is completely determined (from the initial colouring) by its restriction on
S_{k,n}(v)
. Hence the group
G_{k,n}(v)
is isomorphic to the automorphism group of the
k
-regular rooted tree of depth
n
, which is finite.

We note that
S_{n,2n}(w_{n})\subset S_{n+1,2(n+1)}(w_{n+1})
, and we claim that
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}S_{n,2n}(w_{n})=T_{\omega}
.
Indeed, a vertex
v
belongs to
S_{n,2n}(w_{n})
as soon as
n
is larger than
d(v,w_{0})
and all colours of edges on the path from
w_{0}
to
v
are smaller than
n
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, denote
G^{0}_{\xi}(n)=G_{n,2n}(w_{n})
.
Since
S_{n,2n}(w_{n})\subset S_{n+1,2(n+1)}(w_{n+1})
we have
G_{\xi}^{0}(n)\subset G_{\xi}^{0}(n+1)
and we finally show that
\bigcup G_{\xi}^{0}(n)
is dense in
G_{\xi}^{0}
.

Let
g\in G_{\xi}^{0}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
i\geq m\implies g(w_{i})=w_{i}
.
For a finite subset
F
of vertices, there is
n\geq m
large enough such that
F\cup g(F)\subset S_{n,2n}(w_{n})
(it suffices to consider the subtree
\Conv(w_{m},F)
and its edge colours). Since any partial automorphism of
S_{n,2n}(w_{n})
(that is an isomorphism between subtrees containing the root
w_{n}
) can be extended to an automorphism of
S_{n,2n}(w_{n})
, there is an element of
G_{\xi}^{0}(n)
coinciding with
g
on
F
.

This proves that
G_{\xi}^{0}
contains a countable dense union of finite subgroups.
Therefore
G_{\xi}^{0}
is amenable, so its
\mathbb{Z}
-extension
G_{\xi}
is amenable.
∎

7.3.3
The case of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
.

Fix a (commutative) field
\mathbb{K}
with a non-trivial complete non-Archimedean norm
\lvert\cdot\rvert
.
The valuation ring
\mathcal{O}=\{x\in\mathbb{K}\colon\lvert x\rvert\leq 1\}
has maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}=\{x\in\mathbb{K}\colon\lvert x\rvert<1\}
and residue field
\mathbb{k}=\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{m}
.
Note that the residue map
\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{k}
is continuous for the discrete topology on
\mathbb{k}
(which is induced by the trivial norm), hence for any other topology (including those induced by non-trivial norms).

More generally for
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
denote
\mathcal{O}_{r}=\{x\in\mathbb{K}\colon\lvert x\rvert\leq r\}\supset\mathfrak{m}_{r}=\{x\in\mathbb{K}\colon\lvert x\rvert<r\}
.
These are both clopen subsets of
\mathbb{K}
and yield as
r\to 0
a basis of neighbourhoods of
0\in\mathbb{K}
.
For all
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the subsets
\mathfrak{m}_{r}\subset\mathcal{O}_{r}
are fractional ideals of
\mathbb{K}
, and their quotient
\mathcal{O}_{r}/\mathfrak{m}_{r}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{k}
or
0
according to whether
\mathbb{K}
has or has no elements of norm
r
(if
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{r}\setminus\mathfrak{m}_{r}
then multiplication by
\lambda
sends the pair of ideals
\mathcal{O}\supset\mathfrak{m}
to the pair of ideals
\mathcal{O}_{r}\supset\mathfrak{m}_{r}
).

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, the framed
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
\mathbb{K}^{n}
is endowed with the norm extending
\lvert\rvert
on
\mathbb{K}
, defined using its canonical framing on any
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in V
by
\lvert v\rvert=\max\{\lvert v_{i}\rvert\colon 1\leq i\leq n\}
, and yielding an ultrametric on
\mathbb{K}^{n}
.
Hence the
\mathbb{K}
-algebra of matrices
\operatorname{M}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
, is endowed with the
operator norm
defined by

\|g\|:=\sup\left\{\lvert gv\rvert/\lvert v\rvert\colon v\in\mathbb{K}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\right\}

which induces the topology given by uniform convergence for its action on
\mathbb{K}^{n}
.
It can be computed, using the ultrametric inequality for
\lvert\cdot\rvert
as
\|g\|=\max\{\lvert g_{i,j}\rvert\colon 1\leq i,j\leq n\}
, so it also coincides with the canonical norm on the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
\operatorname{M}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
and induces the product topology of its components.

The group of invertibles
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{K})\subset\operatorname{M}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
with the induced topology is a topological group, and its projectivization
\PGL_{n}(\mathbb{K})=\GL_{n}(\mathbb{K})/\mathbb{K}^{\times}\mathbf{1}
is endowed with the quotient topology.
If
\mathbb{K}
is separable then
\mathbb{K}
it is a Polish field, and
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{K}),\PGL_{n}(\mathbb{K})
are Polish groups.

The group
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{K})
acts on its Bruhat-Tits affine building and its essentialization: we only recall the definition of the building as metric space, and of the isometric action of
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{K})
but for more details we refer to
[
69
, §6.2]
and
[
60
]
(or its French original
[
59
]
).

On a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
V
of dimension
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a
norm
is a function
\eta\colon V\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

•

\eta(v)=0\iff v=0
,

•

for all
v\in V
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}
we have
\eta(\lambda v)=\lvert\lambda\rvert\eta(v)

•

for all
u,v\in V
we have
\eta(u+v)\leq\max(\eta(u),\eta(v))

The distance
d_{\infty}
between two norms
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
on
V
is given by

d_{\infty}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\sup\left\{\left|\log\tfrac{\eta_{1}(v)}{\eta_{2}(v)}\right|\colon v\in V\setminus\{0\}\right\}

Given a basis
(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
of
V
, a norm
\eta
on
V
is called
split
when for all
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{K}
,

\textstyle\eta\left(\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\right)=\max\{\lvert a_{i}\rvert\eta(e_{i})\colon 1\leq i\leq n\}.

A norm is
splittable
when there is a basis in which it is split.
The
affine building

\mathcal{I}(\GL(V))
is the set of all splittable norms with the distance
d_{\infty}
. The action of the group
\GL(V)
by precomposition is continuous and isometric.
Its essentialization, denoted
\mathcal{I}^{e}(\GL(V))
, is the topological space of homothety classes of splittable norms, and the distance between two homothety classes is the infinimum of the distances betwen their representatives.
The action of
\GL(V)
on
\mathcal{I}(\GL(V))
by isometries quotients to an action of
\PGL(V)
by isometries on
\mathcal{I}^{e}(\GL(V))
.

Once a determinant has been fixed on
V^{n}
, we may realize
\mathcal{I}^{e}(\GL(V))
in
\mathcal{I}(\GL(V))
as the subspace of splittable norms of volume
1
, hence it is endowed with the induced metric
d_{\infty}
.
The isometric action of
M\in\PGL(V)
on
\eta\in\mathcal{I}^{e}(\GL(V))
is given for any representative
M\in\GL(V)
by
(\eta\circ M)/\sqrt[n]{\lvert\det(M)\rvert}
.

We now focus on the case
n=2
and choose a basis
e=(e_{1},e_{2})
to identify
V=\mathbb{K}^{2}
and
\GL(V)=\GL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
.
We also use the determinant associated to that basis to identify the Bruhat-Tits building
\mathcal{I}^{e}(\GL_{2}(\mathbb{K}))
as above: it is a real tree
[
69
, §6.2.20]
which from now on we denote by
T
.
When the valuation associated to the norm is discrete, the construction of the tree can be obtained by using lattices (see
[
69
, §6.2.23]
), but otherwise the real tree is not the geometric realization of a simplicial tree.

We define the
base point

\eta_{e}\in T
as the homothety class of the standard norm
\eta(a_{1}e_{1}+a_{2}e_{2})=\max\{\lvert a_{1}\rvert,\lvert a_{2}\rvert)
; its stabilizer is
\PGL_{2}(\mathcal{O})
.
The orbit under
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
of
\eta_{e}
consists of all splittable norms whose image is contained in
\lvert\mathbb{K}\rvert\subset\mathbb{R}
.
The points of
T
in this orbit are called
special

[
60
, §3B1]
; their stabilizers are all conjugate to
\PGL_{2}(\mathcal{O})
.

The projective line
\mathbb{K}\mathbf{P}^{1}
identifies naturally with the boundary
\partial T
(see
[
69
, § 6.2.20]
), and the projective line
\mathbb{k}\mathbf{P}^{1}
identifies with the set of connected components of the complement
T\setminus\eta_{e}
(this follows from
[
69
, Theorem 6.2.16.1(b)]
).
In particular very special point
x\in T
is a branch point in the sense that its complement
T\setminus x
has at
\geq 3
components (which are open since
T
is locally connected).

We now show
3
-transitivity of the action on
\partial T
and describe stabilizers of boundary points.

Lemma 7.24

(action on boundary)
.

The action of the topological group
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
by isometries of its real tree
(T,d_{\infty})
satisfies the first two conditions in
Definition

7.11
:

•

the action on
\partial T
is
3
-transitive

•

every
\xi\in\partial T
its stabilizer
G_{\xi}\subset\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is amenable.

Proof.

The boundary
\partial T
identifies naturally with the projective line
\mathbb{K}\mathbf{P}^{1}
, so that the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is
3
-transitive (see
[
69
, § 6.2.20]
).

For a point in
\xi\in\partial T
, its stabilizer
G_{\xi}\subset\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is conjugated to the projectivized group of upper-triangular matrices, which is solvable hence amenable.
∎

In the tree
T
, the set of homothety classes of norms split in the basis
e
forms a subset
T_{A}
which is isometric to a real line (this is an apartment in the language of buildings).
In
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
, the classes of diagonal matrices form a subset
A
uniquely parametrized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
according to
A^{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Such matrix
A^{\lambda}
acts on
T
as a loxodromic element, with translation axis
T_{A}
and translation length
\left|\log\lvert\lambda\rvert\right|
(this computation follows from the definitions of
\|\cdot\|
and
d_{\infty}
, and we refer to
[
60
, §3B2]
for details).

Since the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
on
\partial T
is
3
-transitive, for all distinct
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\partial T
, there exists
g\in\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
sending the geodesic
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
to
T_{A}
, in particular the geodesic
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
contains special points.
In fact,
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
acts transitively on pairs consisting of a special point included in an apartment (this follows from the fact that
\mathbb{K}
is complete, implying that the system of apartments is maximal
[
69
, Remark 6.2.17.3 (c)]
and
[
60
, Proposition 2.18.]
).

We will not need the following
remark

7.25
but it gives more consistency to this whole discussion, in particular the next
Lemma

7.26
.

Remark 7.25

(topologies on
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
)
.

It is explained in
[
69
, §6.2.10.1.(2)]
that the topology induced by the operator norm (called the strong topology in this reference) coincides with the so-called building topology (the coarsest topology for which pointwise stabilizers of bounded subsets are open).

Since
T
has a non-discrete metric topology, one may also consider the topology of pointwise convergence on
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
, that is the coarsest topology for which orbit maps are continuous. By construction of the distance on
T
, the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
endowed with the topology induced by the operator norm is continuous, namely the operator topology is finer than the topology of pointwise convergence.

Let us sketch a proof of the converse.
Consider any point
x\in T
.
First, we claim that, if a sequence of elements of
g_{n}\in\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
converges to the identity for the pointwise convergence topology then for
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough we have
g_{n}x=x
.

First assume that
x\in\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})\cdot e
is a special point (that it is a branch point) and choose three points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
in three distinct components of
T\setminus\{x\}
so that
x
is the only intersection
\cap_{i\neq j}[x_{i},x_{j}]
.
For
n
large enough, the element
g_{n}x_{i}
lies in the same (open) connected component of
T\setminus\{x\}
as
x_{i}
, hence
g_{n}x=x
.

Consequently, for all positive value of the norm
M\in\lvert\mathbb{K}^{\times}\rvert\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
n(M)\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
n>n(M)
then
g_{n}
fixes the base point
\eta\in T
and the three special points obtained by intersecting the sphere of radius
M
around
\eta
with the lines
(\eta,0),(\eta,1),(\eta,\infty)
, hence
g_{n}\equiv\mathbf{1}\bmod{O_{M}}
namely
\|g_{n}-\mathbf{1}\|\leq 1/M
.
This proves that pointwise convergence implies uniform convergence.

We now check that the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
on
T
admits coarse Cartan decompositions.

Lemma 7.26

(coarse Cartan decompositions of rank-
1
)
.

The action of the topological group
G=\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
by isometries of its real tree
(T,d_{\infty})
admits coarse Cartan decompositions of rank-
1
as in
Definition

7.11
:
for every loxodromic
a\in G
generating a subgroup
A
, there is a
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bounded subset
B
such that
G=BAB
.

Proof.

Consider a loxodromic element
a^{\prime}\in\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
, generating a subgroup denoted
A^{\prime}
and with translation axis denoted
T_{A^{\prime}}
.
Choose a point
x\in T_{A^{\prime}}
which is special (in the
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
orbit of the base point).
The set
B=\{g\in\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})\colon d_{\infty}(gx,x)\leq\ell(a^{\prime})\}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bounded by definition, and note that it contains
\Stab(x)
.

Choose
g\in G
. Since
G
acts transitively on pairs consisting of an apartment with a special point in an apartment, there is
k\in\Stab(x)
such that
kg(x)\in T_{A^{\prime}}
.
Hence there is
a^{\prime\prime}\in A^{\prime}
such that
d(a^{\prime\prime}kgx,x)\leq\ell(a^{\prime})
. Thus
a^{\prime\prime}kg
belongs to
B
and
G=BA^{\prime}B
.
∎

At this point, we have verified all conditions in
Definition

7.11
.
We are left to show the assumptions in
Theorem

7.14
.
This will be done
Proposition

7.33
which requires some lemmata.

For
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\mathbb{K}
with
\lambda\neq 0
, denote the following matrices of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
:

D^{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&1/\lambda\end{bmatrix}\quad L^{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
\mu&1\end{bmatrix}\quad R^{\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\nu\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\qquad S=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}

(25)

which satisfy the "generalized braid" identities:

L^{-\lambda}R^{1/\lambda}L^{-\lambda}=R^{1/\lambda}L^{-\lambda}R^{1/\lambda}=-SD^{\lambda}
.

Lemma 7.27

(unipotent factorisation)
.

For all
g\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
there exist
i,j\in\{0,1\}
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{O}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{*}
with
\lvert\lambda\rvert=\|g\|
such that

g=S^{i}\cdot L^{\mu}\cdot D^{\lambda}\cdot R^{\nu}\cdot S^{j}=(S^{i}\cdot L^{\mu}S)\cdot(R^{1/\lambda}L^{-\lambda}R^{1/\lambda})\cdot(R^{\nu}\cdot S^{j}).

(26)

Proof.

Let

g=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
.
After replacing
g
by
S^{-i}gS^{-j}
for some
i,j\in\{0,1\}
we may assume that
\lvert a\rvert=\|g\|
, and Gauss-reduction yields

g=L^{\mu}\operatorname{diag}(a,d^{\prime})R^{\nu}

where
\mu=c/a,\nu=b/a\in\mathcal{O}
and
d^{\prime}=\det(g)/a\in\mathcal{O}
.
Thus if
g\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
then
d^{\prime}=1/a
so
\operatorname{diag}(a,d^{\prime})=D^{\lambda}
with
\lambda=a
hence
g=L^{\mu}D^{\lambda}R^{\nu}
and writing
D^{\lambda}=SR^{1/\lambda}L^{-\lambda}R^{1/\lambda}
yields the result.
∎

The following lemma describes the coarsely bounded subsets of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
in terms of its action on its building
T
, or of the operator norm
\|\cdot\|
.
A subset
B\subset\operatorname{M}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
is called
\|\cdot\|
-bounded
when there is
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that every
g\in B
satisfies
\|g\|\leq R
.

Lemma 7.28

(coarsely bounded subsets of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
)
.

For a subset
B\subset\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
. The following are equivalent:

1.

B
is coarsely bounded

2.

B
is
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bounded

3.

B
is
\|\cdot\|
-bounded.

Proof.

(1
\implies
2)

If
B
is coarsely bounded then by definition it is in particular
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bounded.

(2
\implies
3)

Assume that
B
is
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bounded.
Let
\eta\in\mathcal{T}
be the canonical norm in the basis
e
of
\mathbb{K}^{2}
given by
\eta(\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i})=\max_{i}\lvert a_{i}\rvert
.
There is
M>0
such for all
g\in B
we have
d_{\infty}(g\eta,\eta)=d_{\infty}(\eta,g^{-1}\eta)\leq M
. In particular, for all
j=\{1,2\}
we have
\max_{i}\left|\log\lvert g_{ij}\rvert\right|=\left|\log\left(\frac{\eta(g(e_{j})}{\eta(e_{j})}\right)\right|\leq M
, which implies that
B
is
\|\cdot\|
-bounded.

(3
\implies
1)

Assume that
B
is
\|\cdot\|
-bounded by some real
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
.
Consider the identity neighbourhoods in
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
defined for
\varepsilon>0
by
U_{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon\{g\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})\colon\|g\|<1\}
.
Note that these form a basis of neighbourhoods of the identity as
\varepsilon
decreases to
0
.

Let us first show that for
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, there is a finite
F_{\varepsilon}\subset\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
such that
B\subset(F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon})^{16}
.
Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}
such that
\lvert\lambda\rvert\geq\sqrt{M/\varepsilon}\geq 1
and
F_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}\lambda^{s}&0\\
0&\lambda^{-s}\end{bmatrix}\colon s\in\{-1,0,+1\}\right\}
.
The set
(F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon})^{2}
contains
F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}\supset\{L^{\mu},R^{\nu}\colon\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{M}\}
and contains
U_{\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon}\supset\{S^{i}\colon i\in\mathbb{Z}/4\}
.
The
Equation

26
shows that
B\subset(F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon})^{2\times 8}
as desired.

Let us finally deduce that
B
is coarsely bounded.
Consider an action of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
on a metric space
(X,d)
by continuous isometries.
Fix
x\in X
and consider the open neighbourhood of the identity
U=\{g\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})\colon d(gx,x)<1\}
.
There is
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
such that
U_{\varepsilon}\subset U
, and we may thus find a finite
F_{\varepsilon}\subset\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
as above so that
B\subset(F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon})^{16}
. Let
M=\max\{d(gx,x)\colon g\in F_{\varepsilon}\}
. For all
f\in F_{\varepsilon}
and
u\in U_{\varepsilon}
we have
d(fux,x)\leq d(fux,fx)+d(fx,x)
so for all
g\in F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon}
we have
d(gx,x)\leq 1+M
and
16-1
similar triangle inequalities show that every
g\in(F_{\varepsilon}U_{\varepsilon})^{16}
satisfies
d(gx,x)\leq 16(M+1)
, hence the image of
B
under the orbit map
g\mapsto gx
is bounded in
X
.
∎

Remark 7.29

(locally bounded)
.

The topological groups
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
,
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
and
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
are locally bounded.

Indeed, the subgroup
\SL_{2}(\mathcal{O})
is open and coarsely bounded in both
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
and
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
, so its image in
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is a coarsely bounded neighbourhood of the identity.

Example 7.30

(field Laurent series is not locally coarsely bounded)
.

Consider a field
\mathbb{k}
with a non-trivial norm
\lvert\cdot\rvert^{\prime}
, and denote
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\{c\in\mathbb{k}\colon\lvert c\rvert^{\prime}=1\}
its valuation ring. (Let us recall that a field admits no non-trivial norms if and only if it is an algebraic extension of the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
of characteristic
p\neq 0
, see
[
7
, Chapitre 6]
).

Consider the field
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{k}((x))
of Laurent series over
\mathbb{k}
consisting of formal series
f(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}x^{n}
with only finitely many non-zero coefficients
c_{n}\in\mathbb{k}
of negative index.
It is endowed with the discrete valuation
v(f)=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{Z}\colon c_{n}\neq 0\}
yielding the (non-trivial and complete non-Archimedean) norm
\lvert f\rvert=e^{-v(f)}
.
The valuation ring
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{k}[[x]]
consisting of regular formal power series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}x^{n}
has maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}=x\mathbb{k}[[x]]
consisting of those with
c_{0}=0
, and the residue map
\mathcal{O}{K}\to\mathbb{k}
is given by
f\mapsto f(0)=c_{0}

This residue map extends to an additive group homomorphism
(\mathbb{K},\lvert\cdot\rvert)\to(\mathbb{k},\lvert\cdot\rvert^{\prime})
which is continuous and surjective, thus it has unbounded image (since the norm
\lvert\cdot\rvert^{\prime}
is non-trivial).
More generally, the
n
-th component map
D^{n}\colon\mathbb{k}[[x]]\to\mathbb{k}
defined by
D^{n}\colon\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}x^{n}\mapsto c_{n}
is locally constant hence continuous, and it a (split) group homomorphism which is surjective in restriction to
x^{n}\mathcal{O}
.

Identifying
\mathbb{k}
with the translation subgroup of
\Isom(\mathbb{k})=\mathbb{k}\rtimes\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, this yields for very
n\in\mathbb{N}
a continuous representation of
\mathbb{K}\to\Isom(\mathbb{k})
such that
x^{n}\mathcal{O}{K}
has unbounded image.
Since the sets
x^{n}\mathcal{O}
as
n\in\mathbb{N}
form a basis of neighbourhoods of the origin in
\mathbb{K}
, we deduce that
\mathbb{K}
is not locally coarsely bounded.

One may generalize this idea to show that other valued fields
(\mathbb{K},\lvert\rvert)
are not locally coarsely bounded, but a general characterization seems out of reach in model-theoretic terms
[
38
]
. Moreover, for every field of positive characteristic
\mathbb{k}
(of which many are infinite, such as
\mathbb{F}_{p}(T)
), there is a discretely valued ring of zero characteristic
\mathcal{O}
of which it is the residue field (if the field
\mathbb{k}
is perfect, then
\mathbb{K}
can be constructed from Witt vectors, otherwise a result of Cohen ensures existence of such a field
\mathbb{K}
, se
[
8
, Chapitre 9]

Now, its field of fractions
\mathbb{K}=\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{O})
of characteristic
0
has an additive group that is divisible, so it cannot act isometrically on
\mathbb{k}
which has characteristic
p
(for instance
1/p
would act trivially).

Remark 7.31

(unipotent locally coarsely bounded only inside
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
)
.

The group of unipotent matrices parametrized by
\mu\in\mathbb{K}
according to
R^{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\mu\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
is isomorphic (as topological group) to
(\mathbb{K},+)
.
Thus for many fields (such as those in
example

7.30
), that unipotent is not locally coarsely bounded.
Still we deduce from
Lemma

7.28
, that inside
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
this unipotent subgroup is locally coarsely bounded.

Question 7.32

(
\mathcal{E}_{d}
-bounded implies coarsely bounded)
.

The
Lemma

7.28
shows that for a representation of
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
, does
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bounded imply coarsely bounded.

Does the same hold for representations of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
?

From now on
G
denotes
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
.
An element of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is called loxodromic when its action on
T
is loxodromic.

Proposition 7.33

(bornologous and coarsely proper)
.

Consider a continuous representation
\rho\colon\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
that is non-elementary.

There is a
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-equivariant boundary map
\varphi\colon\partial T\to\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
which is injective.

Moreover, the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bornologous.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in\partial T=\mathbb{K}\mathbf{P}^{1}
and consider its stabilizer
G_{\xi}
in
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
.
We saw in
Lemma

7.24
that
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
acts transitively on
\mathbb{K}\mathbf{P}^{1}
, so the group
G_{\xi}
is conjugate to the standard parabolic subgroup
G_{\infty}
generated by the homotheties
\{A^{\lambda}\colon\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\}
and transvections
\{R^{\mu}\colon\mu\in\mathbb{K}\}
, in particular it is amenable.
Thus by continuity of
\rho
, the subgroup
\rho(G_{\xi})\subset\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is amenable.
Hence (as in the proof of
Lemma

7.15
) it must satisfy (at least) one of the following cases:

1.

\forall\xi\in\partial T
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
fixes a point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
,

2.

\forall\xi\in\partial T
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
stabilizes a geodesic line,

3.

\forall\xi\in\partial T
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
fixes a single point in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}

and we will show (for
\xi=\infty
) that neither of the cases 1 nor 2 can occur, so that only case 3 occurs.

The first case would imply, as in
Lemma

7.15
, that
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
fixes a point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. Observe that if
\rho(G_{\infty})
has either
0
or
\geq 3
fixed points in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, then it must have a fixed point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. Thus it suffices to show that the fixed point set of
\rho(G_{\infty})
is not of cardinal
2
.

Assume by contradiction that
\rho(G_{\infty})
has exactly two fixed points
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and no fixed point in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. In particular all
A^{\lambda}
are elliptic or loxodromic.
Fix
\mu\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
, and consider sequence of
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}
with
\lvert\lambda\rvert\to\infty
.
We have
A^{\lambda^{-1}}R^{\mu}A^{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\mu/\lambda\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\to\mathbf{1}
, hence by continuity of
\rho
we have
\rho(A^{\lambda^{-1}})\rho(R^{\mu})\rho(A^{\lambda})\to\mathbf{1}
.
Since the length function is invariant by conjugacy and continuous, we have
\ell(\rho(R^{\mu}))=0
so
\rho(R^{\mu})
is elliptic or parabolic and this type is independent of
\mu
. Since
\rho(G_{\infty})
has exactly two fixed points in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
, the only possibility is that
R^{\mu}
is elliptic, hence fixes every point on the geodesic
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
. If all
A^{\lambda}
are elliptic then the geodesic
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
is pointwise fixed by
\rho(G_{\infty})
, a contradiction. Hence there is
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
such that
\rho(A^{\lambda})
is loxodromic, hence has
\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}
as pair of fixed points. Since
A^{\lambda}\in G_{0}
as well, the points
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
are also fixed by
\rho(G_{0})
in
\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
. Finally, since
G_{\infty}
acts transitively on
\mathbb{K}\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{\infty\}
, the points
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
are fixed by
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
. This contradicts the non-elementarity of the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.

This proves that for all
\xi\in\partial T
, the group
\rho(G_{\xi})
has no fixed points in
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
and a unique fixed point denoted
\varphi(\xi)\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
.
We have thus defined a boundary function
\varphi\colon\xi\in\partial T\mapsto\varphi(\xi)\in\partial\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
which is
\rho
-equivariant.
Since
G
acts
2
-transitively on
\partial T
, the group
\rho(G)
acts
2
-transitively on the image
\varphi(\partial T)
, so the map
\varphi
must be either injective or constant, but if it were constant then
\rho
would have a global fixed point at infinity contradicting non-elementarity, so
\varphi
is injective.

The subgroup of upper triangular matrices
G_{\infty}
and the subgroup of lower triangular matrices
G_{0}
intersect in the group of diagonal matrices
\{A^{\lambda}\colon\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\}
, thus
\rho(A^{\lambda})
stabilizes the geodesic line
(\varphi(\infty),\varphi(0))
, and it consists of either elliptic or loxodromic elements.
Define the function
\Xi\colon\mathbb{K}^{\times}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\lambda\mapsto s\cdot\ell(\rho(A^{\lambda}))
where the sign
s\in\{+1,-1\}
only needs to be defined when
\rho(A^{\lambda})
is loxodromic, as
s=+1
if
\rho(A^{\lambda})^{+}=\varphi(\infty)
and
s=-1
if
\rho(A^{\lambda})^{+}=\varphi(0)
.
This function
\Xi
is a homomorphism (as the composition of the homomorphisms
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\mapsto A^{\lambda}\in A
and
s\ell\colon\rho(A)\to\mathbb{R}
).
Let us show that its kernel is equal to the subgroup of units
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\subset\mathbb{K}^{\times}
.

Fix
\mu\in\mathbb{K}\setminus\{0\}
and
x\in(\varphi(0),\varphi(\infty))
.
By continuity of
\rho
, for all
\varepsilon>0
, there is
M>0
such that if
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
has
\lvert\lambda\rvert>M
then
\dH(\rho(A^{\lambda^{-1}}R^{\mu}A^{\lambda})x,x)<\varepsilon
.
The isometry
\rho(R^{\mu})
sends the geodesic line
(\varphi(\infty),\varphi(0))
to the geodesic line
(\varphi(\infty),\varphi(\mu))
.
In particular for fixed
\mu\in\mathbb{K}\setminus\{0\}
, the function of
y\in(\varphi(\infty),\varphi(0))
defined by
y\mapsto\dH(\rho(R^{\mu})y,y)
is monotonous, with limits
\dH(\rho(R^{\mu})y,y)\to 0
as
y\to\varphi(\infty)
and
\dH(\rho(R^{\mu})y,y)\to+\infty
when
y\to\varphi(0)
.
\varphi(0)
\varphi(\infty)
\varphi(\mu)
\rho(R^{\mu})\cdot x
x

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Up to inversion we may assume
\lvert\lambda\rvert>1
, hence as
n\in\mathbb{N}
goes to
+\infty
we have
\lvert\mu/\lambda^{n}\rvert
goes to
0
.
We have
\dH(\rho(R^{\mu/\lambda^{-n}})x,x)=\dH(\rho(A^{\lambda^{-n}}R^{\mu}A^{\lambda^{n}})x,x)=\dH(\rho(R^{\mu})\rho(A^{\lambda^{n}})x,\rho(A^{\lambda^{n}})x)
and by continuity of
\rho
the left-hand side tends to
\dH(\mathbf{1}\cdot x,x)=0
.
Thus applying the previous observation to
y_{n}=\rho(A^{\lambda^{n}})x
, we deduce that
\rho(A^{\lambda^{n}})x
tends to
\varphi(\infty)
as
n\in\mathbb{N}
tends to
+\infty
, thus
\rho(A^{\lambda})
is loxodromic with (attractive, repulsive) fixed points
(\varphi(\infty),\varphi(0))
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
.
Suppose by contradiction that
\ell(\rho(A^{\lambda}))>0
, and to inverting
\lambda
we may therefore also assume that as
n\in\mathbb{N}
goes to
+\infty
we have
\rho(A^{\lambda})^{n}x\to\varphi(\infty)
thus by the previous observation we have
\dH(\rho(R^{\mu})\rho(A^{\lambda})^{n}x,\rho(A_{\lambda})^{n}x)\to 0
.
However
\dH(\rho(R^{\mu})\rho(A_{\lambda})^{n}x,\rho(A_{\lambda})^{n}x)=\dH(\rho(R^{\mu/\lambda^{n}})x,x)
and as
\lvert\mu/\lambda^{n}\rvert=\lvert\mu\rvert
, we have that
\{\rho(R^{\lambda^{n}\mu})\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is coarsely bounded by
Lemma

7.28
so
\dH(\rho(R^{\mu/\lambda^{n}})x,x)
is bounded as
n\to\infty
.
This yields a contradiction.

So far we have shown that
\Xi
factors through the quotient
\mathbb{K}^{\times}\bmod{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}\to\mathbb{R}
to a function that is proper in the sense that
\lvert\lambda\rvert\to\infty\implies\lvert\ell(\rho(A^{\lambda}))\rvert\to\infty
.
Moreover we saw that if
\lvert\lambda\rvert>1
then
\rho(A^{\lambda})^{+}=\varphi(\infty)
and if
\lvert\lambda\rvert<1
then
\rho(A^{\lambda})^{+}=\varphi(0)
.
Hence there is a proper increasing function
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\Xi(\rho(A^{\lambda}))=f(\lvert\lambda\rvert)
, so
f\colon(\mathbb{R}_{>0},\times)\to\to(\mathbb{R},+)
which is a group homomorphism.
Such an increasing homomorphism
f
must be continuous, so there is
c>0
such that
f=c\log
.
In particular for all
M>0
the set
A_{M}=\{A^{\lambda}\colon\lambda,\lambda^{-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{M}\}
has bounded images by
\rho
in
\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
.

Finally, the Cartan decomposition for
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})=KAK
with
K=\PSL_{2}(\mathcal{O})
.
Since
K
is coarsely bounded by
lemma

7.28
, we deduce from with previous paragraph that a subset
B\subset PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
is
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bounded (namely any point
x\in T
has bounded displacement under
B
) if and only if there is
M>0
such that
B\subset KA_{M}K
.
This proves that the
\rho
-action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
on
\mathbf{H}^{\kappa}
is
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bornologous.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

7.17
for
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
.

Lemma

7.24
and
Lemma

7.26
show that the action of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})
on
(T,d_{\infty})
satisfies
Definition

7.11
, and
Proposition

7.33
shows that an non-elementary representation
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{K})\to\Isom(\mathbf{H}^{\kappa})
is
\mathcal{E}_{d_{\infty}}
-bornologous, thus
Theorem

7.14
applies.
∎
